#5921. Hakeem Jeffries declares bid for House speaker after GOP's midterm massacre

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries told MS NOW’s "Morning Joe" that Democrats should focus on lowering costs after their midterm victories, saying Americans want results rather than retribution.

Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., is officially running to be Speaker of the House in the next Congress.

It comes after Democrats made historic gains in Tuesday night's midterm elections, taking back both the House and Senate for the remainder of President Donald Trump's term.

Jeffries sent a letter to fellow House Democrats on Wednesday afternoon congratulating them on their victories and adding, "I write to humbly request your support in connection with my candidacy to serve as the next Speaker of the House of Representatives."

Jeffries told reporters that he intended to run for the top job in the House during a press conference with his top deputies on Wednesday, House Minority Whip Katherine Clark, D-Mass., and House Democratic Caucus Chairman Pete Aguilar, D-Calif.

DEMOCRAT SEIZE HOUSE CONTROL IN STUNNING BLUE TSUNAMI, VOW RETRIBUTION AGAINST TRUMP ADMIN

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries speaks during a press conference on Capitol Hill on Nov. 3, 2025, in Washington, D.C.  (Roberto Schmidt/Getty Images)

Clark and Aguilar also signaled they would run for the No. 2 and No. 3 positions in the House.

And despite a small group of far-left socialists elected to Congress who are expected to pose a headache for Jeffries and other establishment Democrats, he will likely have little trouble winning the speaker's gavel given the wide majority his party won on Tuesday.

At the time of writing this story, Democrats were projected to win 237 seats compared to 139 for the GOP.

But 59 races are still too close to call, and Jeffries told reporters earlier that he anticipated a total of 255 or more Democrats in the House next year.

In his lengthy letter to colleagues on Wednesday, Jeffries touted past victories and outlined his vision for the next two years.

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries speaks alongside Rep. Pete Aguilar and Rep. Katherine Clark during a news conference in Washington, D.C., on Jan. 8, 2026. (Anna Moneymaker/Getty Images)

He pledged to "try to find bipartisan common ground whenever and wherever possible" on the issue of affordability while also criticizing the right's handling of high cost-of-living.

"Donald Trump and House Republicans promised to lower costs on Day One. They failed. Instead, toxic GOP policies like the Trump tariffs and war in Iran have done the exact opposite," he wrote.

AMERICA SAW THE BLUE WAVE COMING — BUT DRASTICALLY UNDERESTIMATED THE EXTENT OF THE DEM TSUNAMI

Jeffries also vowed "accountability" for the Trump administration, writing the House "will assume its place as a check and balance on an out-of-control executive branch."

He suggested Democrats would seek to undo Republicans' Medicaid cuts, extend enhanced Obamacare tax credits that expired at the beginning of this year, and end Trump's war in Iran.

President Donald Trump congratulated Democrats on Tuesday following a disastrous Election Day for Republicans. (Jacquelyn Martin/AP)

"Finally, we must restore classic American values like liberty and justice for all, equal protection under the law, the unfettered right to vote in a representative democracy and our heritage as a nation of immigrants. To that end, I look forward to drawing upon the insights and perspectives of every single part of the Caucus in the days and weeks ahead. House Democrats must deliver dramatic change," Jeffries wrote.

House Democrats are slated to hold their leadership elections in mid-December.

Meanwhile on the right, House Judiciary Chairman Jim Jordan, R-Ohio, and House Majority Leader Steve Scalise, R-La., will be vying for the role of minority leader in the next Congress.

Elizabeth Elkind is a politics reporter for Fox News Digital leading coverage of the House of Representatives. Previous digital bylines seen at Daily Mail and CBS News.

#5918. The Missing Critical Minerals Investment

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

Tom Moerenhout is a Professor at Columbia University’s School of Public and International Affairs, Director of Critical Materials at Columbia University’s Center on Global Energy Policy, and Executive Director at the Geneva Platform for Resilient Value Chains.

Kevin Brunelli is a Nonresident Fellow at Columbia University’s Center on Global Energy Policy and previously served in the Office of Critical Minerals and Energy Supply Chains at the U.S. Department of State.

To Catch Up to China, America and Its Allies Must Dig

The United States and its allies are finally taking supply chains for critical minerals seriously. China has spent the last three decades building a dominant share of global mineral production, and the export controls Beijing has introduced periodically since 2023 have made clear just how dangerous it is for other countries to rely on China for access to materials that are essential components of semiconductors, defense technologies, and automobiles.

Washington and European capitals are now trying to minimize their exposure. Alongside private firms, these governments have begun investing in both upstream industries including mines, refineries, and recycling infrastructure and downstream industries such as magnets and batteries. The bulk of their efforts so far have focused on mines already in operation or close to it and on refining. It is true that the United States and Europe do not have sufficient facilities to process raw ore into specialized chemicals or refined materials and that building more refining facilities will help reduce their reliance on China and alleviate immediate supply chain concerns.

But this is a temporary fix. Refining facilities are valuable only if they have a steady, long-term supply of raw materials. That, in turn, requires new mines, especially as advanced industries demand ever more minerals. Because building a mine takes roughly 20 years, ensuring a long-term supply of minerals requires investing in new mines now. China, which is committed to maintaining its supply chain dominance, has been willing to allocate substantial capital to such ventures. Japan has, too, ever since China blocked exports of critical minerals to the country amid a dispute over the Senkaku Islands (known in China as the Diaoyu Islands) in 2010, which pushed Tokyo to start the long process of disentangling its own supply chains. But Japan still has a long way to go; after 15 years of investing, the country has reduced its dependence on China for rare earths from about 90 percent to between 65 and 70 percent—not nearly enough to supply its own needs, much less those of its allies.

In short, investing in refining is necessary but insufficient. To avoid future supply chain vulnerabilities, the United States and its allies, especially those in the G-7, must also start building a pipeline of new mines. These investments are long bets, and they are risky. But when successful, they reap high rewards.

The United States and its allies significantly outperform China in mineral exploration. Australian and Canadian firms alone account for roughly one-third of global exploration spending. But the vast majority of geological prospects never become operating mines. This is not for lack of financing in prospecting but because of the many financial disincentives and risks that appear once a mining project moves forward.

The initial stages of mining development rely chiefly on a unique ecosystem of mining companies called “juniors,” so called because they have a small market cap, focus exclusively on one or a few projects, and are nowhere large enough to list on major stock exchanges. Most of them list in Australia or Canada and concentrate on the initial high-risk exploration stages. Geological uncertainty makes betting on junior ventures extraordinarily risky, but if a mineral deposit proves to have significant reserves, the junior can sell the project or bring in a new partner, and that payout is large.

Yet in the next stages, investors tend to lose patience or run out of capital. Although initial prospecting is relatively cheap, subsequent feasibility studies that estimate a deposit’s size, engineering needs, costs, and expected returns can easily run into the tens of millions of dollars, in addition to the costs of drilling, engineering, permitting, and development planning. Commercial lenders stay away because these projects have no immediate revenue, no assets to offer as collateral, and no guarantees they will even be viable. The risk, in short, is too high to take for uncertain returns. Large mining companies also avoid these projects because they face pressure from shareholders to provide maximum returns. Even as demand for minerals has risen, the share of revenue that major mining companies have reinvested in new development has stayed flat.

Without outside financing, juniors cannot complete the studies needed to attract additional financing for construction, even if they have found a promising deposit. As single-asset companies, they have no other cash flow or assets to offer as collateral for additional loans. This moment is often referred to as the “valley of death.” Many companies looking for a way out of this predicament have turned to one reliable source of investment: China. Chinese companies are much more willing to take on this type of risk and provide the necessary capital to advance mining projects, and they are often the only viable option for juniors. Thus, China’s dominance in global supply chains relies on Western companies’ exploration. It endures not because China offers more competitive terms but because at a key financing stage, it is the only game in town.

China has been expanding its supply pipeline for critical minerals for decades. In 2025, it spent a record $32 billion on metals and mining projects through its Belt and Road Initiative, breaking the previous year’s record of $21 billion. Chinese firms are also willing to invest in equity, buying ownership stakes in companies that come with no guaranteed returns. Additional spending takes the form of resources-for-infrastructure deals, by which China funds infrastructure such as ports, energy facilities, and railways in mineral-rich countries in exchange for a share in the profits of mining projects—which Chinese companies and state banks may also help finance. By investing in a broad portfolio of mineral projects across several countries, Chinese firms can let the profits of those that succeed cover the losses of those that fail.

Apart from capital, the most important part of China’s mining strategy is know-how. Because failure is so common, the ability to identify deposits with the highest potential to deliver is crucial. Beijing relies on specialized state-owned enterprises, geological firms, private companies, and government officials to vet projects and work with host governments to secure permits, infrastructure, and delivery. The only G-7 country that maintains a comparable on-the-ground presence for mining projects is Japan, in countries such as Australia, Canada, Peru, and Uzbekistan. These projects are intended to secure critical minerals that can support Japan’s industries, but they are currently insufficient to meet all of Japan’s production needs, let alone broader allied needs.

The disparity between China’s mining investments and those of the United States and its allies is most evident in Africa, home to some of the largest untapped mineral resources. Whereas the United States invested about $300 million in critical mineral projects on the continent in 2023, China invested about $8 billion to $10 billion. Washington has recognized the need to close this gap, but it still has a long way to go.

In the Democratic Republic of the Congo, a particular focus of the Trump administration, Chinese companies control about 70 percent of cobalt and copper mines. Between 2008 and 2020, Chinese banks and companies extended over $9 billion in credit for mine development and operations in Congo, in addition to $3 billion in infrastructure loans from the Export-Import Bank of China. The United States and other G-7 countries had the opportunity to take part in these investments but judged the projects to be too risky. In 2015, for example, the Canadian-listed mining company Ivanhoe Mines sought funding for its Kamoa-Kakula copper project in Congo. With no Western investors willing to take on the risk, the Chinese firm Zijin Mining acquired an ownership stake. Today, Kamoa-Kakula is on track to become one of the largest copper mines in the world. Meanwhile, in 2016 and 2020, the only major American mining company active in Congo sold its stakes in two substantial copper-cobalt deposits, its only remaining stakes in the country, to a Chinese mining company.

The owners of a mine control where its minerals eventually go. When Chinese firms own a mine, the output flows either to their refineries or downstream manufacturers in China. The Chinese battery giant CATL, for example, has an ownership stake in the Kisanfu mine in Congo, where it secures a steady supply of copper and cobalt for its batteries. U.S. automakers and allied battery producers have also invested in some projects, but none to the same degree—even still, much of that material flows through China. The concentration of this refined material in China gives Beijing ever-greater market power and the ability to weaponize it.

In theory, the United States and its allies have the means of financing their own pipeline of future mining projects. In 2024, two Toronto-based stock exchanges hosted nearly 1,100 publicly listed mining companies, 40 percent of the global total. These companies account for roughly one-third of the world’s total mining equity capital raised over the last five years. About half of the listed companies’ projects were based in Canada. The Australian Stock Exchange is another key exchange, with about 850 companies listed in 2024 for mining development.

But these companies are facing pressures that discourage investment in promising projects. The world’s most accessible mineral deposits have already been tapped, so potential new deposits are often located in more complicated geological environments, making the early stages of exploration more difficult and costly. Moving from exploration to production is also taking more time, as increased social and environmental requirements draw out permitting processes. This lengthening timeline lowers private investors’ appetite for risk because they have to wait longer for their investments to start generating returns. At the same time, Chinese overproduction of critical minerals has depressed prices for key commodities. Despite strong signals that demand for the minerals will continue to increase, these low prices discourage new players from entering the game and weaken investors’ incentives to help those already in it.

U.S. and allied governments therefore need to draw in substantial new investment. The United States has made a start by taking equity stakes in domestic and international mining companies and expanding loans to them. Official announcements claim that these investments total around $50 billion, although the value of the deals that have been signed falls closer to $9 billion, and the money actually moving adds up to around $4 billion. Most of this money does not go to the projects languishing in the valley of death. Instead, the projects getting funded are those that are closer to production, because they can help tackle immediate supply risks.

This is a good start, but ventures that remain at early stages still need funding, and private investors are still wary of mining projects. Since 2025, the few institutional investors that have gotten involved tended to do so after the U.S. government announced an equity stake or loan to back a project. Although such caution is not new, relying on government signals and public investment sets up a perverse incentive for private companies not to invest in the absence of public support, which is exactly what governments want to avoid.

To really pull in private capital for exploration and mine development, the United States and the European Union should take a page from Canada’s book. The Canadian government offers tax credits to companies engaged in mineral exploration, and its “flow-through shares” system helps mining companies finance exploration and development by offering tax incentives to investors. If the rest of the G-7 can establish this kind of financial architecture, far more exploration capital would flow to critical mineral projects. And if member countries link their systems by letting investors claim tax benefits for exploration anywhere in the G-7, they could build the large, shared pool of exploration and development capital needed to support projects through to construction. In addition, G-7 countries must remove barriers for large institutional investors to enter the mining sector—primarily by reforming their tax codes, which currently disincentivize tax-exempt investors, such as pension funds, endowments, and foundations, from investing in mining projects because returns on such investments can be taxed as income.

Finally, when the incentives for private investment still do not line up for certain projects, G-7 countries should use public capital to directly help mining companies escape the valley of death. To do this, the United States, Japan, and European countries should set up a joint facility to pool financing for feasibility studies, which are particularly difficult to raise private funding for, establishing a broad portfolio so that the losses from any failures are covered by the winners. This pooled fund would allocate grants to promising projects in exchange for equity in the project or a royalty once the mine goes into production. Many projects would still fail, but with a dedicated, well-staffed facility, the quality of investments would likely improve—as they have for China, which takes a similar portfolio approach to early-stage mining. And the benefits to both investors and national interests would far outweigh the lost capital.

Today’s chokepoints for critical minerals sit mostly at the processing or refining stage, but future threats to the minerals needed for manufacturing, energy, artificial intelligence, and defense technology will be at the mines. G-7 countries have the markets and the policy tools to build a pipeline of secure mining projects and prevent the next economic emergency. Investment in refining facilities means little if the United States and its allies have no minerals to feed them a decade from now. The only choice, then, is to commit to financing new mining projects now.

How to Outcompete Chinese State Capitalism

Surviving the New Age of Economic Coercion

Washington Cannot Achieve Tech Dominance on Its Own

How Long Will Elites Stay Loyal to Kim Jong Un?

Why the Country’s Food Insecurity Could Prevent a War Over Taiwan

The Case for an Economic Security Alliance

Theodore Bunzel, Edward Fishman, and Addar Levi

#5917. Newsom breaks with socialists over billionaire tax, and California voters appear poised to reject it

An Economist warned California's wealth tax could cause damage, questions its national appeal and highlights key election wins despite broader setbacks.

In the lead-up to the November midterms, the Democratic Socialists of America (DSA) put its support behind California’s Proposition 40, a measure that would impose a one-time 5% tax on billionaires in the state.

Although the secretary of state's election results show it on the path to defeat as of Wednesday afternoon, the DSA’s support for the policy has created a flashpoint for larger discussions about socialism's redistributive aims and the capital flight critics say could follow.

"In their mind, every tax they do is some attempt to get money from people who create wealth and move it to the state," Richard Stern, an economics scholar with Advancing American Freedom (AAF), said in an interview with Fox News Digital.

The DSA's stance on Proposition 40 has also brought it into conflict with other Democratic figures, including Gov. Gavin Newsom, who opposed it.

SHOCKING PERCENTAGE OF DEMOCRATS IDENTIFY AS 'DEMOCRATIC SOCIALISTS' ACCORDING TO NEW POLL: 'TOXIC POLICIES'

California Gov. Gavin Newsom, left, and a group of DSA supporters holding a banner. (Charly Triballeau/AFP via Getty Images; Spencer Platt/Getty Images)

The proposition would have applied a 5% tax on assets like businesses, securities, art, collectibles and intellectual property while excluding select pensions, real estate and retirement accounts.

To prevent billionaires from simply leaving the state, Proposition 40 was designed to retroactively apply to billionaires who were residents of California on Jan. 1, 2026.

On the website for its California chapter, the group offered a PowerPoint slideshow supporters could use to argue its merits.

"We have the money in California to pay for the quality schools and healthcare that we need. It just needs to be transferred from the wrong pockets to the right ones. We can do that by taxing the rich," the slideshow reads.

The DSA argued the policy would help cover up to a projected $100 billion shortfall in federal funding for the state’s Medi-Cal program that provides health insurance for low-income individuals, seniors and people with disabilities. The proposition also outlined that 90% of the generated revenue would go to healthcare, while the remaining 10% would be set aside for education, food assistance and administration of the tax.

As part of its argument in favor of the policy, the DSA framed it as a scaled-up version of something that already exists: a progressive tax, a kind of tax that scales relative to income.

"It calls for a temporary 5% wealth tax on just 250 Californians — the very richest people in the state — who together own $2 trillion in wealth," its materials explain.

"Let’s be clear: if the Billionaire Tax passes, none of the billionaires will actually lose money. They have been averaging a growth rate of 7 or 8 percent per year in their wealth. Thus, a 5% hit doesn’t even cut into what they have; it just slightly slows down the growth of that wealth."

But to experts like Stern, the AAF economist, the policy reflects a fundamental misunderstanding of wealth.

"The left looks at a billionaire, and they assume that they’re sitting on $10 billion, $100 billion in cash. I hate to break it to them: There’s no cash," Stern said.

"They have no idea that we’re talking about a trillion-dollar company, we’re talking about a trillion dollars’ worth of factories and warehouses and delivery trucks and machinery."

LET SOCIALISTS ROB FROM THE RICH 100%, AND YOU STILL WON’T BE ABLE TO PAY THE BILLS

California Democratic Gov. Gavin Newsom has come out strongly against a proposed state billionaire tax while pushing a similar national tax. (Carlin Stiehl/Los Angeles Times via Getty Images)

Notably, Newsom has said he is opposed to the measure, arguing it would put California at a competitive disadvantage.

In an interview with Fox News Digital, Newsom cited opposition from a number of different groups.

"The firefighters opposed it. The teachers opposed it. Planned Parenthood opposed it. The vast majority of labor opposes it. I think the majority of Californians oppose it. So, we all have our reasons why," Newsom said ahead of the election.

"I don’t think it’s a good idea to do [it] at a state level. It’s a badly written initiative. It focuses just on one part of the state’s budget. It’s one-time money and the impact is already being felt. People have left the state."

Instead, Newsom called on voters to adopt a poison pill countermeasure. Proposition 42 was designed to prohibit new state taxes on personal property and restrict certain retroactive taxes. That measure looks to be on track to succeed.

Even so, Newsom noted that his views on a 5% tax might change if implemented at the federal level.

"Looking at that from a national prism, very different conversation than a state-by-state prism. That’s been the debate in California. I think the majority of Californians see that distinction even though they absolutely believe that we need a new tax bracket in this country," Newsom said.

Stern noted that Newsom’s openness to considering such a policy nationally comes with many of the same difficulties, but on an international scale.

'CASTRO'S PLAYBOOK': EX-AG WARNS DOZENS OF CONGRESSIONAL BILLS ARE PUSHING SOCIALIST IDEAS

Berkeley with San Francisco in the background (Neal Wong/Getty Images)

"You can more easily leave California, but you could give up American citizenship and move abroad," Stern said.

"[It] gets back to the old half-joke about communism and the wall in Berlin, which is, as Reagan said, they build walls that keep people in where normally we build walls to keep people out."

When asked what distinguishes Proposition 40 from a simple tax increase, Stern pointed back to the policy’s implications for businesses, in his view, the main source of most billionaires’ wealth.

"We're taking the means of production out of the hands of people that built those businesses, that manage those businesses, and giving them to Bernie Sanders and AOC and politicians to run them," Stern said, referring to two of the country’s most well-known congressional figures who have received backing from the DSA in the past.

Leo Briceno is a politics reporter for the congressional team at Fox News Digital. He was previously a reporter with World Magazine.

#5915. Hegseth's future draws ominous predictions from insiders after GOP losses, but Trump isn't budging yet

Democratic congressional candidate Cait Conley told CNN she would support impeaching War Secretary Pete Hegseth.

President Donald Trump stood by War Secretary Pete Hegseth Wednesday, saying he was doing "a very good job" even as current and former administration figures continued to question how long the Pentagon chief could remain in his post after Republicans’ bruising midterm losses.

"I think he’s doing a very good job," Trump said Wednesday when asked by the media whether he was considering replacing Hegseth.

The public support came a day after Democrats secured majorities in both chambers of Congress in Tuesday's midterm elections, setting the stage for a shift in power on Capitol Hill in January.

The Iran war and its economic fallout loomed large in the election, with Hegseth among the administration's most vocal advocates for the campaign. His future remains the subject of speculation among current and former administration figures despite Trump's public backing.

TRUMP PLEDGES TO VETO 'INSANE' DEM BILLS, PINPOINTS ISSUE PARTIALLY TO BLAME FOR REPUBLICAN LOSSES

Pete Hegseth, U.S. President-elect Donald Trump's nominee to be secretary of defense, testifies before a Senate Committee on Armed Services confirmation hearing on Capitol Hill in Washington Jan. 14, 2025. (Reuters/Elizabeth Frantz)

A White House official told Fox News Digital Tuesday that the election results "would have looked very different without the Iran War." The official predicted Trump would ultimately hold Hegseth, a former Fox News host, accountable, saying, "That will play out in the lame duck."

"Hegseth is on the chopping block," a former White House staffer told Fox News Digital later Tuesday of Hegseth's future.

An administration official said there were "signs" Hegseth could be ousted but added that "Trump can turn on a dime." The official said the possibility had been discussed for some time, though "only Trump knows what he’s gonna do."

"It was clear Bessent became the go-to after Pete couldn't deliver an Iran victory," the source said, referring to Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, who led the sanctions campaign against Iran known as Operation Economic Outcast.

Beyond the Iran war, Hegseth also championed a plan to livestream the firing-squad execution of Nidal Hasan, the former Army major who killed 13 people and wounded 32 others at Fort Hood in 2009. The proposal drew bipartisan backlash before Trump ultimately scrapped the livestream, while leaving Hasan's execution in place.

A foreign policy source with ties to Hegseth expressed doubt to Fox News Digital that the secretary will remain in the job through the end of the year. The source said Republican Kentucky Rep. Thomas Massie’s impeachment push could force Republicans eyeing 2028 re-election bids to "saddle themselves with a pro-Hegseth vote."

GOP SEN THOM TILLIS URGES TRUMP TO REPLACE PETE HEGSETH, BLASTS PENTAGON LEADERSHIP

Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth steps away from the podium with Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff Gen. Dan Caine, right, and Adm. Brad Cooper, left, after a press briefing at the Pentagon April 16, 2026, in Washington. (Kevin Wolf/AP Photo)

Hegseth has faced criticism over his prominent role in the Iran war, which has continued to generate political and economic fallout long after the White House's initial three- to four-week timeline. He was among the administration's most vocal defenders of the campaign, declaring in March that the U.S. was carrying out "our retribution" against Iran.

Although sustained U.S. strikes have ended, tensions with Tehran remain unresolved with lingering concerns about Iran's nuclear and missile capabilities and the possibility of renewed military action.

Renewed U.S. military strikes against Iran remain under serious consideration by the White House, sources told Fox News Digital.

That possibility could weigh against an immediate change at the Pentagon. Installing new leadership as the administration considers renewed military operations would create uncertainty at a critical moment, while Hegseth has already demonstrated a willingness to move quickly in carrying out Trump's military directives.

Chief Pentagon spokesperson Sean Parnell told Fox News Digital Hegseth remains "100% committed to leading the Department until the end of President Trump’s term in office."

"Secretary Hegseth will not back down or be deterred by politicians seeking to score partisan points or launch phony investigations where no wrongdoing exists," Parnell said.

"The Department of War has followed the law, and our secretary will continue to stand and fight every single day to support and defend the Constitution, lead our troops and protect America. We will continue to drive President Trump’s agenda at the Department of War despite threats of sham witch hunts from Democrats."

Democrats have signaled that Hegseth will be a focus of the incoming Congress. Before the election, House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries said Democrats would "run Pete Hegseth out of town" if they won the House. Democrats have also discussed investigations into the Iran war and Pentagon leadership, with impeachment among the options.

Scrutiny from Democrats could keep Hegseth and the war he has championed at the center of the administration’s political troubles.

Massie, R-Ky., has separately vowed to force a vote on his impeachment resolution during the lame-duck session if Hegseth remains in office.

DEM FLIPS KEY SENATE SEAT IN BATTLEGROUND NORTH CAROLINA AS COOPER DEFEATS TRUMP ALLY

President Donald Trump and Secretary of War Pete Hegseth listen to questions from the media during a Cabinet meeting at Camp David in Maryland July 31, 2026. (Aaron Schwartz/AFP via Getty Images)

As war secretary, Hegseth pushed sweeping changes to military culture, including banning transgender service members; eliminating diversity, equity and inclusion programs; and imposing uniform combat fitness standards for men and women. He also sought to overhaul the Pentagon bureaucracy by consolidating Army commands, cutting senior military leadership and expanding the use of artificial intelligence and advanced weapons technology.

His tenure has also been marked by controversy, including the sharing of sensitive details about planned strikes against Houthi militants in Signal chats that inadvertently included a journalist. Internal infighting, leak investigations and the dismissal of several top aides have fueled scrutiny, though Trump has repeatedly defended his leadership.

The political pressure on Hegseth echoes a past moment when midterm losses and an unpopular war put a defense secretary’s future in question.

On Nov. 8, 2006, President George W. Bush announced Defense Secretary Donald Rumsfeld’s resignation, one day after Republicans suffered major midterm election losses amid growing criticism of the Iraq War.

Ashley J. DiMella reports on politics for Fox News Digital.

#5913. Fox News/NORC Study: Voters look to 2028 focused on the economy

President Donald Trump weighs in on Republicans’ midterm losses, the war with Iran and working with a Democratic-controlled Congress.

After the midterm elections, attention will turn to the 2028 race for the White House. What is on voters’ minds as they look ahead to the next presidential election?

Most Republicans want the next president to follow President Donald Trump’s policies, if not his style. Nearly 4 in 10 MAGA Republicans want Trump’s successor to emulate both, and about half want the next president to follow his policies alone. Non-MAGA Republicans are less enthusiastic: 45% want his policies to continue, but only about 15% of non-MAGA Republicans want a president with the same style.

Independents prefer something entirely different: 6 in 10 want the next president to break with both Trump’s policies and his style. Likewise, few Democrats want to see either his style or his policies repeated.

Even as the Democrats have won control of the Senate and the House of Representatives, and Trump finds his approval rating at 39%, a bloc of voters remains loyal to the president and is likely to influence the 2028 campaign. While half of voters want a successor who does not emulate his style or follow his policies, 26% want a president who will carry on his policies, and another 12% want the next president to copy his policies and his style.

FOX NEWS VOTER POLL: 2026 MIDTERM ELECTIONS USHER IN A BLUE WAVE AS DEMOCRATS WIN CONTROL OF CONGRESS

Fox News/NORC study results show the views of registered voters nationwide on political, economic and policy issues. (Fox News/NORC)

Looking ahead, voters across party lines say improving economic conditions is the most important issue for the country to address over the next five years. Six in 10 voters feel they are holding steady financially while about 3 in 10 feel they are falling behind. Few, about 1 in 10 voters, feel they are getting ahead financially. Voters who describe their own financial situation as less than satisfactory, or say they are falling behind, are more likely to name the economy as the top issue.

What should Tuesday's winners prioritize once sworn into office? When asked what is most important for newly elected officials to work on first in 2027, 3 in 10 voters want elected officials to address grocery costs. About 2 in 10 want action on gas prices, and a similar share say they should work on healthcare and prescription drug costs.

Slightly fewer are concerned about housing costs, while about 1 in 10 want officials to address electricity and other utility costs.

Voters are closely divided on whether the country can look forward to better days or if the good days are behind us. And it’s linked to how they’re feeling financially. About two-thirds of those who say they are getting ahead financially are optimistic about the future. However, about two-thirds of voters who are falling behind financially think the country’s best days are in the past.

Beyond the economy, priorities diverge. About 1 in 5 Democrats point to access to affordable healthcare, while about a similar share of Republicans say protecting individuals from government overreach is the top issue. About 1 in 10 independents say the country should address the affordability of healthcare, and about the same share say government overreach.

Voters are divided over AI’s effect on the U.S. economy during the next 10 years. About 4 in 10 say it will be harmful, while 3 in 10 expect benefits. About 1 in 10 expect AI to make no difference, while slightly more, about 2 in 10, are unsure. About 4 in 10 expect AI to negatively affect the environment and the arts and entertainment industry.

About half of voters want substantial changes in how the country is run. Party identification shapes how much change voters want and which branches of government they think most need reform. Democrats are about three times as likely as Republicans to want complete upheaval of how the country is run. Half of democratic socialists favor complete and total upheaval, compared with about 1 in 10 MAGA Republicans.

Fox News/NORC study results show the views of registered voters nationwide on political, economic and policy issues. (Fox News/NORC)

In the 2024 Fox News Voter Analysis conducted for the presidential election, most Republican voters said they wanted substantial change or total upheaval. Now, with a Republican in the White House and their party controlling both houses of Congress, they are less eager for major change.

FOX NEWS VOTER POLL: HOW DEMOCRATS TOOK CONTROL OF THE SENATE

Fox News/NORC study results show the views of registered voters nationwide on political, economic and policy issues. (Fox News/NORC)

Overall, 4 in 10 voters think Congress should be substantially reformed, including three-quarters of MAGA Republicans and half of non-MAGA Republicans. Democrats are less likely to say Congress needs reform, but half say the executive branch should be improved. Few voters in either party want changes to the Constitution. Democrats are more likely than Republicans or independents to say the Supreme Court needs reform.

Fox News/NORC study results show the views of registered voters nationwide on political, economic and policy issues. (Fox News/NORC)

Voters across party lines support term limits for Congress and Supreme Court justices. Democrats favor abolishing the Electoral College, but Republicans and independents are less enthusiastic. Most Republicans support limiting mail voting to people physically unable to vote in person. MAGA Republicans are more supportive of limiting mail voting than non-MAGA Republicans (63% vs 40%). Democrats oppose the idea, regardless of whether they support the Democratic Socialists of America.

AMERICANS CLASH OVER SUPREME COURT EXPANSION AS FIGHT OVER 9 JUSTICES HEATS UP: ‘LET’S PACK IT NOW’

Fox News/NORC study results show the views of registered voters nationwide on political, economic and policy issues. (Fox News/NORC)

The expansion of artificial intelligence has triggered some public backlash against AI data centers. Voters are divided on whether AI will help or hurt during the next 10 years. More Democrats say the technology will be harmful while more Republicans expect benefits.

Fox News/NORC study results show the views of registered voters nationwide on political, economic and policy issues. (Fox News/NORC)

Among Republican voters, MAGA Republicans are more enthusiastic about AI than non-MAGA Republicans. Forty-five percent of MAGA Republicans believe AI will help the U.S. to compete with other countries, and another 4 in 10 feel AI will do more to help the economy.

Among Democrat voters, supporters of democratic socialism and those who do not support the movement are similarly likely to feel AI will help aspects of American society. However, supporters of democratic socialism are more likely than other Democrats to feel AI will do more to hurt than help.

Overall, 6 in 10 voters are extremely or very concerned about the effect of data centers on the price of utilities in the communities where they are being built. About half are concerned about the effect of data centers on pollution of natural resources, land development or the health of the local population, regardless of whether they live near a data center. Democrats are more concerned about the effects of data centers than Republican voters, again, regardless of their proximity to the facilities.

Voters across party lines support requiring companies to pay for electric grid upgrades needed for their data centers, increasing government regulation of AI, and limiting AI in schools. Most Democrats and independents oppose building a data center in their community. Most voters also oppose local construction, whether their county already has a data center.

AMERICA’S MULTI-BILLION TECH BOOM COMES INTO FOCUS AS 7 KEY STATES PUT IT TO A MIDTERM TEST

Fox News/NORC study results show the views of registered voters nationwide on political, economic and policy issues. (Fox News/NORC)

The economy is a top issue for most voters, yet 4 in 10 say they could support a candidate whose economic views differ from their own.

After the economy and jobs, nearly 6 in 10 voters see healthcare as an important issue, and about 6 in 10 would not support a candidate who disagrees with them on healthcare policy. About half could not back a candidate whose views on Israel and the Palestinians differ from theirs. Overall, about 6 in 10 say they could never support a candidate they disagree with on abortion, guns, or immigration.

Explore the results here: https://civichealth.norc.org/crosstabs.html

The NORC Civic Health & Elections Project (CH&EP) is a national survey of registered voters conducted Oct. 26 through Nov. 3, 2026, with 113,020 interviews. Interviews were conducted in English and Spanish. The margin of sampling error for the national sample, including the design effect, was ±0.5 percentage points for voters and ±1.2 percentage points for nonvoters. Margins of sampling error for individual states and subgroups vary based on sample size.

#5910. Vance courting disgruntled Trump voters to form ‘new right’ alliance after Republican bloodbath, sources say

Midterm election developments unfold as Donald Trump addresses reporters at the White House following key congressional races. Plus, former RNC chairwoman Ronna McDaniel discusses the GOP's performance.

EXCLUSIVE: Vice President JD Vance is seeking to forge a new alliance with disaffected Trump voters ahead of the 2028 election cycle, Fox News Digital has learned.

Vance took control of his own messaging Wednesday morning in the wake of a brutal Republican midterm beatdown, breaking with President Donald Trump on foreign policy and America's relationship with Israel.

A source familiar with post-election happenings told Fox News Digital that there was a White House meeting Wednesday morning aimed at aligning the entire administration officials on post-midterm messaging. A second source familiar described the meeting as a regular senior staff meeting.

Vice President JD Vance called out Democrats on Friday, saying that the midterms will be a referendum on "common sense versus pure unadulterated crazy." (Getty Images)

VANCE'S SCATHING IRAN WAR POSTMORTEM PUTS POTENTIAL 2028 GOP SHOWDOWN IN FOCUS: 'EVERYONE KNOWS WHY'

"The reason we lost is the war," Vance's post said. "The president himself said so last night."

"If you'd like to argue it was worth it, that losing the Senate for the next 6 years was worth it, then do so. Argue it was a worthwhile cost to pay. Agree or disagree, that's at least honest," he went on.

"But everyone knows why we lost. And any honest conversation about where we go from here must start there."

In the post, Vance said a pollster and "one of the smartest people I know in politics" laid out four tasks that would bring voters back to the GOP — end the war in Iran, focus on the cost of living and inflation, put America first, and "change our relationship with Israel."

JD VANCE DECLINES TO ENDORSE NETANYAHU DESPITE TRUMP'S STRONG BACKING

A Trump administration official who spoke with Fox News Digital said that the four tasks are Vance's rallying cry to the "new right," broadly defined as a coalition of Republicans that aligns with the president's original "America First" movement — staying out of foreign conflicts, making America an affordable place for Americans to raise families, and closing the borders.

But those voters, they said, also believe that Trump is too aligned with Israel on foreign policy matters.

Vice President JD Vance points to President Donald Trump at the Republican convention Thursday, Sept. 10, 2026, in Dallas. (Alex Brandon/AP Photo)

Among some on the political right, the war in Iran is intrinsically tied to Israel's influence in the Middle East, and Trump's relationship with Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu.

TRUMP TOLD DONORS 'WE NEED TO ELECT JD,' SOURCE CONFIRMS, AS HE WEIGHS VANCE OR RUBIO IN 2028

The official made it clear that Vance’s message is not a separation from Trump, à la former Vice President Mike Pence who denounced Trump after the Jan. 6, 2021, riot at the capitol. Rather, they said, he is aiming to send a clear message to Republicans who support Trump and disagree with or feel betrayed by his foreign policy.

The White House did not return Fox News Digital's request for comment at time of publication on the meeting or Vance's post-election messaging.

The vice president has not strayed much from Trump and the White House's preferred messaging for the past two years — though he has been one of the few voicing concern over the ongoing war in Iran.

Despite his friendship with Secretary of War Pete Hegseth, reports and comments from the vice president himself have expressed how his stance on the Iran conflict differs.

Trump acknowledged earlier this year that Vance was "maybe less enthusiastic" about attacking Iran from the jump.

TRUMP 'COULDN'T TRANSFER THE LOVE' OF HIS SUPPORTERS TO OTHER REPUBLICANS AMID HISTORIC MIDTERM LOSS

Vance is widely considered a top GOP presidential candidate in 2028 — along with Trump's Secretary of State Marco Rubio.

So far, Democrats stand at 54 Senate seats to Republicans' 44, after sweeping wins in typically red Texas, ruby-red Kansas and other Republican strongholds like South Carolina.

DEMS SEIZE THE SENATE AS BLUE TIDAL WAVE CRASHES DEEP INTO TRUMP COUNTRY, TOPPLES GOP STRONGHOLDS

Votes are still being counted into Wednesday in a tight race in Alaska, that could see Democrats winning yet another seat in the Senate and widening their majority over the GOP.

The outcome of the midterms could have massive implications for the 2028 election, and Republicans are grappling now with how to face one of their biggest-ever challenges in trying to flip back the upper chamber despite a widened gap throwing them deep into a minority in January.

Needing only 218 to control the House, Democrats on Wednesday afternoon had won 237 seats, with more projected to filter in as ballots are counted and races are called.

Peter D'Abrosca is a politics reporter at Fox News Digital.

#5907. Florida congressman diagnoses GOP's blue tsunami losses: 'We failed to make them think we cared about them'

Rep. Randy Fine says Republicans failed to understand what Americans wanted, even as Sunshine state GOP slate bucked the national trend.

As Republicans sift through the wreckage of a devastating election night that handed Democrats control of both chambers of Congress by large margins, one Florida Republican who bucked the national trend says the GOP fundamentally misread the electorate.

In an exclusive interview with Fox News Digital, Congressman Randy Fine — who expanded his own winning margin by five points despite the national backlash — offered a candid assessment of his party's shortcomings and warned that Republicans must now prepare for an "ugly two years" battling Democrats over immigration and other contentious issues.

Representative Randy Fine, a Republican from Florida, outside the US Capitol in Washington, DC, US, on Thursday, Dec. 18, 2025. (Graeme Sloan/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

While Republicans held their ground in Florida, winning the governor's mansion, the Senate race, and maintaining their 20-8 edge in the US House, nationally the GOP got crushed under the weight of a blue tsunami.

ILHAN OMAR HIT WITH EXPULSION THREAT AS GOP LAWMAKER VOWS TO BOOT HER FROM CONGRESS OVER DOJ PROBE

Congressman Fine presents no easy answer to the GOP's divergence in fortunes between Florida and the rest of the country:

"You know, I think that's a hard question to answer. What I would say, I can speak to my race, and I'm actually one of the few Republicans in the country whose margin increased from my last election. I won my special election after having $14 million spent against me. I won it by 14 points. I won last night by 19 points. And so what I can tell you is what worked for me.

"My constituents want a fighter, someone who says what they believe. They know it's not driven by polls or anything else and fights for them, whether it's fighting for their constituent service issue or fighting on illegal immigration or fighting to bring money to the district. And that's why I went up so much. I took my race seriously because I knew the winds were against us.

"And so, Florida, I think just people know how good we have it and they don't want to screw up the state. I think that's why we won all 20 of these races."

Fine refuses to single out anyone in GOP leadership for the electoral outcome, and believes the Republicans must approach each election cycle as a unified team:

Florida Republican gubernatorial candidate, Rep. Byron Donalds, R-Fla., gestures as he arrives to an election rally with U.S. President Donald Trump at Alico Arena on the Florida Gulf Coast University campus on Oct. 23, 2026 in Fort Myers, Fla. (Alex Wong/Getty Images)

"Well, look, I'm not a finger pointer, but what I would say is this. We're a team and the team lost. And so then the whole team bears responsibility and that includes me. I mean, while I did well, my team lost, and I'm not going to be here pointing the fingers at anybody.

FROM CAMPUS RADICALS TO BEIJING'S AI SABOTAGE: REP RANDY FINE SAYS 'US FIGHTING TO SAVE WESTERN CIVILIZATION'

"I think that what we did is we failed to understand what the American people wanted. I think we failed to make them think we cared about them. Now, I didn't fail in my district, but I think nationally people came to believe that we weren't interested in them and that's wrong.

"We were, but we failed to communicate it, and maybe that's part of my special sauce is I know how to communicate better than many of these people."

With Democrats firmly in control of both chambers of Congress, Fine anticipates two years of playing defense, and a continuous fight to explain why Democrats' policies will be harmful to the country:

"I don't know that we're going to push the agenda. I think what we're going to be is we're going to be on defense. We're going to have to fight back. And you know, as my father told me this morning, he goes, 'Randy, you've been a fighter your whole life. You were built for the fight.'

"So we're going to be having to fight against stupid policies that they will be pushing. And it's going to be an ugly two years, but we're gonna have to explain why all of the things that they said they would do during the campaign are so bad. We obviously didn't succeed in doing that. But as they put those policies forward, we're going to have to make the argument for why they would damage America."

Immigration is the single issue that Fine identifies as having the greatest potential to harm the country:

"I think they will seek to reopen the border and allow illegals to flood in. I think they will seek mass amnesty, rewarding all of the illegals that are here. And I think they will stop any requirement for people who come to this country to assimilate. They believe in the ‘third worldification’ of America. And that's going to be the number one thing we have to fight back against."

Florida and Texas painted a particularly stark contrast in electoral outcomes. Fine suggests the Sunshine state GOP was better prepared to face headwinds:

"Well, I think maybe our candidates at the top of the ticket were better. You know, Byron Donalds is an incredible spokesman for the conservative movement. Ashley Moody is a multiple-time winner statewide. And I think Texas allowed itself to slide on a lot of these things.

"They had a big influx of illegal immigrants. And they also really didn't fight back against the Islamification of their state in a way that we have in Florida. Demographics are destiny. And I think, to some degree, Texas was overtaken by that yesterday."

Fine has long been an outspoken critic of Michigan Senator-elect Abdul El-Sayed, and believes he represents a danger to the country:

"Of all the races last night, that one breaks my heart the most. I mean, someone who literally supports Muslim terror, someone whose family is embedded in so many aspects of Muslim terror. Someone who's really a danger to this country being elected to the United States Senate. It's an unspeakably horrible thing, a historically bad thing. Someone who doesn't support the United States. Someone who supports the Muslim Brotherhood.

Democratic Senate Candidate Abdul El-Sayed speaks during a campaign town hall at Cadillac High School on October 10, 2026, in Cadillac, Michigan. (Chip Somodevilla/Getty Images)

"It breaks my heart. I'm not from Michigan, I don't know what they were thinking, but it's a terrible, terrible, terrible day. Look today for me has been a hard day. You know I won my election last night, I was super excited. My margin went up. It's unheard of in this cycle and so I'm super excited, then I watched the bottom fall out of the election results and realized so many good people lost today.

"So it's a challenging day for me, the high of winning so decisively with the low of realizing the battle that we have ahead of us, but God puts us where he wants us to be and I'm a fighter and I am ready for the fight."

Finally, as it appears that either Jim Jordan or Steve Scalise is likely to be the next House Minority Leader, Fine declined to weigh in:

"No, I mean, these are discussions. I've not spoken with either Scalise or Jordan at this point. They've got a lot of phone calls to make, and we'll get to that when we get to that. But it won't be Mike Johnson. He's taken himself out of consideration."

#5899. Ted Cruz says 'blue tsunami' took over Texas after Democrats swept statewide races

In a victory speech after winning the Texas U.S. Senate race, the speaker celebrates a campaign turned into a movement built by underdogs. Emphasizing a politics of love over division, the newly elected senator pledges to represent all Texans, tackle economic inequality, and get big money out of politics to bridge deep political divides.

Sen. Ted Cruz, R-Texas, delivered a scathing assessment on Wednesday of Republicans' sweeping midterm election losses, describing the results as a "blue tsunami" and warning that his party had lost touch with voters, particularly in the Lone Star State.

In a lengthy post on X, Cruz acknowledged that Republicans had suffered historic defeats, losing control of both chambers of Congress while Democrats swept statewide races in Texas.

"2026 was not simply a blue wave; it was a blue tsunami. It was an ass kicking," Cruz wrote. "It was not simply a bad 2006 midterm election. It was a Watergate-level repudiation."

His blunt assessment came after a landmark Democratic breakthrough in traditionally Republican strongholds across the country, including in his home state, where Democrats won every statewide race, ending decades of Republican dominance.

FLORIDA'S DESANTIS DESCRIBES GOP MIDTERMS WIPEOUT AS 'CLOSE TO AN EXTINCTION-LEVEL EVENT' FOR REPUBLICANS

Sen. Ted Cruz, R-Texas, delivers remarks on the second day of the Republican Midterm Convention at the American Airlines Center in Dallas, Texas, on Sept. 10, 2026. (Laura Brett/Sipa USA)

"Nowhere are the consequences of this election greater than Texas," Cruz wrote. "In the Lone Star State, we lost every single statewide election."

"Texas is blue. That is a hard sentence to utter. But Texas, today at least, is blue," he continued.

Democrats scored two major victories in Texas, with James Talarico defeating Republican Attorney General Ken Paxton in the U.S. Senate race — marking the first Democratic Senate victory in the Lone Star State in nearly four decades — and state Rep. Gina Hinojosa unseating longtime Republican Gov. Greg Abbott, according to the Fox News Decision Desk.

TRUMP'S GAMBLE ON KEN PAXTON BACKFIRES AS DEMOCRAT PULLS OFF HISTORIC TEXAS SENATE UPSET

Democratic Senate nominee Texas state Rep. James Talarico speaks at an election night event in Austin, Texas, Tuesday, Nov. 3, 2026. (Eric Gay/AP Photo)

Cruz argued that Republicans' political defeat stemmed from their failure to address growing frustration over the cost of living, as voters continued struggling with everyday expenses.

"Food is too high, gas is too high, diesel is too high, insurance is too high, life is too high," he wrote.

"Understandably, people were pissed off, and elected Republicans didn't do enough to address their very real pain," Cruz said.

He also criticized tariffs and the prolonged war with Iran, arguing that both had contributed to voter frustration.

CONSERVATIVES STUNNED BY 'CATASTROPHIC' MIDTERM BLUE TSUNAMI, BLAME TRUMP'S IRAN WAR, HIGH GAS PRICES

Democrat Gina Hinojosa defeated longtime Republican Gov. Greg Abbott in Texas' 2026 gubernatorial election, marking a historic Democratic victory in the traditionally Republican stronghold, according to the Fox News Decision Desk. (Danielle Villasana/Getty Images)

He said Republicans had lost support among blue-collar workers, young voters, farmers, ranchers and Hispanic voters, while evangelical Christians failed to turn out with the same enthusiasm as in previous elections, concluding that his party had "alienated rural America."

Cruz said he called Talarico after the election to congratulate him on his victory.

"Last night, I called James Talarico. I congratulated him on his very impressive victory," Cruz wrote, adding that the two had a "pleasant conversation" and that he was willing to work with the Democrat on issues benefiting Texas.

Despite the sweeping losses, which also included Democratic wins in the state's lieutenant governor and attorney general races, Cruz expressed optimism that Republicans could regain ground in Texas.

DEMS SEIZE THE SENATE AS BLUE TIDAL WAVE CRASHES DEEP INTO TRUMP COUNTRY, TOPPLES GOP STRONGHOLDS

Sen. Ted Cruz speaks during a roundtable discussion at the U.S. Capitol on March 3, 2025 in Washington, DC. (Kayla Bartkowski/Getty Images)

"But hear me now: we will bring Texas back," he wrote. "Texas will be red again."

Throughout the campaign, Talarico cast himself as a moderate dedicated to representing Texans rather than Democratic interests in Washington.

Paxton, along with President Donald Trump and other national Republicans, labeled Talarico a "communist" and a vegan in barbecue-loving Texas, pointing to his support for the transgender community while in the Texas Legislature and a floor speech in which he said God was "nonbinary."

"We have not suddenly become a bunch of tofu-eating, gun-banning, tutu-wearing pinko commie liberals," Cruz wrote. "That ain’t us. Texas is still Texas."

"We will rebuild as a true blue-collar party, not by embracing socialism, but by being real free-market conservatives," he continued, predicting a political comeback for Republicans.

"It is dark right now, but it is always darkest before the dawn, and morning is coming," he said.

#5898. Kiley loses House reelection bid in California

Rep. #5895. Chuck Schumer heralds Democrats' 'generational remake' of Senate

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., speaks during a press conference at the Democratic Senatorial Campaign Committee in Washington, DC, on Nov. 4, 2026. Saul Loeb/AFP via Getty Images hide caption

Midterms coverage: Live results | Takeaways | Stories around the countryStay connected: NPR Politics Podcast | NPR Politics newsletter | NPR app

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer told reporters Wednesday that the massive blue wave that brought Democrats control of the House and Senate signifies a "generational remake of the Senate map."

"These voters did not give us the majority so we can do whatever we want," the New York Democrat said outside the Democratic Senatorial Campaign Committee in Washington, D.C. "They entrusted us with the majority to do what they — the American people — want."

Democrats secured major gains in red states President Trump has previously carried, including Iowa, Ohio, South Carolina and Texas. Their victories include a history-making win in Kansas, which hasn't sent a Democrat to the Senate in 94 years.

Schumer, who is poised to become Senate Majority leader in January, credited these wins to a crop of candidates who "knew how to tailor their message to the states."

Democrats now have a 54-seat majority, including the two independent senators who caucus with the party. Two races have still not been called: Alaska and Mississippi.

Schumer vowed that "relief is on the way" for voters, and acknowledged that some remain skeptical that Democrats will deliver. He said Democrats are intent on bringing down the cost of living, ending the Iran war, and undertaking investigations into the White House to address allegations of corruption.

"The days of Trump's supplicant Senate are over," Schumer declared.

President Trump hasn't shied away from saying he will use his veto pen on Democratic legislative priorities, but has also signaled some openness to cutting deals with the new Democratic-controlled Congress.

"We are going to work hard to drive costs down with the president or without him," Schumer said.

#5887. Navajo voters hop tractors, make extraordinary efforts to vote after widespread flooding

Lilien Begay, 92, seated at left with daughters (clockwise) Berneice, Darlene, and Vera at the voting site in Denehotso, Arizona on Nov. 3, 2026 Adam Burke/KSUT hide caption

People on the Navajo Reservation had to make extra effort to vote this year.

It's a region of the southwest that's been hit this fall by repeated flooding. Roads washed out, driving thousands of people from their homes.

But voters turned out, in the parts of Arizona, Utah and New Mexico that the reservation stretches across. Yesterday at the Dennehotso Chapterhouse, voters rolled up in trucks with mud-caked tires.

The town is framed by the picturesque sandstone cursive of Comb Ridge, the stone spires of Monument Valley peeking over the ridgeline.

In the parking lot, people gathered to eat fry bread and mutton stew, something common at Navajo polling sites.

"I love to see our people come out and talk amongst each other," said 27-year-old Urbina Yazzie, adding that this year's election is unusual.

"People are having to drive on tractors to get from one road to another, just to come and vote," she said.

Dennehotso has been knocked around by floods this fall, three in the last month. But the big one that residents will talk about for decades to come, started on October 22.

Local contractor Kev Woolboy was helping coordinate response efforts, checking watersheds along the highway, as floodwaters from as far as 60 miles away raced toward town.

"We have some man-made earth dams that were supposed to slow (the water) down," Woolboy said. "But there was so much rain, those earth dams didn't stand a chance."

The floodwaters ravaged homes and newly-built roads, knocking out power and water, and leaving part of the town covered in thick, goopy mud.

"We thought we were safe," said 74-year-old Phyllis Yazzie.

Yazzie was at home with her son that day, having a late breakfast, when her niece called.

"And she says, 'Auntie! You better get out of there!'" Yazzie recalled. "I said, 'Why?' And she says, 'the flood's heading that way.' And so I went outside, I started looking towards the wash, and I saw the water rising.'"

They tried to drive out their dirt road, but were forced back to the highest point on their land, while a torrent of water carved ravines on both sides of their house.

Kev Woolboy at the home of Phyllis Yazzie, and elderly woman he helped rescue from flooding Adam Burke/KSUT hide caption

"We parked the car and we spent the night in the vehicle," Yazzie said. "But they were trying to rescue us."

While Yazzie and her son huddled in their car, Kev Woolboy was working his way toward them, as the local guide for the Navajo Police Department, who had brought in an amphibious vehicle to rescue residents.

"I was jumping into the water and bringing the kids and some of the elders on board and helping them with their personal belongings," Woolboy said.

As it grew dark, and the flood waters rose, Woolboy says it became impossible to tell the difference between the ground and a swirling mix of rainfall and reddish brown silt.

They got close to Phyllis Yazzie's house, Woolboy asked the officers to stop the vehicle.

"I told the guys something doesn't look right," Woolboy recalled. "So they turned on their floodlights to where I could see. There was no road there. We had to turn around, we couldn't get Phyllis that night."

Two weeks later, residents were still digging out. On election day, a backhoe was building a a temporary road so that residents cut off from town could reach the polls at the Chapterhouse

Volunteer drivers, and Chapter employees were shuttling people there, too. Some people drove hours to get there.

Vera Begay was there, with her 92-year-old mother Lilien Begay, who lost her home and all of her identification documents.

"She's never missed her vote- all her life she's come here for voting," said Vera Begay.

The elder Begay, speaking in Navajo, said she is emotionally devastated.

This year in Dennehoto, and across the region, flooding has brought out the best in communities.

"It means a lot more to our people to come out and vote," said 27-year-old Urbina Yazzie. "Because this is a very very important time to do so, especially with the flood and all that's happening all over the Navajo Nation. It's tough out here."

But on election day, people who may disagree about politics were laughing and crying together, bringing their community back to life.

#5882. It was a big night for doctors to win seats in Congress, riding the blue wave

Dr. Annie Andrews, who won the race for Senate in South Carolina, talked about the measles outbreak in the state in her campaign. She's pictured at an election night party in Charleston. Mic Smith/AP hide caption

The U.S. Senate will soon have a practicing physician who's a Democrat for the first time since 1938, with the election of Dr. Annie Andrews, a pediatrician, in South Carolina.

Her win — among many other physician candidates Tuesday night — marks a dramatic shift when it comes to doctors with political power across the country. Historically, doctors have been elected to Congress more often as Republicans, not Democrats. But that changed this cycle.

Check out the NPR Politics Podcast for daily analysis of the biggest news out of Washington, D.C.

In the recent past, the Republican party attracted doctor-candidates who were essentially small business people who had concerns about high taxes and government overreach. The new crop of candidates from health professions often work for large hospital systems and are interested in issues that align with Democratic priorities like public health, equity and affordability.

Andrews told NPR before she won that she was motivated to enter politics after watching parents worry about the cost of things like inhalers for asthma patients and insulin for kids with diabetes.

"I watch as worried parents leave the hospital in the evening so that they can go work their third shift job just so they can make ends meet," she said. "And then, like the majority of pediatricians in America, I've cared for kids who've been shot." Guns are the leading cause of death for children in the U.S.

"It really clicked to me that the path to catalyzing this change that I want to see in America for the children in America is through politics," she explained.

In the current Senate, all four of the physicians are Republicans. Two of them, Sen. Bill Cassidy, R.-La., and Sen. Roger Marshall, R-Kan, lost their reelection bids this year. With the election of South Carolina's Andrews and Dr. Abdul El-Sayed, an epidemiologist, in Michigan, there will now be an equal number of Republican and Democratic physicians in the upper chamber. (El-Sayed trained as a physician but did not do a residency or practice medicine, instead pursuing public health.)

In the House, the six members of the Democratic doctor's caucus will be joined by at least two more: New Jersey's Dr. Adam Hamawy, a former U.S. Army surgeon, and pediatrician Dr. Richard Pan in California. In the GOP doctor's caucus, neurosurgeon Dr. John Cowan was elected in Georgia, but at least two incumbents lost their seats, with a few races still too close to call.

Many of these new Democratic physician-lawmakers were recruited and backed by 314 Action, a name that refers to the mathematical constant pi. The organization supports progressive candidates with science backgrounds to run for office, and it invested more than $30 million in dozens of Democratic candidates competing in state and federal races, including 48 physicians.

The group's president, Shaughnessy Naughton, says that the organization does leadership development and recruits scientists to run across the country, then helps candidates in a variety of ways.

"We work to help them put together their campaign strategy, communication strategy, media training, we help to get them local and national media exposure, work with them on their fundraising, and fundraise for them," she explains.

Those candidates won big time on Tuesday. In addition to big national names like Senators-elect Andrews and El-Sayed, and Governor-elect Dr. Amy Acton of Ohio, many of the downballot candidates 314 Action supported won as well. Among them are several incoming state legislators who will be the only elected physicians in their chambers: Dr. Nikhil Wagle in Iowa, Dr. Amelia Gallitano-Mendel in Arizona, and Dr. Jarod Fox in Florida.

Ohio Democratic gubernatorial candidate Dr. Amy Acton at her victory party in Columbus, Ohio. Carolyn Kaster/AP hide caption

Naughton attributes that success to health care and affordability being front of mind. Also, polls show the public trusts doctors, and the candidates effectively countered messaging from Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr., a longtime anti-vaccine activist who has downplayed ongoing measles outbreaks.

"People were sick of it," Naughton says. "We should not have American children dying — or anyone dying — of measles in 2026. And yet, not only did we have that happening, we had a health secretary that only made the situation worse."

Outgoing chair of the powerful Health, Education, Labor and Pensions committee, Sen. Bill Cassidy, R.-La., spoke often from the dais about how his experience as a physician shaped his perspective as a lawmaker and committee chair.

During Kennedy's confirmation hearing in Jan. 2025, Cassidy memorably spoke about the root of his passion for vaccination. "In the early 2000's, I was loading a patient onto an air ambulance, an 18-year-old young woman, to go to get a liver transplant from acute liver failure due to hepatitis B," he said, noting the expensive surgery and ongoing hospital bills she would face, if she lived. "It was the worst day of my medical career, because I thought: $50 of vaccines could have prevented this all."

Still, Cassidy ultimately cast a key vote to confirm Kennedy into the job, despite Kennedy's long history of anti-vaccine activism. Kennedy flaunted vaccine-related promises Cassidy said he made to earn that vote, and even supported the Republican primary challenger who managed to unseat Cassidy earlier this year.

Sen. Roger Marshall, R-Kan., speaks before President Donald Trump at a campaign rally at the Salina Regional Airport, Sunday, Nov. 1, 2026, in Salina Kan. Charlie Riedel/AP hide caption

In a surprise Tuesday night, Dr. Roger Marshall, R.-Kan., also lost his reelection bid — another senior member of the HELP committee. Marshall is an OBGYN and staunch supporter of Kennedy. In September, the New York Times reported that Marshall had sued more than 700 patients for unpaid medical bills. One of the patients filed a class action lawsuit against him last week. Marshall described his actions as a way to "keep the lights on" in a last-minute social media video. He lost to challenger Adam Hamilton, a Methodist minister, by four points.

Dr. Chadd Kraus has studied physicians in Congress. Kraus is now an emergency room doctor and the vice chair for research for the Department of Emergency and Hospital Medicine at Jefferson Health Lehigh Valley in Pennsylvania. Two decades ago, after studying political science as an undergrad and pursuing a masters in public health, he worked on an analysis of the physicians in Congress.

"In 1776, 10.7% of the signers of the Declaration of Independence were physicians," the paper notes. Kraus explains that was an era where practicing medicine involved leeches; germs hadn't been discovered and antibiotics hadn't been invented. Kraus suggests those physicians happened to be "part of a class that owned land that was educated."

In the next two hundred years, medicine transformed, and for a time, very few physicians served as federal lawmakers. "In the period of time that we studied [from 1960-2004], there were 25 physicians total who had served in the U.S. Congress, whether the House or the Senate," he explains. "In the current Congress, there are as many physicians — nearly — as there were in that whole period of time that we studied, which was several decades."

To be specific, there are 20 physicians in the current Congress, and Republicans outnumber Democrats two to one. There are six Democrats and 10 Republicans who serve in the House. All four doctors in the Senate are Republicans.

In the new Congress, there will be at least 18 physicians in Congress, 10 of them Democrats. Depending on the outcome of several House races, Democratic physician-lawmakers may outnumber Republican physician-lawmakers. Another notable win was in Ohio, where oncology nurse practitioner Kristina Knickerbocker flipped a Republican House seat. Nurse Lauren Underwood, a Democrat, held onto her House seat in Illinois.

In addition to Republicans Cassidy and Marshall losing their Senate seats, in the house, Rep. Neal Dunn of Florida, a urologist, retired, and Reps. Mariannette Miller-Meeks, an ophthalmologist in Iowa, and Jen Kiggans, a nurse in Virginia lost their seats.

Kraus says, when it comes to making laws, "physicians have a valuable perspective, not a monopoly on good policy," he says. "And the need for physician voices in government has grown, I think, over the course of time, as healthcare has become more complex and politically consequential."

He says the emergency department, especially, is a place where you can see the effect of health policy in real time. He recalls at the beginning of the opioid crisis, how emergency physicians were among the first to sound the alarm to policymakers. When the uninsured rates increase, they see patients coming in sicker than they would otherwise be, he says. "We see the challenges of health policy and the consequences of it," he says

Many of the new members of Congress campaigned on health policy issues, like the cost of health insurance and prescription drugs. There's widespread dissatisfaction with the cost of health care across the political spectrum. Democratic leaders have said that reversing Republican cuts to premium subsidies for health insurance and work requirements for Medicaid will be at the top of their agenda.

Beyond those immediate issues, more than 15 years after the passage of the Affordable Care Act, there may be political appetite and public demand for major new health legislation in the coming years. Some candidates ran on Medicare-for-all, a national health insurance plan, which has become increasingly popular in public opinion surveys.

"I think we all certainly agree, no matter one's political views or perspective, that there is the potential for a better system," Kraus says. "I do think that having physicians and other health care professionals meaningfully incorporated into the policymaking process is really critical to finding what those innovations might be."

Naughton of 314 Action says the health-professional candidates her group supported care about and have ideas about how to change the health care system, but that hasn't been their only pitch to voters.

"I do think that they really lead with what they essentially are, which is problem solvers," she says. "We can fix this problem — we can make health care accessible and affordable — and not make it a choice between life or debt."

Lexie Schapitl contributed reporting to this story.
