#5874. Democratic leaders reflect on midterm results and what's next for the party

Michigan Democratic Senate candidate Abdul El-Sayed speaks during a midterm election night watch party at the Department at Hudson's on November 4 in Detroit, Michigan. Salwan Georges/Getty Images/Getty Images North America hide caption

Democrats coasted into victory late last night, taking control of both the House of Representatives and the Senate by considerable margins. Several states that are traditionally characterized as "deep red," including Texas, Kansas and Iowa, flipped towards Democrats in the Senate for the first time in decades. Democratic candidates also took a convincing majority in House races, despite competing in several states whose districts were redrawn to favor Republicans.

Across the country, Democratic gubernatorial candidates also burst ahead, winning races in GOP strongholds like Georgia, Ohio and Texas. Heading into 2027, Democrats will officially retake control of Congress and hold at least 28 governorships.

Newly elected Democratic officials, party leaders and pollsters joined Morning Edition on Wednesday morning to share their insights on the massive wave of Democratic victories in state and local races. In their interviews with NPR's Steve Inskeep and Michel Martin, these Democrats discussed takeaways from the midterm results, the effects of mid-decade redistricting efforts, the mandate received from voters and the next steps for the party. You can read some key takeaways from the discussions below.

Democrat Abdul El-Sayed made history in Michigan, becoming the first Muslim elected to the Senate as Democrats retake control of Congress. He said his opponent, Republican Mike Rogers, campaigned weaponizing El-Sayed's name and faith rather than addressing issues Americans really care about, like Medicare for All and the price of everyday items.

El-Sayed also discussed his opinions on legislation surrounding the war in Iran, and he agreed with Trump's scheduled end to roughly $4 billion in annual military aid to Israel.

Martin asked him about his lack of support from some Jewish and Black voters, something El Sayed said he hopes to remedy.

"There's always more work to do. There's always more to build. There's always more conversations to have," he said.

To listen to Abdul El-Sayed's full Morning Edition interview, click here.

Eric Holder, chairman of the National Democratic Redistricting Committee and attorney general under former President Obama, discussed how mid-decade redistricting affected the results of the midterm. Speaking with Martin, he said that GOP gerrymandering efforts backfired, causing candidates to lose in districts that were specifically drawn for their success.

"The redistricting efforts that [Republicans] had didn't just fail — they were dismantled," Holder says.

To listen to Eric Holder's full Morning Edition interview, click here.

Democrats in Georgia won seats up and down the ballot, and Stacey Abrams, founder of Fair Fight Action, said the party has been building towards this outcome since 2010. In her interview with Martin, she added that she's proud of Governor-elect Keisha Lance Bottoms, the first Black woman elected governor in United States history. Abrams campaigned for the same gubernatorial spot in 2018 and again in 2020, though she was never elected to the position.

Abrams said Democratic victories for the governor, attorney general, secretary of state and in the state house "gives us an opportunity to start correcting and restoring rights that we've seen lost across the South for more than 20 years."

To listen to Stacey Abram's full Morning Edition interview, click here.

Ken Martin, chairman of the Democratic National Committee, said yesterday's election marks the biggest win for his party in more than a century. Voters gave Democrats a mandate to address affordability, health care and the systems that exclusively benefit wealthy Americans, he explained to Martin and Inskeep, and he thinks newly elected Democrats can deliver.

"Our candidates across the board ran for something, not against something," he said. "They gave people a sense of a positive vision of what Democrats would do while we're in power."

To listen to Ken Martin's full Morning Edition interview, click here.

Pollster and Democratic strategist Margie Omero explained why last night's results are a direct reflection of voter's distrust of leaders in Washington. She said contested races consistently broke towards Democrats, despite high GOP spending during campaign season.

"Voters really wanted to send a real clear message, and they couldn't have been any clearer than what we heard last night," Omero said.

To listen to Margie Omero's full Morning Edition interview, click here.

#5870. Elon Musk will face scrutiny from Democrats after midterms

Elon Musk gave more than $445 million to Republican candidates, political action committees and outside groups, according to federal election filings but most of the candidates he backed on Tuesday lost. Kevin Dietsch/Getty Images hide caption

Elon Musk wasn't on Tuesday's ballot, but he stands to lose a lot from Democrats' sweeping midterm victories in the House and Senate. In no state was this more acutely felt than in Texas, home to the headquarters of Musk's companies and where he spent more than $200 million, according to federal filings, in a mission to sway the Senate race in the Republican candidate's favor.

With a Democratic majority in Congress, the billionaire, who controls Tesla and SpaceX, will likely face investigations into the billions of dollars of government contracts his companies have been awarded since President Trump returned to office and his role leading the Department of Government Efficiency at the beginning of Trump's second term.

Musk gave more than $445 million to Republican candidates, political action committees and outside groups, according to federal election filings analyzed by OpenSecrets, a nonprofit that tracks campaign spending. That makes him the top donor in the midterms and among the very largest political donors in American history.

The central question Democratic lawmakers say they want answered is whether Musk used his influence inside the Trump administration to benefit his own businesses, and whether taxpayer money, government data and public institutions were put at risk in the process.

During the four months Musk led DOGE, he received at least $6.3 billion in government contracts. Since then, he has gained at least another $10 billion in government contracts, according to the Democracy Defenders Fund, a legal advocacy nonprofit.

"The DOGE chickens are coming home to roost. Elon Musk waved a chainsaw on stage and laughed while firing public servants and gutting the services Americans rely on. Then he lied to the American people about the savings. He must answer for it under oath," Rep. James Walkinshaw (D-Va.) wrote on X.

Musk's foreign dealings are also under scrutiny. Democratic Sen. Chris Coons of Delaware posted on X that Musk's links to Russia's President Vladimir Putin are "One more thing to add to the list of questions for Musk when I subpoena him."

U.S. Rep. Mike Levin of California highlighted that Musk restricted Ukraine's use of his Starlink satellite internet service days after Trump eased sanctions on Russia and began buying Russian diesel in October. Starlink and its military-grade counterpart, Starshield, are vital for Ukraine's civilian infrastructure, frontline communications and drone operations in its war against Russia.

"How on earth did we get to this point? How is it possible that Musk can disrupt an American ally's military operations whenever he chooses, maintain a clear line to the Kremlin, and face zero accountability to the American people or the U.S. government?" Levin wrote on X.

Musk did not respond to a request for comment from NPR.

While leading DOGE, Musk was a part-time special government employee, and as such was subject to a federal law prohibiting covered executive branch and related personnel from participating in official matters affecting their personal financial interests, said Richard Painter, former chief White House ethics lawyer in the George W. Bush administration.

"SpaceX is a big contractor and gets a lot of money from NASA and the Department of Defense. If [Musk] touched anything that he knew would have an impact on SpaceX, he would've committed a crime," Painter said.

Texas in particular has become a big focus of Musk's efforts to exert political power, as he has made the state the nerve center of his sprawling business empire. Tesla's corporate headquarters and a major vehicle manufacturing facility are in Austin. X, formerly Twitter, is based outside of Austin. SpaceX, which makes rockets and satellites, is based in southern Texas. Musk's Neuralink and The Boring Company are also headquartered in the state.

Much of Musk's estimated wealth is tied up in his ownership stakes in his companies. Even as markets rose Wednesday after Democrats' midterm wins, SpaceX shares fell nearly 4% and Tesla dropped 3%.

SpaceX is reportedly exploring several possible Texas sites for large data centers. Tesla owns and operates its massive Giga Texas data center in Austin. In August, plans for a $16.8 billion SpaceXAI semiconductor fabrication project in Texas were announced.

Musk directed at least $225 million of his midterm spending to Texas, according to OpenSecrets, including supporting Republican Ken Paxton's bid for U.S. Senate and Gov. Greg Abbott's reelection campaign.

But Paxton and Abbott both lost, suggesting Musk's money wasn't enough to overcome a wave of enthusiasm that saw Democrats capture every statewide office in Texas.

"Elon Musk wants our land. He wants our water. He wants our tax dollars. He wants his very own personal United States senator. He is trying to buy Texas," said James Talarico, who defeated Paxton in the Senate race, at a campaign rally in Lubbock last month.

Elon Musk's own posts on X about Talarico were often personal and incendiary. "Talarico will vote to trans your kids," he wrote on Nov. 1.

Talarico addressed Musk in his victory speech on Tuesday.

"We just sent a message to every billionaire mega-donor, every trillionaire mega-donor, every corrupt defender of this broken political system in this state, in this country. Power belongs to the people," Talarico said. "The real fight in this country is not right versus left; it is top versus bottom."

As results came in on Tuesday night, Musk wrote on X, "Guess my election-interfering space lasers didn't work this time," followed by a shrug emoji.

#5861. Where voters stand on state ballot measures, from abortion to LGBTQ rights to guns

Voters weighed in on 146 statewide ballot measures across dozens of states. Mario Tama/Getty Images hide caption

Tuesday's midterm elections were a huge sweep for Democrats, among other takeaways.

And across 39 states — plus Washington, D.C. and the Northern Mariana Islands — voters weighed in on 146 statewide ballot measures spanning issues from taxes to transgender rights to traffic cameras to data centers and AI.

Three states moved to preserve abortion access

The U.S. Supreme Court overturned Roe v. Wade in 2022, leaving the legality and regulation of abortion up to each state. In subsequent elections, over a dozen states have put access to abortion on their ballots — and overwhelmingly moved to secure it.

This year, four states considered abortion measures. Three passed, while Idaho voted to preserve the state's abortion ban from 2022.

In Missouri, voters rejected a Republican-led amendment that would have banned abortion with few exceptions. The move affirms support for abortion just two years after voters mobilized to reject Missouri's ban, which was one of the strictest in the country.

Nevada residents voted to enshrine abortion up to the point of viability in their constitution. They did so for the second election in a row, since state law requires a proposed constitutional amendment to pass twice.

Virginians voted to create a "fundamental right to reproductive freedom" in their constitution. That includes the right to an abortion during the first two trimesters (and afterward in certain cases), as well as the right to contraception, fertility, postpartum care and more.

Many states considered changes to how they run their elections.

It was too early to call Wednesday afternoon whether voters had approved some of the more far-reaching changes, in Alaska and Arizona.

Other states — including Nevada, North Carolina and Ohio — passed measures toughening identification requirements for voters, while California voted against such an effort.

Several states — including Alaska, Arkansas, Kansas, South Dakota and West Virginia — approved measures reinforcing that U.S. citizenship is required to vote in their elections.

Redistricting was also top of mind for voters. President Trump kicked off a redistricting race earlier this year by urging Republican-led states to redraw their districts ahead of the midterms. Republicans and Democrats in multiple states did so. But many of the districts altered to boost GOP prospects ultimately went to Democratic candidates.

Missouri voters rejected a Republican-friendly congressional map, while Maryland voters passed a redistricting amendment that could pave the way for a Democrat-leaning map in 2028.

Virginians passed a measure automatically restoring voting rights to anyone convicted of a felony once they have completed their sentence.

Virginians also passed a constitutional amendment officially repealing its ban on gay marriage and adding language confirming that the commonwealth will recognize marriage between two adults regardless of their sex, gender or race.

Virginia's ban has been moot since the Supreme Court made same-sex marriage legal nationwide in 2015, but this measure is seen as a safeguard in case Obergefell v. Hodges gets reversed (as some have feared in the wake of the court's overturning of Roe v. Wade). Other states have made similar moves, including Colorado and California in 2024.

Vermont overwhelmingly voted to add an amendment to its constitution guaranteeing equal protection under the law "race, ethnicity, sex, religion, disability, sexual orientation, gender identity, gender expression, or national origin."

A number of states considered measures restricting transgender kids' access to school sports teams and gender-affirming care, with Colorado passing both. A Washington state measure to ban trans students from competing in girls' school sports was still leading on Wednesday.

Arizona passed a proposal requiring schools and athletic associations to restrict students, athletes and employees from using facilities — including restrooms, locker rooms, and other private spaces — that are not designated for their sex assigned at birth. Arizona law already bans trans girls from playing on school sports teams designated for girls, and this measure would extend that prohibition to recreational organizations like Little League.

One of the most high-profile ballot measures asked Floridians to weigh cutting property taxes for homeowners, which critics say would take billions of dollars in local government revenue away from much-needed public services like food banks, libraries, transportation and infrastructure. Voters overwhelmingly passed the amendment.

In California, voters pondered whether to approve a one-off 5% tax on individuals with more than $1 billion in assets, with most of the money raised going toward health care services. The fight against that ballot measure was one of the most expensive in the state's history. The contest is too close to call as of Wednesday afternoon.

Tennessee voters approved a constitutional amendment prohibiting its government from levying a statewide property tax, which it has not collected since 1949. Arizona voters passed a measure that bans cities and towns from raising taxes on groceries until July 2027. North Carolinians approved an amendment to cap the maximum personal income tax at 3.5%.

Washington voters considered whether to repeal an income tax on individuals and households earning more than $1 million, which is scheduled to take effect in 2028. Early results indicate they want to keep that tax.

A polar-opposite pair of gun ballot measures

Massachusetts voted to keep a 2024 law tightening firearm regulations. The law made Massachusetts gun control measures among the strictest in the nation, as it also included requiring "ghost guns" to have a serial number, broadened the definition of "assault-style firearm" and expanded the state's red flag laws.

Meanwhile, in Idaho, a ballot measure asked voters for help choosing the official state gun from six different brands and models. The Winchester Model 1873 — aka the "Gun that Won the West" — leads in the polls as of Wednesday, though the state legislature would need to pass a separate law to make the winner official.

The ballot initiatives about data centers

At least 38 jurisdictions voted on the sprawling facilities needed to power AI, and Tuesday revealed how many voters dislike them. Jurisdictions in California, Florida, Illinois, Kansas, Maine, Nevada, New Jersey, North Dakota, Ohio, and Wisconsin voted to restrict data centers.

Of these, at least 12 localities voted to restrict the size of data centers, while six others voted to halt development and ban construction of all facilities, according to Data Centers on the Ballot.

The one community that supported data centers is in Harwood, North Dakota, where 75% of voters did not want to place restrictions on industrial projects, including data centers.

Ohio, one of the frontline states on the issue, had 21 ballot measures mentioning data centers. Voters there moved to restrict the AI facilities: Trenton, Ohio voted to ban data centers over 25 megawatts, while Hubbard moved to ban construction of data centers of any size, including converting existing buildings. Ohio voters also rejected rezoning areas from rural residential to light industrial for data center usage.

Candidates spent up to $40 million on ads about data centers this election cycle. They were a contentious issue in many statewide elections, as 77% of Americans say they are concerned about data centers raising electricity costs, according to a June Reuters/Ipsos poll. The poll also showed two-thirds of Democrats and half of Republicans said they'd oppose a data center in their community.

Alabama voters passed ballot measures requiring local school boards to adopt policies directing public K-12 schools to recite the Pledge of Allegiance and, separately, play or perform the first stanza of "The Star-Spangled Banner" at least once a week during school hours.

In Arizona, voters passed a measure banning the state from installing and using photo enforcement systems (like speed and red-light cameras) to catch people who violate certain traffic ordinances.

Residents of Washington, D.C. had just one ballot initiative to consider: banning the controversial duck or goose liver dish known as foie gras. The debate went viral in recent days, prompting a slew of Halloween costumes, a four-course restaurant tasting menu and public appearances by a real duck named Olivia. D.C. voted overwhelmingly to prohibit the force-feeding of birds to enlarge their livers, and the sale of the delicacy itself. Across the country, voters in Denver approved a similar measure.

#5858. Mach debuts autonomous mowing platform for solar project sites

Autonomous technology developer Mach is launching a platform for adapting commercial lawn mowers for autonomous deployment on solar project sites. This technology factory integrates with major commercial mowers.

“Utility-scale solar sites present one of the most demanding operational environments in off-road autonomy: narrow clearances, dense panel shadows, GPS-degraded zones and zero tolerance for infrastructure strikes,” said Colin Hurd, CEO of Mach. “By delivering our full-stack autonomy as a Tier-1 partner to commercial equipment OEMs, we provide solar asset owners with the rugged, dealer-supported machinery they already trust, supercharged with driverless capability. This ecosystem approach enables solar developers to scale vegetation management across gigawatts while giving equipment manufacturers an immediate, turnkey path to lead the autonomous solar market.”

Using Mach’s proprietary RTK-GNSS automation infrastructure, mowers are designed to work automatically in conditions with dusty, tall grass and sun glare while avoiding structural and electrical elements on solar arrays. These mowers can be deployed on multiple sites and managed by a single operator.

#5853. CPP Wind Engineering develops wind testing certification for utility-scale solar

CPP Wind Engineering, a global wind engineering consultancy for solar, is launching a wind certification program for utility-scale solar projects, which the organization believes is the first of its kind in the industry. The new program aims to improve the performance and durability of utility-scale solar over a range of wind conditions.

The program offers two services. Site Certification confirms that each project has properly assessed climate and site conditions, implemented an effective stow strategy, suitably positioned anemometers and used a qualified tracker or fixed-tilt system. System Qualification is a one-time review of a tracker or fixed-tilt product line’s wind engineering design data and methodology.

CPP’s new program is built from the organization’s 45 years of experience in wind engineering, including wind tunnel testing of solar projects, wind stow and siting studies and forensic investigation of wind damage to solar projects. Tornado risk falls outside the certification program and is assessed separately using a probabilistic risk approach.

“Wind governs how a solar power plant is built, yet there has been no consistent way to show that the wind loads behind a design were derived and applied correctly,” said David Banks, president of CPP Wind Engineering. “Most of the wind damage we investigate occurs at wind speeds sites see every year, and most of it can be engineered out. This program gives owners, manufacturers and their advisors an independent basis for confirming the reliability of wind engineering. Tornadoes, on the other hand, can’t be engineered out. We must predict the probability of loss for those.”

For project owners and developers, CPP’s Site Certification provides comparisions of mounting design options and a documented record of wind engineering diligence for financing, insurance and project sales. For manufacturers, CPP’s System Qualification evaluates the wind engineering basis of a tracker or fixed-tilt product line, providing a foundation for determining its suitability for site-specific wind conditions. For independent engineers, CPP serves as a partner, contributing wind engineering expertise to the due diligence they lead.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5847. Op-ed: Plug-in solar expands access to renewables, but needs a strong safety framework

By Michael Becker, Global Business Director, Energy Storage/Energy & Power, CSA | October 29, 2026

Access to plug-in solar is becoming increasingly available across North America. As more consumers explore technologies such as balcony solar and plug-in photovoltaic (PIPV) systems, these emerging solutions have the potential to make renewable energy more accessible and support broader participation in the energy transition. Solar remains one of the most accessible and promising renewable energy tools being added to the grid, and plug-in solar products may help expand that access to consumers who have traditionally faced economic barriers to participation.

As adoption grows, however, products designed to be convenient and accessible must still be treated as electrical products that interact with a building’s infrastructure. Product design, connectors, wiring, system integration and installation practices all play an important role in safety and performance. Expanding access and maintaining confidence in emerging technologies are complementary goals, and achieving both will require continued attention to the frameworks that support responsible deployment.

Clear safety standards, code-compliant installation practices and appropriate regulatory requirements will be essential to helping ensure that innovation can reach more consumers safely and responsibly. Solar-related issues are often associated not with the panels themselves, but with connectors, wiring and improper installation practices. A major misconception is that consumers can simply purchase a solar panel with a standard plug and connect it to a household outlet. Responsible deployment of PIPV systems depends on several complementary elements working together: products evaluated to appropriate safety standards, compliance with the National Electrical Code (NEC), qualified installation practices, and applicable state and local requirements.

No single element alone can provide the framework needed to support broad and responsible adoption.

An important emerging component of that framework is UL 3700, which is helping establish a product-safety benchmark specifically for plug-in photovoltaic systems. UL 3700 can provide manufacturers with a clearer benchmark for product development and evaluation, while giving regulators greater confidence as they consider how plug-in PV should be addressed within electrical codes and regulatory frameworks. Continued industry involvement in the development of UL 3700 will also be important as the standard evolves toward broader consensus and adoption. At the same time, UL 3700 is only one part of the overall approach. It does not replace NEC compliance, qualified installation practices or applicable state and local requirements that govern the deployment of electrical systems.

Emerging safety benchmarks are already moving from concept to practice. CSA has certified a microinverter to UL 3700, providing an early example of how the standard can be applied to products entering the market. The certification demonstrates how a consistent benchmark can be applied as new technologies enter the market, while helping support confidence among regulators, manufacturers and consumers as plug-in solar continues to develop.

The regulatory landscape for PIPV continues to evolve, with proposed changes to the NEC, state laws and local codes and ordinances aimed at facilitating the safe installation of these systems. As the industry works to establish appropriate requirements, keeping standards, codes and regulatory frameworks aligned with technological advancement will be critical to supporting the growth of PIPV products. Well-aligned requirements can help create a pathway for innovation while maintaining consistency in how emerging technologies are evaluated and deployed.

Technology itself continues to advance rapidly. Improvements in inverter technology are helping make solar more affordable while enabling greater integration of renewable energy into the grid. As inverters become more energy-dense and cost-effective, they can help lower the overall cost of PV systems and support broader adoption. New inverter capabilities can also provide valuable grid-support functions that help integrate more renewable resources and contribute to a more flexible and resilient electric grid. As these technologies evolve, standards, codes and regulatory requirements must continue evolving alongside them so that innovation and deployment can advance together.

Ultimately, greater accessibility brings greater responsibility. As plug-in solar technologies become easier for consumers to adopt, the safety frameworks that support them must keep pace with innovation. By developing appropriate product standards, maintaining NEC-compliant installation practices, utilizing qualified installers and advancing state and local requirements alongside technological progress, the industry can help expand access to renewable energy while maintaining the confidence of consumers, manufacturers, regulators installers and other stakeholders.

The statement “A major misconception is that consumers can simply purchase a solar panel with a standard plug and connect it to a household outlet.” is, in and of itself a misconception. If the plugin systems are safely designed, that is exactly what they are meant to prov ide the consumer–a solar panel with a plug they can connect to a household outlet. The fact that several millions of such systems have already been plugged in around the world without incident amply demonstrates their safety. All the hand-wringing about safety seems to be originating with existing vested interests that stand to loose significant revenues built on the existing over-complicated and over-regulated systems.

Helpful safety framing. One practical reader check is to separate product evaluation from deployment permission: UL 3700 does not replace NEC compliance, installation instructions, or state/local and utility rules, and effective dates vary by state. PluginForSolar.com maintains a U.S. state-by-state guide plus exact-system readiness checks so readers can verify those layers before buying.

→ Baca artikel asli

#5843. DTE utility program funded a 132-MW Michigan solar project

DTE Energy’s 132-MW Fish Creek Solar Park near Sheridan, Michigan, is now operational. The $268 million development was fully funded by customers who voluntarily participate in DTE’s MIGreenPower program. The solar park can generate enough energy to power approximately 30,000 homes.

“Our customers expect us to keep the lights on and keep bills as affordable as possible, and bringing new generation like Fish Creek online is part of how we deliver on that commitment,” said Matt Paul, president and COO of DTE Electric. “Fish Creek adds more Michigan-made energy to our system while supporting local jobs, generating new tax revenue and delivering lasting benefits for the communities that helped make this project possible.”

Fish Creek will generate tax revenue, supporting regional infrastructure, such as schools, first responders, road maintenance and veteran and senior citizen services. The solar park also created hundreds of local jobs during construction and will provide participating landowners with supplemental income.

In addition, Bushnell and Evergreen townships each received $330,000 through Michigan’s Renewables Ready Communities Award, a grant available to local governments that host large-scale renewable energy projects. The townships plan to invest the funding in town hall and fire station renovations, along with other community improvements.

Fish Creek is part of DTE’s broader plan to meet Michigan’s growing energy needs with a mix of resources. DTE currently has 20 wind parks and 37 solar parks across Michigan, which together generate enough clean energy to power nearly 1 million homes. DTE’s 2026 Integrated Resource Plan includes an additional 15 GW of renewable energy by 2042.

When we connect job seekers with employers, we bridge the gap between opportunity and talent, strengthen the workforce, and ensure that the people who need access to these careers have a seat at the table.” said Lyric Darru, grants manager of workforce development at GRID Alternatives. “By connecting job seekers directly with employers and opening the door to the broader RE+ expo at no cost, we’re helping more people explore pathways into clean energy careers and ensuring the benefits of this growing industry reach more communities. We thank our partners and sponsors for their support in bringing this event to Las Vegas.”

In 2025, more than 37,000 people attended RE+, an event with more than 1,300 exhibitors from businesses in solar, energy storage, electric vehicles, grid technologies and other sectors. This year’s conference will take place November 16–19 at the Las Vegas Convention Center.

“WRISE is excited to co-host this important event with GRID again this year,” said Lauren Friedman, senior manager of programs and events at WRISE. “The continued success of the job fair year over year speaks to the lasting strength and enthusiasm for our industry. We’re glad to make these opportunities available to the WRISE membership and our larger clean energy community.”

#5814. Hyundai IONIQ 3 Named European Car of the Year 2027 Finalist

SEOUL/OFFENBACH — Hyundai Motor’s all-electric IONIQ 3 has been named one of seven finalists for the European Car of the Year 2027 award. Selected by an independent jury of automotive journalists from across Europe, the nomination places IONIQ 3 among the contenders for one of Europe’s most established automotive awards. The winner will be announced on 8 January 2027 at the Brussels Motor Show.

Designed in Europe and produced at Hyundai Motor Türkiye’s İzmit plant, IONIQ 3 has been developed specifically for European customers. The fully electric compact hatchback combines Hyundai’s distinctive ‘Art of Steel’ design language, a spacious interior and intuitive technology with a driving range of up to 497 km (WLTP) in the Long Range version. With a starting price below €30,000 across European markets, IONIQ 3 aims to make electric mobility accessible to a broader range of customers.

“Being named a finalist for European Car of the Year is important recognition for IONIQ 3 and for the work that has gone into its development. We set out to create an electric car designed around the needs of European customers, combining distinctive design with class-leading aerodynamics, strong driving range and exceptional interior space, together with advanced technology at an accessible price. The response from customers has been very encouraging, with orders already exceeding our initial expectations. This nomination reflects the strength of that overall package and our ambition to make electric mobility both desirable and practical for more people.” —Christopher McKinnon, Vice President, Head of Product and Product Director Office for Europe at Hyundai Motor Europe.

IONIQ 3 combines several class-leading features that address key requirements of European customers. Its aerodynamic design contributes to driving efficiency, while its spacious interior offers the practicality of a larger vehicle despite its compact exterior dimensions. Intuitive technology supports everyday usability, and accessible pricing makes electric mobility available to a broader range of customers.

The European Car of the Year award is an independent automotive competition established in 1964. Its international jury consists of 65 automotive journalists from across Europe, who evaluate eligible new vehicles based on criteria including design, innovation, performance, value for money and market positioning.

Seven finalists are selected from the initial list of candidates. Following further evaluation and voting, the overall winner of the 2027 award will be announced on 8 January 2027 at the Brussels Motor Show.

#5808. Can Silicon Valley Build A Safe Class 8 Truck? Early EV Semi Fires Expose The Experience Gap

If you drove through the East Bay area of California on Tuesday night, you probably got caught in a nightmare. A Tesla Semi traveling down Interstate 880 in Hayward caught fire during the evening commute. There was no crash, no multi-vehicle pileup, and no obvious collision. The driver noticed something was wrong, pulled over near Highway 92, and got out unharmed.

Then the lithium-ion pack did what high-energy battery chemistry does when it gets pushed past its thermal limits… By the time crews finished foaming down the wreckage, all that remained of the tractor was its rear axles and a pile of ash.

The California Highway Patrol shut down both directions of the freeway out of concern over toxic chemical smoke. Northbound lanes eventually opened back up, but southbound lanes stayed closed for nearly ten hours while fire crews managed the blaze and let the pack burn itself out. When the sun came up, all that remained of the tractor was its rear axles and a pile of ash.

This is the second time in just over two years that a single Tesla Semi has shut down a major California highway for half a day. Back in August 2024, a Tesla-operated Semi drifted off Interstate 80 near Emigrant Gap, hit a tree, and caught fire. It took 50,000 gallons of water, air drops from Cal Fire, and a 15-hour closure to clear the road.

Whenever this happens, the anti-EV crowd immediately pounces and claims electric trucks are rolling time bombs. That’s a lie, as we’ve repeatedly explained here, but the knee-jerk reaction from tech enthusiasts, which claims this is just normal trucking business, is equally disconnected from reality.

Something deeper is going on here, and it highlights an important cultural divide between Silicon Valley and the century-old world of heavy-duty commercial trucking. Instead of trashing people and pointing fingers, let’s look at a deeper lesson the industry can learn from this.

First, let’s look at the numbers before anyone starts cherry-picking data.

According to the National Fire Protection Association, US fire departments respond to roughly 15,000 large truck fires every single year. That is about 40 diesel rigs catching fire every day across the country. Blown turbochargers spray oil on hot exhaust manifolds, wheel-end bearings seize at 70 mph, and dragging drum brakes ignite recap tires. Diesel trucks burn all the time, so fire is not a phenomenon unique to electric trucks.

The difference is how they burn. When a diesel tractor catches fire, the local volunteer fire department can usually knock it down with an engine tank and some foam in 45 minutes. A heavy tow truck drags the scorched chassis off the road, sweepers clear the debris, and traffic starts moving again before the morning rush hour is ruined.

When an 800-plus kilowatt-hour commercial battery pack enters thermal runaway, the playbook goes out the window. The cells generate their own oxygen as they break down, turning the battery into a self-sustaining furnace. First responders cannot just drown it and walk away. Their only choice is to pump tens of thousands of gallons of water on it for hours or stand back in defensive mode to keep it from spreading while half a county gets gridlocked. At best, all they can do is hopefully cool it down enough to get it off the road before it might catch fire again.

Then there is the sample size problem. There are roughly 4 million Class 8 commercial diesel trucks on American roads. By contrast, the active Tesla Semi fleet is estimated somewhere between 150 and a few hundred early-production and pilot tractors.

Mathematically speaking, you cannot calculate a clean failure rate on an N of two. A single flat tire that catches fire and spreads to a battery pack can swing the statistical safety record of a 100-truck pilot fleet by 50%. But you also cannot ignore it when two trucks in a tiny pilot fleet cause catastrophic, 10-to-15-hour interstate shutdowns.

So, instead of being lazy and relying on just the numbers, we need to look for more context.

This is not uniquely a Tesla problem. It is an electric startup problem.

Remember Nikola? In the summer of 2023, several of their Tre battery-electric trucks caught fire, including one at their Phoenix headquarters and another in customer testing. Nikola initially hinted at foul play, but third-party investigators quickly found the real culprit: a coolant leak inside the battery packs supplied by Romeo Power had caused an internal electrical short. Nikola had to issue a voluntary recall on all 209 trucks they had built, halting sales and dragging every pack back to the shop to fix the design.

Startups approach trucking like consumer electronics. They design from a clean sheet, prioritize maximum aerodynamic efficiency, integrate components into custom monolithic structures, and move fast.

But, when you take the “move fast and break things” approach, the stakes are a lot higher in the trucking world.

Class 8 trucking is a brutal environment that eats clever ideas for breakfast. A big rig does not live the gentle life of a passenger car in sunny California. It flexes under 80,000 pounds of gross weight, takes violent torsional twisting over uneven loading docks, endures constant low-frequency road battering, and rattles across millions of frost heaves.

If there is a slightly loose coolant quick-connect, an undersized wire harness, or a busbar that flexes half a millimeter under torsional load, commercial duty cycles are like Liam Neeson in Taken. If you’re a problem of any kind, the duty cycle will find you, and it will kill you.

This brings us to the traditional truck builders. Why aren’t we seeing Freightliner eCascadias or Volvo VNR Electrics burning down to the rims on I-80? They’re electric, too, right?

It’s not because traditional truckmakers possess magical, fireproof battery chemistry. They buy lithium-ion cells from the same tier-one suppliers as everyone else. In fact, if you dig into public regulatory filings, other manufacturers have run into the exact same terrifying failure modes that bit Nikola.

In 2023, Volvo and Mack filed safety recall reports with the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA) covering hundreds of electric trucks. What was the defect? Their battery supplier had two separate manufacturing errors: coolant lines that were not fully locked into place, creating the risk of conductive fluid leaking across cells, and over-torqued internal fasteners that could snap off and rattle around inside the pack as loose metallic debris. Both defects created direct short-circuit and fire risks.

Why didn’t you read about those trucks burning down on the highway? Because the so-called legacy manufacturers have spent a century building institutional safety systems.

They caught this problem when a small fire actually broke out in a battery pack in the factory. The fire stayed contained within the pack, but Volvo didn’t try to hide it or explain it away. Instead, they did what the old school companies do, even if it slowed them down. Within a week, there was a stop-ship order on the packs and trucks already on the road were called in for a swap.

Traditional truckmakers move at a glacial pace compared to Silicon Valley, and tech fans love to criticize them for it. But, that slow pace comes from decades of scar tissue earned in battle. When an old school builder sees an internal assembly error or gets a strange sensor reading in fleet monitoring, their reflex is not to push an over-the-air firmware patch and hope for the best. They trigger stop-ship orders, use their huge dealer networks to ground the fleet, file Part 573 defect notices with the feds, and tear the battery packs open in a service bay.

Their systems caught the defect in the shop before it had the chance to catch fire on the freeway.

There is also an important philosophical divide in how these trucks are packaged.

Tesla chose the moonshot approach. To hit a 500-mile range target while hauling full freight, they needed maximum energy density and minimal tare weight. That led them to build a massive, integrated, structural battery pack that sits low between the frame rails. It is an engineering marvel in packaging efficiency, but it is also a monolithic block of energy. If one section suffers an internal failure, the entire pack is at risk.

Traditional builders like Volvo and Freightliner (Daimler Truck) took a far more conservative, regional-haul route. Instead of packing nearly a megawatt-hour of energy into a single tightly integrated structural assembly under the cab, they use smaller, lower-capacity modular packs—with Volvo literally hanging separate battery enclosures off the outside of the frame rails where diesel saddle tanks normally sit.

Hanging heavy battery boxes on the side of the chassis is terrible for maximum range and adds unnecessary weight, and may leave them more vulnerable to collisions. But, from a safety and service standpoint, it is brilliant. The packs are physically isolated from each other. If one module suffers a mechanical puncture, a coolant leak, or a thermal event, the damage is far easier to contain, inspect, and unbolt.

Silicon Valley brought the heavy trucking industry kicking and screaming into the electric era. Without Tesla pushing the envelope on megawatt charging and long-range electric haulers, diesel truckmakers would still be dragging their feet with compliance pilot programs, if even that.

Building an electric truck that can pull 82,000 pounds is not just a software problem, and it’s not just a battery chemistry problem. It is a mechanical durability problem that has to survive millions of miles of rough roads, rough drivers, and unpredictable operating conditions.

Experienced truck builders know how to build a chassis that survives that abuse, but they were slow to embrace the battery. Silicon Valley knows how to build the battery, but they are still learning how punishing the road really is.

Until those two worlds finally learn from each other, fire crews are going to keep having very long nights on the interstate. Hopefully more cross-pollination and experience eventually lead to improvements for all sides of the industry.

Jennifer Sensiba is a long time efficient vehicle enthusiast, writer, and photographer. She grew up around a transmission shop, and has been experimenting with vehicle efficiency since she was 16 and drove a Pontiac Fiero.

#5804. PJM’s Emergency Order Should Be a Wake-Up Call for Michigan: Build More Power Before the Next Crisis

By Terence Parker, Michigan resident and Solar Mounts LLC team member

The U.S. Department of Energy’s October 12, 2026, emergency order directing PJM Interconnection to use additional generating resources should be a wake-up call for Michigan and the entire PJM region. Effective October 13–16, the order authorizes specified power plants to operate as needed for reliability and allows backup generators at large facilities to help reduce grid demand during emergencies.

This does not mean blackouts are inevitable. However, it highlights the serious challenges facing our electricity system and the need to add capacity before emergency measures become routine.

As a Michigan-based company, Solar Mounts LLC, a designer and manufacturer of commercial and utility-scale solar mounting systems, recognizes the potential implications for electricity prices, air quality, local businesses, and families across the state.

Rising Costs and the Consequences of Delayed Generation

PJM’s capacity auctions have exposed the difficulty of securing enough electricity for future demand. Prices reached $329.17 per megawatt-day for the 2026–2027 delivery year and $333.44 for 2027–2028, with the latter auction falling short of PJM’s stated reliability requirement.

Source: Vicinity Energy’s Analysis of PJM Capacity Auctions

Capacity costs are only one component of electricity bills, but rising prices signal growing pressure on consumers. We need more power before shortages force increasingly expensive emergency responses.

The October 2026 order permits designated generating units to exceed specified operating limits when necessary for reliability. It also authorizes PJM to direct backup generation at data centers and other large facilities, potentially including diesel generators as a last resort.

Running fossil-fuel plants more frequently can increase emissions, depending on the units involved. Operating diesel generators will worsen local noise pollution and expose operators to high diesel prices. If these measures become more common, we should ask why cleaner generation and storage are not coming online faster.

Weather Uncertainty Makes Planning Urgent

NOAA’s October 15, 2026, winter outlook anticipates warmer-than-average conditions across much of the northern United States, including the Midwest, influenced by a strong El Niño.

Source: NOAA’s 2026–2027 U.S. Winter Outlook

Seasonal forecasts cannot predict every period of extreme demand. Aging infrastructure, maintenance, changing weather, and growing electricity consumption reinforce the need for advance planning. Temporary authority to run additional fossil-fuel resources cannot replace long-term investment in generation.

Solar, Wind, and Batteries Can Help Close the Gap

Utility-scale solar, wind, and battery storage can help meet demand on timelines that many conventional generation projects cannot match. Suitable solar projects may be built within 18 months, depending on permitting, equipment, and interconnection. New nuclear facilities generally require substantially longer development periods.

Solar and wind will reduce emissions when they displace electricity from fossil-fuel generators. Batteries store surplus electricity for periods of higher demand. Note: actual emissions benefits depend on which generators are displaced and when electricity is delivered.

Faster interconnection reviews are also essential. Qualified solar projects shouldn’t remain in queues for years because of avoidable administrative delays. Better coordination among PJM, utilities, regulators, developers, and state governments, alongside necessary transmission upgrades, will bring capacity online sooner.

Solar Development and Agriculture Can Coexist

At Solar Mounts LLC, we witness concerns about installing solar on productive farmland here in rural Calhoun County (home to Solar Mounts LLC). However, well-designed projects can include restoration plans that allow agricultural use to resume when leases end. Solar leases can also provide farmers with supplemental income when crop prices fail to keep pace with production costs.

Agrivoltaic systems combine electricity generation with compatible crops or livestock. Thoughtful design and community engagement can balance renewable energy development with agricultural productivity, rural traditions, and farmland preservation.

Opportunities extend beyond prime farmland. Parking lots, commercial properties, brownfields, landfills, and other underutilized sites can accommodate solar generation. Solar carports provide electricity, shade, and weather protection, while community solar expands access for people unable or unwilling to install their own panels.

Solar Mounts LLC supports practical, site-appropriate solutions that expand generation while respecting farmers, landowners, and rural communities.

Hyperscale Data Centers Must Help Provide Their Own Power

Growing data center demand, particularly in Virginia and other parts of PJM’s territory, is adding pressure to an already constrained grid. Consequently, developers of new hyperscale facilities should demonstrate how they will secure adequate electricity and pay their fair share of supporting infrastructure. Dedicated generation, renewable energy contracts, batteries, flexible demand, and grid upgrades can all contribute.

Requiring every facility to build a gas-fired peaker plant is not necessarily the best answer. Gas turbines face equipment and supply-chain constraints, while additional fossil-fuel generation carries emissions and operating costs. A combination of solutions tailored to each location is more practical.

Existing facilities should also reduce peak demand where possible. Energy efficiency remains one of the most immediate ways businesses can limit electricity consumption.

Solar Mounts LLC sees the opportunity to expand commercial and utility-scale solar infrastructure across Michigan and the country. We encourage this expansion by supporting faster approvals, better grid planning, and a serious commitment to adding power generation and energy storage before shortages worsen.

Reliable electricity underpins our economy, jobs, and daily lives. Repeated emergency orders, rising capacity costs, and greater reliance on high-emission generation are not sound long-term strategies.

We shouldn’t wait for the next crisis to build the power system we already know we need.

#5802. Antwerp’s Electric Cranes Can Handle More Than Today’s Cargo

Katoen Natie has put two all-electric mobile harbour cranes into service at the Port of Antwerp, with four more joining them across three deep-sea multipurpose terminals. The completed fleet will include four 200-tonne cranes and two 150-tonne machines, with paired lifting capacity reaching 400 tonnes. Katoen Natie expects the six cranes to displace as much as 500,000 litres of diesel annually once they’re operating.

The October commissioning announcement is worth attention because these are heavy, mobile machines working in a commercial port. They aren’t small demonstrations, and the engineering addresses the conditions that historically made mobile harbour cranes difficult to electrify.

Fixed container cranes were straightforward candidates for electrification. They stay in one location, have repetitive operating patterns and can be connected to substantial electrical supplies. Mobile harbour cranes travel along the quay, move between assignments and handle cargo that can vary considerably from one vessel to the next. An onboard diesel generator provided that mobility without requiring the terminal to build electrical connections everywhere the crane might work.

Konecranes’ solution separates the energy needed for lifting from the energy needed for travel. External grid electricity supplies the heavy working loads, while rechargeable batteries allow the cranes to move between operating positions. This avoids carrying an enormous battery for work that can be powered directly from the grid. It also eliminates diesel exhaust at the equipment and reduces local noise.

The bigger advantage is that the investment isn’t tied to one type of cargo. A mobile harbour crane can handle steel, industrial equipment, large transformers, wind components, scrap, project cargo, conventional bulk and containers. Port operators can change their handling arrangements as customers and cargo flows change without replacing the basic lifting machinery.

That matters as the energy transition changes maritime trade. In an earlier CleanTechnica analysis, I examined how ports face rising capital requirements even as fossil-fuel cargoes begin their long-term decline. Coal, oil and gas have supported major investments in berths, terminals, channels and inland connections. Their declining role creates commercial risks for infrastructure designed around individual commodities.

Ports are looking for new business in containers, renewable-energy equipment, industrial products and alternative fuels. Some will succeed, but they cannot all capture the same growth in container traffic, and low-carbon molecules will not replace fossil-fuel cargo tonne for tonne. Equipment investments have to make sense under more than one plausible freight forecast.

Electric mobile cranes are well suited to that uncertainty. A machine capable of lifting a transformer today can handle scrap steel or construction machinery tomorrow. Its electrical supply can also be part of a wider investment supporting yard equipment, trucks, shore power and harbour craft.

That doesn’t make every new crane or grid connection worthwhile. A terminal still needs sufficient durable activity, suitable electrical infrastructure and a sound operating case. But investing in flexible electric equipment is less dependent on predicting which replacement cargo wins than building another dedicated fossil-fuel or specialized alternative-fuel facility.

Katoen Natie’s investment demonstrates how ports can reduce diesel consumption today while retaining more options for the freight they handle tomorrow.

My TFIE Strategy Briefing article connects Antwerp’s electric mobile harbour cranes with the larger challenge of investing in ports as fossil bulk declines, new industrial cargoes emerge and electrical infrastructure serves more of the waterfront.

#5798. Elon Musk’s Attack Ads Are Backfiring As Midterms Near

US Federal Election Commission data revealed this week that Elon Musk has donated at least $445 million into Republican-aligned political action committees and groups since last year. Interestingly, it seems that Musk’s deep influence isn’t enough, and he is worried.

If Democrats win big on Tuesday in midterm elections, Musk insists US democracy will come to an “end.”

“This would result in America becoming a permanent one-party state and the end of democracy in any meaningful sense of the word,” Musk wrote Friday on X. “All that is needed to ensure this doesn’t happen is 51 Republican senators in 2029.”

The Tesla and SpaceX CEO has a long track record of engaging in political quid pro quo. His companies receive billions in federal contracts. The once climate-is-all advocate now weighs heavily on the side of oil/fossil fuels. His DOGE actions may have forced labor shortages in regulatory agencies that were scrutinizing his businesses.

But now Musk seems distraught that newfound Democratic Congressional power could make him a target of investigations after the midterms.

And his fears may have merit. Musk’s blatant attempts to influence election results are getting a lot of recent analysis and condemnation.

“I worry about what Musk’s impulsive bullying is doing and has done to others more vulnerable than I am,” former Secretary of Labor Robert Reich wrote earlier this month after Musk exclaimed, “Prison for Reich.” Such extreme comments are part of Musk lifelong pattern, Reich continued, in which “he’s threatened and racially denigrated and pushed around (people) his whole life.”

Reich added that he is worried for that voters whom Musk has been “deluging with TV ads for Republican candidates” as well as for “federal workers who have been hurt by his so-called Department of Government Efficiency, Americans who have been harmed by his wanton destruction of public services, and people around the globe who are worse off because of cuts to USAID pushed by Elon’s DOGE.”

Musk’s America PAC has contributed to 43 US House and Senate races, and that influx of cash has funded vast amounts of television commercials, digital ads, text messages, and mailers. It’s almost easy to forget that Musk became a persona non grata with Trump and the Republican Party last year.

America PAC stands for “secure borders, safe cities, free speech, sensible spending, fair justice system, and self-protection,” according to its website.

These objectives might sound fair and reasonable to many voters, yet a majority of America PAC money goes to attack ads. Musk typically cross-posts America PAC’s damaging content to his more than 200 million followers on X, the social media site he owns and whose social commentary he controls.

Ezra Klein, co-author of Abundance, recently interviewed Quinn Slobodian, professor of international history at Boston University. Slobodian interprets Musk’s midterm campaign finance moves as “his flexing of his own power to make or break candidates, as he wishes,” which, in turn, is a way “fine-tune that control over the media and messaging ecosystem and get out ahead of what he worries would be a regulatory wave that would accompany a Democratic majority.”

Why are such media messages — even those that are based on false premises — so compelling during the run-up to the 2026 US midterm elections? Voters frequently base their own political decisions on fake information and images rather than on reality. Social historian Heather Cox Richardson explains that many such techniques draw scenarios of a chaotic society and destroy individuals’ “ability to make sense of the world.” Some media consumers “turn to a strongman who promises to create order.” Others will get so exhausted they simply refuse to fight back. The path of least resistance is succumbing to the manipulation and “giving up control of their government to an authoritarian.”

In Texas alone, America PAC has provided more than $33 million toward the Ken Paxton Senate campaign. Additionally, Musk donated $168 million contribution to the Senate Leadership Fund, which routes money back to the Paxton campaign.

“Elon Musk wants our land. He wants our water. He wants our tax dollars,” James Talarico, the Texas Democratic nominee for Senate, said at a recent rally. “He wants his very own personal United States senator.” Talarico’s comments were supported by loud boos from the crowd.

At a rally at the University of Texas, Talarico said Musk was trying to “stop the movement that we are building in our state.” In contrast, he offered that his own campaign is “funded by 1.3 million individuals who have donated their hard-earned money.” Instead of corporate PAC money, Talarico has depended on small-dollar donations.

The day before that, Talarico’s campaign released a digital advertisement accusing Paxton of being a mere pawn to Musk. “Let’s remind Elon: Texas is not for sale.”

Interestingly, Democratic candidates in Texas and elsewhere across the US are holding their own — many electoral races are too close to call, even with Musk’s influence and campaign finance imbalances helping out Republicans.
