#5794. Record Demand Catches (Some) OEMs with Their Pants Down — Europe September 2026

BEVs remain in high demand in Europe, with pure electrics reaching a record 31% share last month thanks to a record 422,000 fully electric passenger cars being registered in Europe, which is not only a 63%(!) increase year over year (YoY), but is also the fastest growth rate in over three years!

Overall, plugin vehicles were up 49% YoY, dragged down by plugin hybrids, which grew only 23%, to 167,000 units, which was also a new record high for plugin hybrids in Europe, thus placing September’s total plugin volume at a best ever 590,000 units and its share at 43% (31% for BEVs only).

With BEVs pulling the market upwards, the year-to-date share for BEVs is now at 25% (36% for PHEVs and BEVs combined), which is already higher than the 2025 final result (20% BEV share, 29% adding PHEVs). This is an encouraging sign if we want to be close to 100% PEV share by the mid-2030s.

Looking at other powertrains, while petrol (18% share in September, down 21% YoY) and diesel (4%, down 28% YoY) continue their downward trend into irrelevance, the main news is the fact that we could have already passed the point of peak HEV — in September, plugless hybrids still grew, albeit less (+5% YoY) than the overall market (+10%, to 1.4 million units).

More important than that, if we compare the 34% share earned by HEVs in September 2026 with the share they had 12 months ago (36% then), we might be witnessing the beginning of plugless hybrids’ downward slope. So … if 2026 is the year of peak HEVs, how far are we from peak PHEVs?

Looking at the best sellers in several size categories, the EV push is starting to get visible. While we are far from the EV domination we see in China, things are indeed evolving in Europe.

Before, the only EVs that we saw on the overall podiums were Teslas. Now, besides those two usual suspects, the 3rd placed model in the midsize category, the Mercedes GLC, is also heavily electrified, with 59% of all its sales coming from either its new BEV model (representing 44% of its total sales) or the PHEV versions (15% of sales), leaving the ICE versions representing just 41% of total sales.

The A-segment has the Renault Twingo as the 2nd placed model. Like in China before, expect this category to be quickly electrified. Firstly, the ICE competition is shrinking. Secondly, not only do we have the Renault Twingo already on the podium, but the new Dacia Spring is set to become a success, so expect it to join the Renault sibling on the podium. As a third musketeer, the VW ID.Up is set to land sometime next year, which is another strong bet from the German brand — and nothing shorter than a podium position would do.

And even if all these competitors cannot remove the eternal Fiat Panda from the podium … then the 2028 Fiat Koala and Citroen 2CV surely will. And let’s not forget the Chinese competition, namely the future second-generation Leapmotor city car, said to be developed together with the Koala and 2CV. Not counting others, by 2028, we will have half a dozen serious candidates for podium positions in the city car category.

Looking at the other segments, the B-segment is still ICE-heavy, with none of the top 3 models having a plugin version, the C-segment is getting increasingly electrified (#1 VW Golf is 31% PHEV, #2 VW Tiguan is 32% PHEV, and #3 Jaecoo 7 is 47% PHEV).

In the full size category, electrification is also significant, with the #1 BMW 5 Series (including the BMW i5) having 74% of sales coming from either the BEV or the PHEV version; the #2 BMW X5 being 28% electrified (either with the BEV i5 or the PHEV version); and the #3 Range Rover Sport having 33% of its sales coming from the PHEV versions.

So, electrification in the D-segment is quite advanced, while the A, C, and E segments are showing good potential, with real progress being made. But the all-important B Segment is showing that it is in the laggard category.

(A note on the full size category and its importance on the European market: the best selling E-segment model was just #109 overall … which says a lot about the (in)significance of it in the larger scheme of things. True, margins are attractive, but it’s a faaar smaller market than its counterpart categories in the USA or China. The full size category represents 18% of the market in both China and the USA, while in Europe, it represents only 6%.)

Looking at September’s best selling EV models, the big news this month was Tesla winning 1st and 2nd place, and by a large margin — so much so that the #1 plus #2 win extended into the overall market! Yes, you have read that right — for only the second time ever, the other time being in December 2022, Tesla placed the Model Y in 1st and the Model 3 in 2nd on the overall market! Notice, also, BYD placing two models in the top 5. Here’s a more detailed analysis of the top 5 EVs in September:

#1 Tesla Model Y — Tesla’s midsize crossover is back in the driver’s seat, thanks to an amazing 41,987 registrations in September, a 52% increase YoY, and its best result in four and a half years! The Berlin-made crossover was the overall #1 in Germany, France, Iceland, Ireland, Sweden, the Netherlands, Norway, Switzerland, Denmark, Finland, and … maybe a few more I might be forgetting. This was its second best performance ever in Europe, only behind the 46,512-unit score of March 2023. With Model Y prices starting at €40,000, and even after six years on the market, the crossover offers an appealing package and, together with its Model 3 sibling and a few Chinese models, is reviving a lost category on the European automotive market — the midsize mainstream market. Remember a time when the Ford Mondeo, Opel Vectra, and others sold at least as much as the models of the premium Germans? Well, it seems that with their prices now starting above 50,000 euros, that has created a hole in the market, where between 40,000 to 50,000 euros you can get a lot of great midsize models (both Teslas, the Xpeng G6, the Mazda 6e/CX-6e, the Deepal S07, the BYD Seal/Seal U, the Leapmotor C10, the Zeekr 7 GT, etc.), for a fair bit less money than the German brands. Currently, you buy one of these with your wallet/head, not your heart. And that’s exactly where the market is headed. People don’t care as much about badge, they care about value. That is exactly the kind of buyer that is now searching for EVs. These people don’t want a badge, they simply want to lower their costs by switching from ICEVs to BEVs.

#2 Tesla Model 3 — Tesla’s sedan was 2nd in September thanks to 26,770 registrations, an awesome surge of 120% YoY that allowed it to be 2nd in the overall European market. Benefiting from the same tailwinds as the Model Y (mass surge in EV demand, competitive pricing, and rapid production ramp-up), the 10-year-old sedan is racing to meet soaring demand, as proven by the current 3- to 4-month waiting list. Its demand surged because price-conscious buyers are in the market and its prices, starting around €35,000, place it, a midsize sedan, in competition with models one segment below, compact hatchbacks. As of this moment, it seems Tesla’s sedan will recover its podium position in 2026, and maybe even provide a gold plus silver win for Tesla at the end of the year … which is no mean feat for a 10-year-old model wearing a body type (sedan) not popular in Europe.

#3 BYD Seal U (BEV+PHEV) — The Chinese SUV remained in the top 5 and hit a record 12,779 registrations last month, meaning that while the BYD’s star is recovering in China, thanks to its new-generation Ultra body, the old generation is still able to win medals in Europe all by itself (a bit like when veteran football/soccer players leave top leagues and semi-retire in less competitive ones…). September’s 3rd place finish was much thanks to generous discounts, but still, with the model’s development costs now well behind it, BYD can afford to go into hard discount territory with this one.

#4 Skoda Elroq — The Elroq continues surfing the BEV wave, with the 12,592 registrations representing a record result. Looking ahead, the Czech crossover will have to face fierce internal competition, as the new, smaller, and more affordable Skoda Epiq crossover is about to land. Being simultaneously cheaper and more modern than the Elroq, will the Epiq become Skoda’s new best seller at the cost of the Elroq?

#5 BYD Atto 2 (BEV+PHEV) — The Chinese make has another hit on its hands, with the small crossover scoring 11,111 registrations, mostly thanks to the new PHEV version. Why so popular in Europe? Besides not having real competition in the small PHEV category, the top range version has a 18 kWh battery, providing a usable range (90 km/56 mi). Additionally, it has increasingly important vehicle-to-load (V2L) capability. With the recent Dolphin G PHEV small hatchback using the same powertrain, expect the Shenzhen make to have another representative on the European table soon, especially considering that the new hatchback will be made in Hungary.

Outside the top 5, there were a number of record scores to mention, as would be expected in a record month.

Starting with Mercedes, we have both its representatives, the CLA EV (15th place with 7,206 registrations) and the new Mercedes GLC EV (16th place, 7,099 units) scoring record results.

Regardless of what might happen to the GLC, I am now curious to see whether the Mercedes GLC will be able to surpass to the BMW iX3’s volumes. I mean, the three-pointed-star model’s specs are a degree below the Bimmer’s, but on the other hand, it looks like Mercedes is ramping up production faster than BMW, as one can see from the waiting lists of both models (5 to 6 months for the Mercedes, around 9 months for the BMW). An example of that is that Mercedes is working its Bremen factory 24/6 to make the GLC, while BMW only now added a second shift to its Debrecen plant.

Good news also in the Kia stable: Its star player, the EV3, scored a record high, 7,385 registrations, ending the month in 11th. Even better than that, the new EV2 small crossover (it’s basically a hatchback on stilts, but anyway … marketing oblige) continues to ramp up, now reaching a record 6,458 registrations. With production still in ramp-up mode and a sizeable waiting list, the EV2 looks destined to become the automaker’s best seller in Europe.

Renault’s new baby also shined, with the Twingo rising to 10th thanks to a record 7,776 registrations. How high will the cutesy French EV go? Will 10,000 units in a month be possible?

Despite Volvo’s headaches due to battery issues that are hurting the production ramp-up of the Volvo EX60, its smaller sibling, the EX30, has given the brand something to smile about — the small crossover had its best result in two years thanks to 7,245 registrations.

Finally, a reference has to be given to the Chinese models. Besides the record performance of the #3 BYD Seal U, three other made-in-China models hit top 20 positions and record scores — the Jaecoo 7 PHEV was 13th, with 7,262 units; the Geely Starray plugin hybrid crossover was 17th, with 6,888 units; and the Leapmotor B10 was 19th, with 6,262 units. All three models are compact crossovers. And together with the BYD models, they add up to five Chinese representatives in the top 20. Something to think about….

Outside the top 20, there were records galore, so I won’t mention all of them. Instead, I will mention this:

Looking at the 2026 ranking, the big surprise was the four-position jump of the Tesla Model 3, into 2nd place.

It was to be expected that the Tesla sedan could climb onto the podium, thanks to September being a peak month for Tesla, but that it could surpass a 13,000-unit disadvantage over the (then) 2nd placed Skoda Elroq … now that was a surprise. And all of this is despite the Skoda Elroq posting a record month.

A podium position is now guaranteed for the veteran sedan, and we can even say that it is now the favorite for the 2026 runner-up spot, something few could have foreseen at the start of this year.

Just below the podium, another question is arising — will the Renault 5 be able to resist the advances of the BYD Seal U and Atto 2? As for the latter, the current distance allows Renault some peace of mind. When it comes to the former, … it will be difficult for the Renault 5 to stay ahead of the up and coming model, especially considering that the R5 has to share its factory line with several other models, like the R4, Nissan Micra, Renault Scenic, and Renault Megane, preventing it from ramping its production up further.

As for the remaining changes, the Skoda Enyaq was up one spot, to 8th, surpassing the soon-to-be-retiring VW ID.4. Will the new VW ID.Tiguan improve on the ID.4’s performance? The high point of the outgoing crossover was two 3rd place finishes, in 2022 and 2023. That’s a tall order for the new contender….

In the second half of the table, the Kia EV3 profited from its record score to jump two positions, into 14th, while the Audi Q6 e-tron and Volvo EX30 profited from their Volvo XC60 PHEV and Audi Q4 e-tron siblings’ horrible September performances to surpass them and reach the 17th and 18th positions, respectively.

Finally, a reference must be given to the deliveries of the #11 Mercedes CLA EV. It might not sound like much of a highlight, but the point here is that, historically, the CLA was a niche model for Mercedes, sandwiched between the poor man’s Mercedes A-Class and the more properly statutary Mercedes, the C-Class. As a consequence, the CLA’s overall sales in Europe were no more than 65,000 to 70,000 units per year, in a good year. With the BEV version of the CLA now above 52,000 units, and still three months to go, it won’t be hard for the CLA to beat the upper limit of the sales target this year.

Sure, neither the A-Class nor the C-Class (yet) have BEV versions to cast a shadow over the CLA, but it is a significant performance nevertheless, and with the CLA having a BEV station wagon version, that neither the A or C-classes will have, it sure looks like the CLA will continue to sell in significant numbers. Adding to its fortunes is the fact that it’s one of the most energy efficient EVs around, which allows it to post above-average range despite having average-sized batteries. Go, Mercedes CLA!

Having a quick look at the overall brand ranking, the highlights were the contrasting fortunes of Tesla (7th, up 71% YoY) and Ford (16th, down 18% YoY), as well as the usual rise and rise of the Chinese brands.

Two comments regarding the US automakers:

BYD, also known as the Chinese Toyota, ended the month in 11th, with 49,340 units, up 102% YoY. That’s ahead of mainstream staples like Dacia (12th), but, above all, MG (13th). Yep, despite having a few years of advance and also selling ICE models, SAIC’s MG is now behind BYD in Europe.

But the strength of the Chinese automotive industry is the fact that it doesn’t rely on one major OEM, but rather the diversity it offers to European buyers — China has 15 brands in Europe’s top 50! Of them, the highlights are surging Jaecoo (#22, +157%), Omoda (#27, +111%), Leapmotor (#28, +324%), and Chery (#29, 688%).

(One could say that the overall European market is growing — +10% YoY — thanks to the rise of Chinese OEMs….)

As for the plugin auto brand ranking, the leader, Volkswagen, remained in the lead, but again lost share (8.7%, down from 8.9% in August and down from 9.3% in May). Sure, it still holds a sizeable advantage over rising BYD (8.2%, up 0.2%), and its new EVs (VW ID.Polo, ID.Cross, ID.Tiguan, etc.) should help it recover some sales, but … the German brand cannot allow itself to slow down. If it does, the company will get BYD on its back real fast … in its home market. And if you can’t win at home.…

#3 BMW (6.9%, down 0.3%) is stable in the last place on the podium, but it ended the month just 100 units ahead of Tesla (6.9% share, up from 6% in August). Tesla did benefit from a higher than usual end of quarter high tide, though. This looks to be an entertaining race in the last months of the year, as BMW currently lacks models that can add significant volume that might counterweight the surging demand that Tesla is now experiencing. I mean, the iX3 alone is not enough, the new i3 sports sedan will need to ramp up fast and deliver significant volume in Q4 if BMW wants to have any chance of keeping the bronze medal in 2026.

In 5th we have Mercedes (5.8%). It is holding steady in the last position of the top 5 and even gained a little bit more distance over #6 Audi (5.3%) and #7 Skoda (5.2%).

Arranging things by automotive group, everybody lost share except BYD.

Leader Volkswagen Group lost a significant amount of share, going from 23.9% share in August to its current 23.3%. Sure, the title is more than secured, but, for 2027, the fresh metal coming soon needs to correspond with expectations if the German OEM wants to stay comfortable in the lead.

#2 BMW Group (8.4%, down 0.3%) lost a worrying amount of share, especially considering that the new 3rd placed model is a rising BYD (8.2%, up 0.2%), just 0.2% behind it. At this point, it seems all but inevitable that the Chinese OEM will end the year in the runner-up spot, as its lineup is so vast and the volume deliveries increasing at such speed that it seems BYD’s true target is not BMW’s #2 spot but … VW’s throne.

As for now-4th-placed Stellantis … the never ending slide continues (7.9%, down 0.2%). The multinational OEM’s drops seem to have no end in sight. Worst of all, in a record month when every top OEM had several models scoring record results, Stellantis had … none. The multinational conglomerate lacks a star player. It has plenty of models selling okay — some 2,000–3,000 units per month — but the company lacks a frontrunner. Will that be the next Peugeot 208? One thing is for certain: that new generation has a lot of weight on its shoulders. If it fails….

Outside the top 5, #6 Geely (6.9%, up from 6.8% in August) gained ground on #5 Hyundai–Kia (7.5%). And with 7th placed Tesla (5.9%) now showing up on the radar as well, there could be an interesting race for 6th place.

2026 has been a wild ride in the EV world.

At the beginning of the year, legacy OEMs were happy with the growth of EV share in Europe — 20% to 30% BEV growth rates were good enough to keep investments in EVs steady, investments that would be funded by their still significant ICE business.

2025 ended with only one Chinese model in the European top 20, and Tesla ended the year in 4th, behind VW, BMW, and Mercedes. For the status quo to be perfect, you only need to convince those pesky eurocrats to soften CO2 targets….

Only, on the 28th of February 2026, with US and Israeli attacks on Iran, oil prices surged and the world has changed, and with it, so has the EV world.

In many markets, fuel prices escalated and people saw their wallets grow a hole. So many people who could buy a new car went and bought a BEV, preferably one that was readily available and cheap. It was not an ego-related buy, but a necessity-related one. And that changes the game.

Added to the Chinese auto market slump that was already happening towards the end of 2025, it was perfect timing for local OEMs to launch into export markets — sending production there that was originally meant for demand at home which had gone missing.

Now focusing on Europe, the increased EV demand quickly bought whatever existing stock was available, and waiting lists quickly started to grow.

Only, many people didn’t wanted to wait several months for their new BEV, they wanted to start saving money immediately, so they turned to whoever allowed them to receive a new electric vehicle the next day.

That was the perfect timing for Chinese OEMs and Tesla, which had been seeing its sales slowly erode in the previous couple of years. Because Tesla had competitively priced BEVs and an easily scalable production structure, it managed to quickly profit from the demand surge.

So, a win for Tesla and the Chinese EV makers.

Here’s what most numbers available do not show — how their sales are now compared to a year ago. Are they surfing the EV demand wave? Here’s a sample of European results in September:

Surfing the wave — Kia (+110% YoY), Toyota (+94%), Renault (+73%).

Trying to keep up — Stellantis (+53% YoY).

With their pants down — Ford (+3% YoY), Volvo (+4%), Hyundai (0%), Volkswagen Group (+19% YoY), BMW (+12%), Mercedes (+15%).

Always interested in the auto industry, particularly in electric cars, Jose has pioneered on documenting the plug-in sales evolution through the EV Sales blog, allowing him to gain an expert view on where EVs are right now and where they are headed in the future. Extending that work and expertise, Jose was co-founder of EV-Volumes and currently works with the European Alternative Fuels Observatory on EV sales matters.

#5792. AI Balloon in China Deflates by Half a Trillion Dollars

There’s been a lot of talk this year about a humongous AI bubble. We’ve written about that many times here on CleanTechnica, but focused on the US market. AI software companies have massive valuations/market caps based on hype and growth in AI use but without demonstrating anything close to profitability or a clear plan for profitability. AI hardware company growth is dependent on continued software growth, of course. Well, if it’s a bubble or a balloon, it’s still fully blown up in the US, but there’s reporting now that the AI balloon has been deflating (a lot) in China.

“A report from Reuters suggests that the AI bubble in China ‘has already burst.’ Or it might be more apt to say that it shriveled like a rapidly deflating balloon,” Futurism writes. “Over the past four months, Reuters notes, the country’s STAR50 index of AI and semiconductor companies collapsed by 40 percent, wiping out $480 billion in market value. That is not a typo.”

It seems the same thing has been happening in China with AI hype as we’ve seen in the US, and there have thus also been people over there warning of it being a big bubble. “The stock market later evolved into a speculative crazy bull,” Dong Baozhen, a fund manager in Beijing, told Reuters. “And now we see the bubble bursting.”

Of course, the robotics craze and the AI craze have been going hand in hand for the past year or two. This reporting seems to link the two.

“Shares of Unitree, China’s buzzy robotics company, have halved since the company went public in a much-hyped IPO in August,” Futurism adds. “The chipmaker CXMT, meanwhile, has lost more than a quarter of its value over the past month.”

Is this just the beginning of a much more massively deflation or popping of AI bubbles? We will see.

#5790. Mitsubishi Electric seeks to expand North American grid foothold

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

In August, the Japanese conglomerate said it would spend $1.4 billion to buy PCI Energy Solutions, whose platform manages about 60% of U.S. power generation.

On Friday, Mitsubishi Electric reported a 20% year-over-year revenue increase in its utility-facing business segment as it prepares to launch an electrical components joint venture with fellow industrial firm ABB and acquire an energy management software provider to boost its exposure to North American wholesale power markets.

On Aug. 20, the Japanese conglomerate said it would spend $1.4 billion to buy PCI Energy Solutions. PCI supports utilities, independent power producers and other wholesale market participants in bulk power trading and settlement, generation and transmission operations, supply-demand planning and risk management, Mitsubishi Electric said.

About 60% of U.S. power generation is managed by PCI’s platform, according to Mitsubishi Electric.

The transaction, which is expected to close by the end of 2026, will help the combined company manage a North American bulk power system transformed by rising renewable energy generation, expanded use of distributed energy resources, greater power market volatility and a broad increase in electricity demand, Mitsubishi Electric said.

“These developments are increasing demand for advanced energy management and optimization solutions capable of supporting integrated forecasting, scheduling, trading, risk management, settlement and operational decision-making,” the company said.

Mitsubishi Electric’s Infrastructure segment houses most of the firm’s work for utilities and heavy power consumers. It took in 379.7 billion yen in the second quarter of the 2027 fiscal year, up from ¥316.4 billion in the second quarter of fiscal 2026, according to financial figures released Friday.

The company’s fiscal year runs from April 1 to March 31.

Both of the Infrastructure units serving utilities and large power consumers increased their revenue by double-digit percentages, with Public Utility Systems rising by 13% and Energy Systems by 18%. Together, they comprise about 70% of Infrastructure revenues, with the non-utility Defense and Space Systems unit accounting for the remainder.

Public Utility Systems manufactures and markets water treatment controls, communications systems and power supply equipment for large utility users. Energy Systems makes and sells power plant controls, substation equipment, electric transmission and distribution components, power electronics and power supply equipment.

The Public Utility Systems unit drew strength from “large-scale projects in the uninterruptible power supply business outside Japan,” while Energy Systems “saw robust demand …. from the expanded use of renewable energy and increased investments in data centers,” Mitsubishi Electric said.

Transmission and distribution equipment orders were especially robust, the company added.

Mitsubishi Electric’s just-announced partnership with ABB would create a jointly owned subsidiary to manufacture and distribute circuit breakers, electromagnetic switches, controls and uninterrupted power supply systems rated for 5 kilovolt-amperes or less, and to sell “certain high-voltage power distribution control equipment” and smart meters, the Japanese conglomerate said on Oct. 20.

“ABB holds a leading position in the power distribution and control field, and develops power distribution and control solutions by leveraging its technologies and global sales network developed over many years,” Mitsubishi Electric said, adding that rising electricity demand outside Japan necessitates a partner with deeper exposure to international markets.

Mitsubishi Electric expects the ABB transaction to close by October 2027.

In the first half of its 2027 fiscal year, Mitsubishi Electric earned 45% of its revenue in its home market of Japan, according to its second-quarter earnings presentation. Most of the remainder came from China, other parts of Asia, Europe and North America, with roughly equal shares for each, according to a chart provided by the company.

North America, home of Mitsubishi Electric’s Pennsylvania-based electric power products subsidiary, is the conglomerate’s slowest-growing major overseas market so far this fiscal year, according to company figures. North American revenue is pacing about 5% ahead of the same period in fiscal 2026, compared with about 19% for Europe and 34% for China. Japanese revenue is up about 10% so far in fiscal 2027.

#5788. Donated solar project powers community center in unincorporated New Mexico village  Solar Power World’s Projects of Impact

A building that started as a one-room schoolhouse has through the decades expanded to become a lifeline for a New Mexico community far from any city infrastructure, and a recent solar installation has brought new resilience to this place.

Children run outside of Ojo Sarco, New Mexico’s one-room schoolhouse in a photo from January 1943. The structure still stands today as the Ojo Sarco Community Center. Credit: John Collier, Jr./Library of Congress

The structure housing the Ojo Sarco Community Center (OSCC) was founded in the early 20th century. Ojo Sarco is an unincorporated community home to about 300 people that is located about 43 miles northeast from Santa Fe.

“We have no political voice, really,” said Carol Miller, a grant writer and board member for the Ojo Sarco Community Center. “We’re pretty neglected. We’ve lost resources in the past. We were the first wave of people to lose our post office many years ago. Everybody has to commute pretty much for everything.”

That commute is about 40 minutes round trip for basic amenities, she said.

The community center has tried to fill in those resource gaps with services like a twice-monthly food bank, summer meal programs for kids, safety classes and outings for its residents, such as a night at the opera. The OSCC also recently met all the qualifications to be a state library.

PPC Solar, also known as Paradise Power Company, from Taos, built a 7.5-kW solar project paired with 10.6-kWh of LFP energy storage at the OSCC. The contractor built the array at no-cost to the community center. Every year PPC donates a solar array to a regional nonprofit organization, and after three years of applying, the OSCC was awarded the project.

CEO Daniel Weinman’s father founded Paradise Power Company in the 1970s, and the company has installed solar in off-the-grid communities since the start. Its PV donation program is built out of that tradition.

“We’ve always wanted to find a way to give back to the community,” Weinman said. “The best thing that we found — and we’ve been doing it for over a decade now — is doing an annual nonprofit donation system … We’ve had farming organizations, land conservation organizations, and it goes on and on. Really, it’s about finding the right fit. We wanted to make a good impact for this community center, and we felt resiliency was the best way to do that.”

Solar Power World spoke to both PPC Solar and organizers with the OSCC about what effect this solar project has had in the village since it was built in fall 2025.

Larry Miller, project manager and OSCC board member: I think it’s a transformative project for our small community. We like to talk about the history of the community center, and now this is part of it. If someone doesn’t notice the solar panels on the roof, we point it out to them. It’s integrated into our lives here.

Carol Miller: It’s lowered our electric costs, which is really important for us, because we’re such a shoe-string operation. We’ve had some fun talks with the community because they all know we’ll have the charging capacity. We still have internet available, even with the power out. All the kids that come to our library programs, they’re solar oriented. The benefit of this company and its staff just has ripple effects throughout our greater community.

PPC Solar donated a solar plus storage project to the Ojo Sarco Community Center. This array will keep power running in this facility that has become a haven for Ojo Sarco residents. Credit: PPC Solar

Preston Bastardo, sales and design, PPC Solar: The building itself had some challenges with mounting the array on the roof, but that just took some exploration in the attic and making sure that we could land things where we needed to. The building has two large areas, and so fo installation of batteries had some restrictions, there was a good mechanical closet that we were able to use for batteries. Then as far as backup circuits, we did a partial facility backup where we had a critical loads panel next to their main panel. I think everything went pretty smoothly.

Weinman: Well, it really goes back to our roots. In 1972, there was no technology supporting solar at the time. My dad was a pioneer in the industry and he really developed his own controls and safety protections and found used panels and used batteries and was bringing power to places where there was no power. Our house, for example, we started with kerosene lanterns. We didn’t have electricity until we had a solar power system, and it was really life changing.

Being able to build on that legacy and look at our community in that light and be able to offer systems like this to communities that need it — I think that really adds to that legacy and really is profound in a lot of ways. It feels great to be able to do this and be in a position where we can help others realize and experience the benefits of solar power. We want to continue to participate in any way we can.

Carol Miller: We’re a very old community and proud of it. And having worked with local businesses and our electrical co-op, we know it’s really the exception and not the rule because we own our power. Any technology that is shared equally in a low-income community has ripple effects beyond what I think some of us imagined a number of years ago. It’s for everyone.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5786. PJM’s revised data center backstop procurement plan reduces utility risks: ICF

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The planned procurement could be for as little as 630 MW, down from an initial 6,800-MW target, after utilities in PJM’s footprint lowered their large-load forecasts, Jefferies analysts said.

The PJM Interconnection on Thursday filed a revised reliability backstop procurement, or RBP, proposal with federal regulators, with a goal of beginning the process on March 1 — about seven months later than originally planned.

The revisions come after the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission in late September rejected most of PJM’s initial proposal, which was designed to fill a capacity shortfall driven by data center development. FERC said in its decision that the original plan’s provisions for cost allocation, transmission owner exit rules and load-serving entity, or LSE, collateral requirements may have been unjust and unreasonable.

The proposed revisions were “carefully tailored” to address the issues identified by FERC while keeping the RBP’s fundamental structure and policy objectives intact, PJM said in its recent filing.

Under the new proposal, “PJM is using more current information on large-load growth to improve cost causation, revising the collateral methodology, and, importantly, recognizing that an RBP obligation and its associated collateral can move with the load-serving obligation when a large customer changes LSEs,” Maria Scheller, vice president of energy and planning at the consulting firm ICF International, said in an email to Utility Dive.

The proposal sharply reduces risks for providers of last resort and default service providers — utilities that provide power to customers that don’t buy electricity from power marketers, according to Scheller.

“If a large load initially relies on default service, the [provider of last resort] may temporarily carry significant RBP collateral and liquidity requirements,” she said. “The key credit question is whether state rules provide timely, certain recovery and adequate customer security, so the utility remains an administrative intermediary rather than taking meaningful balance-sheet or stranded-cost risk.”

As a result, the state role is especially important in the process’ outcome, and some state utility regulators may need to craft new rules, according to Scheller.

“Regulators may need to determine customer-specific allocation, establish appropriate security and cost-recovery rules, track changes in suppliers, and decide what happens if the underlying large load shrinks, defaults or disappears,” she said.

The proposal reduces the direct risk to residential and other existing customers, but it does not eliminate it, according to Scheller.

“Administrative costs, collateral and financing costs, regulatory lag, customer default and stranded-cost risk can still create indirect exposure if the state-level mechanisms do not keep the obligation tightly attached to the large load that caused it,” she said.

The planned procurement aims to fill a 6.8-GW capacity shortfall from PJM’s 2028/29 base residual auction.

However, the procurement may shrink significantly from the initial target after PJM recalculates how much capacity is needed, according to equity analysts with Jefferies.

PJM utilities, such as PPL Electric, FirstEnergy and AES Ohio, recently lowered their overall, near-term large load forecasts by 6.5 GW, the analysts said in an Oct. 28 note, citing recent filings to PJM’s Load Analysis Subcommittee. That reduction could reduce PJM’s 2027 load forecast, which would affect the grid operator’s backstop procurement target, they said. With that reduction, the procurement could be for as little as roughly 630 MW, according to the note.

After the Load Analysis Subcommittee vets the utilities’ requested adjustments to their large load forecasts, PJM expects to release its 2027 long-term load forecast in January, Jeffrey Shields, a spokesman for the grid operator, told Utility Dive in an email.

If the final large load forecast for 2028/2029 is lower than what was in this year’s long-term load forecast, it could contribute to a reduction in the amount PJM would procure through the RBP, Shields said.

But other factors will also affect the final procurement target, including updated accreditation numbers for resources that cleared the 2028/29 capacity auction in July, he said.

Also, the procurement could shrink if data center developers enter into bilateral contracts with power suppliers.

Even so, analysis by Charles River Associates discussed Monday by the subcommittee found that PJM could face a roughly 11.5-GW shortfall by 2030. PJM hired CRA to provide an independent data center demand forecast.

Under the proposal, PJM will close the RBP bid window on March 28, with the final procurement target and supply amounts determined on April 28, the grid operator said Thursday. PJM would post the results on May 10.

Editor’s note: This story was updated with comments from PJM.

#5784. Cranberry Bogs Were Unprofitable, Then Solar Saved The Day

One of the things many people still don’t realize is how much solar power and wind power help farmers to be more profitable or to become profitable. Agriculture has never been the easiest industry, but it has been getting especially challenging to make ends meet in some parts of the world and with some types of farming. Extra income from solar farms and wind farms can make all the difference.

I don’t think I’ve ever learned anything about cranberry farming, but, apparently, they are grown in bogs in the northern parts of the US — Wisconsin, Massachusetts, and New Jersey are the three largest producers in the country, in that order, while Oregon and Washington also contribute to the market. We now have news of a couple of cranberry bogs in Massachusetts that were out of service because of financial collapse but have now been saved by solar.

“Distributed Energy Infrastructure (DEI), a leading EPC provider for utility-scale solar and storage projects, announces the harvest of two groundbreaking cranberry agrivoltaics projects: the Black Cat Agrivoltaics Project in Plymouth, Massachusetts, and the Ring Road Agrivoltaics Project in Kingston, Massachusetts. Together, these projects have produced the first-ever agrivoltaic cranberry harvests — a milestone for expanding agrivoltaic applications into challenging wetland environments,” DEI shares.

“The projects are owned by developer Greenbacker Renewables were installed by Distributed. Cranberry farmer Iain Ward, who manages the bogs, was closely involved throughout design and construction to ensure that the sensitive wetland environment remained intact.”

“Building solar projects over active perennial wetland crops was an exciting challenge, and we’re thrilled to see the success of these harvests,” says Prateek Tare, Executive Vice President and Co-founder of DEI. “Iain’s collaboration and input at every stage was essential for us to ensure a reliable, cost-effective build while safeguarding the productivity of these heritage bogs.”

The Black Cat and Ring Road cranberry bogs had been out of service for years because they were no longer financially viable. However, the solar projects brought them back to life! “The solar projects now provide the landowner with additional income through land leasing, making the bogs’ cultivation economically viable again. Iain Warde is paid for agricultural management, in addition to his income from selling the cranberry crops.”

To close, here are some details of the project:

“Both projects were finalists for the North American Agrivoltaics Awards, and both Ward and DEI are founding members of the Solar & Farming Association (SAFA). SAFA is a coalition of organizations representing the interests of the agrivoltaics industry, including developers, agricultural producers, livestock producers, and affiliated organizations.”

Agrivoltaics wins again. Farming and solar power is one of the best combos we’ve seen since someone decided to spread peanut butter and jelly together inside some slices of bread.

Zach is tryin' to help society help itself one word at a time. He spends most of his time here on CleanTechnica as its editor-in-chief and CEO. Zach is recognized globally as an electric vehicle, solar energy, and energy storage expert. He has presented about electric vehicles and renewable energy at conferences in India, the UAE, Ukraine, Poland, Germany, the Netherlands, the USA, Canada, and Curaçao.

#5782. PSEG Power plans 200-MW nuclear uprate as company eyes data center deals

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

But one of the units at the Salem plant in New Jersey, which is jointly owned with Constellation Energy, faces an extended outage to deal with degraded equipment, according to federal filings.

PSEG Power and Constellation Energy are planning a 200-MW uprate at their jointly owned Salem nuclear power plant in southern New Jersey, according to an earnings presentation by Public Service Enterprise Group, PSEG Power’s parent company.

Salem’s current installed capacity is 2,280 MW. PSEG Power would have a 112-MW share of the uprate and Constellation would own the remainder, which would support a recently announced nuclear-focused power purchase agreement with Google.

PSEG Power’s roughly 3,760-MW nuclear fleet ran at a 95.5% capacity factor in the third quarter, up from 92.4% in the same period last year, according to the presentation.

However, there has been a snag at the Salem plant, PSEG said Monday in its quarterly filing with the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission.

During a scheduled refueling outage at Salem 1, a visual inspection last month revealed that the thermal shield inside the reactor vessel is degraded, according to the filing.

“We have initiated further testing, which is ongoing and is expected to extend the refueling outage,” PSEG said. “The impact of the outage on Salem 1 will depend on the timing and results of the inspection and necessary repairs.”

Meanwhile, PSEG continues to explore potential deals with data center developers centered around PSEG Power’s roughly 3.8-GW nuclear fleet, according to Ralph LaRossa, PSEG chair, president and CEO.

“PSEG Power is continuing to develop terms with large load entities for contracting arrangements involving existing nuclear production, future nuclear upgrades and other generation opportunities, similar to recently announced power purchase agreements in the PJM region and specifically in New Jersey,” he said during the earnings call.

FERC’s recent decision to reject key portions of the PJM Interconnection proposed plan for interconnection data centers and other large loads “reinforces that the focus has shifted to the states to address retail curtailment and cost issues associated with large load customers,” LaRossa said.

Public Service Electric and Gas Co., a PSEG utility, is working with the administration of New Jersey Gov. Mikie Sherrill, D, and the state’s utility regulators to limit electric cost increases for consumers and consider new utility regulatory models, according to LaRossa.

PSE&G, for example, plans to ask the New Jersey Board of Public Utilities to approve a plan for recovering the costs for investments made since its last distribution rate case, LaRossa said.

“Our filing … is all about staying aligned with the governor's goals to keep rates flat for as long as possible,” he said. The utility expects to file the proposal by the end of the year, PSEG said in its SEC filing.

Meanwhile, the BPU’s study on modernizing New Jersey's electric utility business model is now in its second phase, LaRossa noted.

“This initiative is focusing on cost discipline, financing modernization, targeted incentives and shared savings and staged performance-based ratemaking,” he said.

Those and other options mentioned in the Phase 1 report, such as multi-year rate plans, performance-based rates, performance incentive metrics, earnings sharing mechanisms, decoupling and shared saving mechanisms, provide “constructive paths to strengthen the connection between customer value, cost accountability and timely recovery of prudent investment,” LaRossa said.

PSE&G may have opportunities to invest in solar and grid readiness for distributed resources, but the extent of it could hinge on the state allowing utilities to own generating assets, according to LaRossa.

“I think the policy is not yet certain here in New Jersey as to whether or not it will move in a regulated direction or stay solely competitive,” he said.

On the financial front, PSEG’s third-quarter income fell to $376 million, or 76 cents/share, down from $622 million, or $1.25/share a year ago, mainly on mark-to-market losses at PSEG Power, the Newark, New Jersey-based company said in its SEC filing.

Third-quarter revenue slipped to $3 billion from $3.2 billion in the same period last year.

#5780. Westward Ho! First Trip Cross Country with Our New 2026 Tesla Model Y & Full Self Driving V14

We were headed west again for the 6th time, driving the ~1500 miles from our lake house in Northern Wisconsin to our winter home near the grandchildren in Northern Utah. We had totaled our 2019 Tesla Model 3 in an accident in May; therefore, this was our first road trip in our new 2026 Model Y running Full Self Driving V14. As usual, we were carrying two big ebikes on the back. We plan to pause in Utah at our home for a few days and then on to Portland, Oregon, and the Pacific Ocean.

The experience was quite different this time because of the new car and the significant upgrade to Tesla’s driving automation: Full Self Driving Supervised V14.3.12.

As we approached our first Supercharger in Wausau, Wisconsin, it looked like the navigation was taking us the wrong way. However, once we got to our normal exit, it was closed. We had to drive a few miles beyond it, do a U-turn, and come back. It cost us roughly an extra 5 miles. I’m going to have to learn to trust the navigation. The Nav knows about road closures like this and even traffic jams. Unfortunately, it doesn’t explain ahead of time why it is suggesting an alternate route.

Our first stop was at the Wausau, Wisconsin Supercharger next to the Hilton Gardens Inn on highway NN. In 7 years, we’ve never seen more than a few cars charging there. For the first time, the charging station was full (see photo below). It was Sunday after a big fall-colors week in Northern Wisconsin and many tourists were headed home. On our screen, it gave a charger wait time of five minutes. As we drove the last 100 miles to Wausau, our computer screen alternated between showing an unspecified wait time and as many as two stalls open.

When we arrived, it listed two stalls as open, but, in fact, for nearly the first time across the last seven years that we’ve been Supercharging, all eight stalls were full. However, here’s the good part: within less than five minutes, one of the cars pulled out and we were able to back into the empty stall. Then, the bad part: it was the slowest charge we’ve ever experienced. It was an older V2 hardware Supercharger where you need to share charging with the adjacent car. That meant that we were charging at only a 50-kilowatt speed for the whole charge. The total charge took about 40 minutes. However, after the bathroom break and walking our small rescue dog, Zuni, we only needed to kill about 15 minutes before setting off again.

The next stop was Menomonee, Wisconsin, halfway to Minneapolis and the charger was showing three empty stalls. Since, as I mentioned, we were traveling on a Sunday, a beautiful day, with full fall colors in the Northwoods, we found another Supercharger that appeared to be full (see below), but there was one stall empty and we pulled in. When we came east in mid-May, there were seldom more than one or two cars in any of the 12 Superchargers en route.

The Menomonee Supercharger has a newer Hardware 4 charger, so we were charging faster, at almost 100 kW during our whole charge. As we pulled out, there were about three cars waiting, but the line moved quickly. No one had to wait more than a few minutes. Then we were on our way to Minneapolis.

About 20 miles east of Minneapolis, crossing the Saint Croix River on the border between Wisconsin and Minnesota, half the lanes of the bridge were shut down. The navigation took us on a town of Henderson detour going on highways U and UU, then back onto the I-90 route across the bridge. It probably saved time avoiding the worst of the traffic backup because of the closure on the bridge. Then we were on an east–west route right through the center of Saint Paul–Minneapolis. Full Self Driving V14 with Tesla navigation found its way through the big city freeways perfectly.

As we left the Minneapolis Supercharger, we couldn’t get Full Self Driving to work! It said “FSD Temporarily Unavailable.” However, temporarily lasted for a very long time. We went through a long troubleshooting process with Ara (the Grok bot) and with Tesla Customer Service on the phone. Not only did FSD fail to work, but it was reporting a speed limit of 15 mph when the actual speed limit was 65 mph. First, we stopped, put the car in park, and did the two-scroll button reboot. No luck. Then we were instructed to use the menu to reboot the car. No luck. Then we tried to recalibrate the cameras as suggested, but it was nighttime and the calibration wouldn’t finish. Not only was FSD not working, but smart cruise control also failed to operate. However, after partial re-calibration of the cameras, it did restore smart cruise control.

On day two of our cross-country trip, we left Worthington in the southwest corner of Minnesota on I-90, passing through a huge wind turbine field (see below) with hundreds of turbines on the north side of the road just as we got into South Dakota.

A short time later, we saw a wind turbine tower factory with hundreds of turbine tower sections laid down as you see in the photo.

As we began our drive through South Dakota we had smart cruise control, but no FSD.

The first Supercharger stop in South Dakota was at Murdo. I went into the pharmacy in the adjacent supermarket and bought some medical tape and taped over the back camera. I did this because we found that, with our older car, strangely, taping over the back camera was the only way to get full FSD to work with bikes on the back.

We restarted the camera celebration, which had failed the night before in the dark. This time we observed the little gray steering wheel icon in the upper-left-hand corner of the screen. A blue circle starts at the bottom around the icon, and it progresses all the way around to indicate that the calibration is in progress. However, when it was almost complete, it started over at the halfway point. It oscillated back and forth a few times for almost an hour before it finally completed the calibration and we got FSD back! We had lost FSD for the better part of two days of our 1500-mile trip. Since we pay $99/month for FSD, will Tesla give us a refund?????

Our stop in the middle of South Dakota was at the Supercharger in Wall, home of the famous Wall Drugstore. See my wife and our Tesla with the bikes at the Supercharger in the photo below.

We charged in Wall, South Dakota, and drove another 80 miles to the wildlife loop at Custer State Park. There, “we ran with the bulls” — I mean, we ran with the bison (buffalo). We were driving right in the middle of a big bison herd, as you see in the photos below.

My wife spotted hundreds of pronghorns on our trip west, and the visit to Custer State Park would not be complete without seeing one, as well as feeding and petting the donkeys.

We made it by 8:00 pm to the tiny town of Lusk, Wyoming, where a four-stall Supercharger is across the street from the Pioneer Best Western where we spent the night. The next morning, we were in eastern Wyoming, where we were following double railroad tracks used to ship coal from the huge, high-quality, low-sulfur coal deposits in northeast Wyoming to power plants farther east.

“Coal Area — The Powder River Basin spans over 1,000 square miles, making it one of the largest coal-producing regions in the US,” Google shares. “Depth of Coal Beds — Coal beds in the region can be as thick as 250 feet, with only 120 feet of soil overburden.”

The photos below show one of the unit coal trains bringing coal from the Powder River Basin as we passed. Clean power is on the rise, but coal is still an important part of electrical generation in the United States.

Powder Basin coal is also going to the Dave Johnson power plant in eastern Wyoming that we passed. It is an 817 MW plant that was scheduled to shut down next year (2027). However, the Trump administration has “rescued” it and it won’t shut down or be converted to gas for at least a couple more years.

On the third day of our trip, FSD was working and we stopped to charge at Evansville near Casper, Wyoming. Then we passed Devils Gap, Independence Rock, and the pioneer handcart company memorial on our way to Rawlins. At Rock Springs, I left my wife at the TJ Max while I charged at the Tesla Supercharger 100 yards away. The final charger was in Evanston, Wyoming, and then we crossed the Utah border and passed Park City, Heber, and drove down Provo Canyon to our winter home in Lindon just north of Provo (30 miles south of Salt Lake City). It took three 500-mile days to make the trip.

Summary: Our new 2026 Tesla Model Y seemed to charge a little slower than our old Tesla Model 3 Long Range. However, it appeared to have longer battery range. This meant that we could often travel as fast as 80 mph, whereas previously we never exceeded 75 mph and occasionally had to slow down as low as 60 mph to make the next charger — in our old Model 3 with the bikes on the back. FSD was a pleasure, keeping the car precisely in the middle of the lane, passing cars, and returning to the right-hand lane just as I would do myself … except when FSD failed to work for a big part of two days during the trip. I’m 86, and it was a strain keeping the car centered in the lane and keeping a foot on the accelerator when FSD wasn’t working.

#5779. The industry races into Las Vegas for RE+

The largest tradeshow focused on the U.S. solar, storage and clean energy markets is back in Las Vegas for 2026. RE+ was the 24th largest tradeshow in the United States in 2025 by attendance, and this year’s showing (Nov. 16-19) is expected to be just as large.

The Formula 1 series will race the Las Vegas Grand Prix the weekend after RE+ concludes. Photos courtesy: F1 and the Las Vegas Grand Prix

The U.S. solar and storage industries have gotten so big that it appears only the Las Vegas Convention Center can accommodate a tradeshow bringing together nearly 40,000 clean energy professionals in one place. RE+ will be held at the Las Vegas Convention Center each November for the foreseeable future.

The convention center is tucked away from the main strip of casinos, but there is still plenty in the area to see and do. The site is also easily accessible, with a stop on the Las Vegas Monorail.

Conference education programming begins on Monday and sessions continue throughout the show at various stages on the exhibit hall floor. The exhibition opens on Tuesday.

Exhibitors will be spread across three halls in the convention center — the main entrance is at West Hall which extends via an over-street walkway to North Hall, and then Central Hall is accessed beyond a lobby area. There will also be a few outdoor booths to peruse located near food trucks. Visiting outdoor exhibits in November will be more comfortable, as the average high temperature is just 70°F, unlike the late summer heat of September.

Special events at RE+ include a golf tournament on Monday and the Run with the Sun 5K on Tuesday. The “Energy in Motion” block party will be at the Convention Center on Tuesday starting at 7 p.m. in the West Hall.

Formula 1 will race the Las Vegas Grand Prix the weekend following RE+. The course takes drivers down the Las Vegas Strip and around the Sphere, and road closures to the public will begin on Thursday. The Las Vegas Convention Center is several blocks north of the racing circuit, so the conference shouldn’t experience disruptions. After-conference activities might be affected later in the week, and attendees should plan a little extra time getting to the airport if leaving before the weekend.

Solar Power World editors will be covering all the sights at RE+ this year, so be sure to follow along on LinkedIn and right here on the website. But first, here’s a preview of a few must-see booths on the tradeshow floor.

Compatible with battery systems from SolarEdge, FranklinWH, Lunar Energy, EcoFlow and Fox ESS, IslandDER is trusted by leading national installers, offering more versatility and compatibility than any meter collar on the market.

#5778. Energy affordability tops voter concerns in election set to shake up Congress

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

“History shows us that energy crises create policy openings, and we think the conditions are probably in place for a window of policymaking,” Hasan Nazar, head of policy at Crux, told Utility Dive.

The outcome of Tuesday’s midterm elections could have serious implications for utilities, as candidates in both parties seek to capitalize on widespread concerns about energy affordability.

Policy areas that could see the biggest impact include data centers, energy tax credits and permitting reform,  experts told Utility Dive.

Hasan Nazar, head of policy at Crux, a finance technology company that connects tax credit buyers and sellers, said he believes energy affordability will be “determinative” in this election.

Polls have given Democrats a strong chance of taking control of the House and possibly also flipping the Senate, but even if they do, Nazar noted that there would still be a split government with a Republican administration in place. He hopes broad support for reform will lead to bipartisan cooperation on energy policy.

“History shows us that energy crises create policy openings, and we think the conditions are probably in place for a window of policymaking,” Nazar said.

All U.S. House seats are up for grabs, along with one-third of the Senate and 36 gubernatorial seats.

An Oct. 8 poll from Pew Research Center found that 74% of U.S. adults surveyed were concerned about the price of gasoline and energy, the largest share of concern among all surveyed issues.

As candidates campaigned this year, energy issues were central in many races.

Dylan Reed, Advanced Energy United’s managing director overseeing federal and political affairs, said Pennsylvania’s race is also significant given the role that Gov. Shapiro has played in the ongoing debate over the PJM Interconnection’s surging prices.

In 2025, Shapiro was one of the PJM state governors who threatened to pull out of the grid operator’s markets.

“Affordability is the message that we see out here very clearly,” Reed said. “I think when you are looking at high prices when it comes to your utility bill, the number one office that can impact that right now is the governor.”

Nazar said he thinks that following the election, in Congress “there’s probably going to be at least a discussion about new kinds of tax credit incentives that could be deployed.”

In 2022, then-Vice President Kamala Harris cast the tie-breaking vote in the Senate to pass the Inflation Reduction Act, which received no Republican votes and established long-horizon tax credits for clean energy along with new tax credit mechanisms like transferability.

In 2025, a Republican-controlled Congress passed the OBBBA, which shortened the horizon for some of the IRA’s clean energy benefits. Now, if Democrats take back control, they could seek to revise the OBBBA’s impacts, observers say.

“A flipped Congress where both houses are with the Democrats could have a dramatic turn on the energy policy landscape, and particularly with federal energy tax credits,” said Heather Cooper, a partner at the law firm McDermott Will & Schulte. “We have seen pretty dramatic flip-flopping in the last five years as Congress has gone back and forth.”

However, Cooper — who advises clients on federal income tax issues related to the energy transition — said she thinks the clean energy industry is prepared for the possibility of future flip-flopping.

“Most of the players who are in this market … The reality is all of their business models are kind of used to a world where these tax credits have 1, 2, 3, 4-year horizons,” she said.

The IRA, which created a longer runway for the first time, resulted in a “phenomenal amount of money poured into this industry,” she added.

Carl Fleming, global co-head of McDermott Will & Schulte’s energy and project finance group, said, “A number of folks have already worked out scenarios where some of these projects pencil out regardless of tax credits.”

“I do think you'll probably see an extension of the solar credit,” he said. “Solar took a good hit under OB3, but with rising energy costs, I think all that stuff's on the table because if I'm running for office or trying to get back into office, and I can find a technology that can help bring those energy costs down … whatever it takes to kind of get these energy costs down, that’s going to be on the table.”

Some utility executives have suggested that the furor over data centers and affordability could die down after the elections.

Fleming said he thinks there may be a more fundamental shift happening. He pointed to Texas’ pause on data center grid interconnections, aimed at weeding out speculative requests, as a potential policy model.

“You could question whether [the pause was] driven by the midterms or just normal processes, but I think that's going to be the way folks will look at this at the state level going forward — taking a kind of investment-type approach to these projects,” he said.

“It's no longer a race to power; it's a race to bankable power,” he continued. “Data centers are coming along so fast, much faster than people had anticipated. It's tough for legislation to keep up with.”

Reed said that the question of how to achieve speed to power, while preserving grid reliability and affordability, is what “every policymaker should be asking.”

Nazar said that post-election, he sees the chance for “durable” bipartisan legislation.

“I think it is actually quite an interesting opportunity next year to have a necessarily bipartisan approach to these policies, where energy is top of mind for voters,” he said. “I think the [Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act] is a good signal of potentially a return to cross-party substantive, serious and impactful energy policymaking that I could foresee carrying into next year.”

The Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act aims to speed federal permitting for energy and infrastructure projects, protect permits for approved projects and expand the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission’s power to eliminate obstacles to new transmission. It was announced in September, the result of careful negotiation between Republicans and Democrats.

But bill co-sponsor Sen. Sheldon Whitehouse, D-R.I., warned in October that the deal could be “[blown] up” by the Trump administration’s moves to revoke federal permits for offshore wind farms.

The bill has also been criticized by some consumer and clean energy advocates who say it would favor oil and gas projects, weaken environmental laws, undermine state and Tribal authority and limit the judiciary’s ability to intervene to prevent harm.

Nazar said he thinks the potential for BAAJA to be amended “seems like that's an ongoing conversation that appears to be in play.”

“That said, I think we will see how the dynamics shift after tomorrow, if they shift at all,” he added.

According to Crux’s conversations with developers, Nazar said, “Predictability is the most important thing they could ask for from the federal government.”

Shortages of skilled labor and equipment, as well as local opposition, are threatening even high-profile projects backed by big tech companies. Developers are responding by embracing flexibility and alternative technologies once considered too unwieldy.

The PJM Interconnection’s plan for curtailing large loads that don’t have matching new generation intrudes on state jurisdiction and appears to be discriminatory, the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission said.

#5777. “Everything Trump Touches Dies” — A Warning For America

In August 2018, Rick Wilson, a long-time GOP media consultant, published a book entitled Everything Trump Touches Dies. In it, he argued that MAGA and Trump will ruin the reputations, careers, and stability of the people and institutions that choose to align with him. Recently, Paul Krugman and Heather Cox Richardson published separate posts on Substack that bring Wilson’s warning forward to the present.

Krugman based his article on the writings of Robert Pape, author of the Substack Escalation Trap, who argues that, as the loss of control of the House and possibly the Senate becomes more likely, it is making Trump more dangerous than ever. Krugman wrote:

“It’s a well-understood proposition that the owners of businesses that are on the verge of going bust often engage in destructive behavior. And Trump is about to go bankrupt — not financially but politically. So he, like a failing businessman, has strong incentives to wreak havoc.

“Pape’s argument is basically a version of the phenomenon often called ‘gambling for resurrection.’ When a company is on the edge of failure, it makes sense for the owner to make highly speculative, even crazy investments that are highly likely to fail but might, just might, make enough money to pull the company back from the brink. After all, for the owner it’s heads he wins, tails his creditors lose.

“In Trump’s case, all indications are that his party is heading for defeat in the midterms. And while a normal president might be able to live with a Congress controlled by the opposing party, there has been so much corruption and malfeasance over the past two years that Democratic control of Congress, and the subpoena and investigative power that will bring, will have catastrophic consequences for Trump and everyone around him.

“So Trump has an incentive to do something — anything — that might shake up this election. Attacking Iran would probably lead to an even bigger Republican defeat, but it might, just might, rally Trump’s base enough to change the outcome. True, a president who cared at all about what is good for America wouldn’t pursue disastrous policies for the slim chance of saving his own political skin. But this is, after all, Trump we’re talking about.”

According to Richardson, the Wall Street Journal has reported that Vladimir Putin has proposed a meeting next month with Trump and China President Xi Jinping to decide the fate of Ukraine.

The report explained that “a three-way summit with China and the U.S. would cement Russia’s status as one of the world’s three great powers, a status Putin has sought since 2000 when he entered the Kremlin. Such a summit would formally acknowledge that the U.S. had broken from Europe and allied with Russia, a goal for Putin as he tries to take control of Ukraine.”

Richardson wrote, “After ten years of Trump insisting that reports of his cooperation with Russia were a hoax, here we are, watching one profoundly unpopular man tear apart the global system under which we have lived for eighty years in order to climb into bed with Vladimir Putin.”

Writing in The Atlantic, national security scholar Tom Nichols said, “The American commander in chief has abandoned the Western order, the most successful — and powerful — security arrangement in human history.” Richardson observed:

“This decision appears to have had nothing to do with the national interest of the United States…. Instead, Trump appears to have thrown out the world order because of his own personal interests, his admiration for Putin, his resentment of Ukraine’s president Volodymyr Zelensky, and quite possibly other factors that so far have remained obscured from the American public.

“Outrage over Trump’s actions has come from across the political spectrum,” she wrote. “Nichols notes that former federal prosecutor turned conservative commenter Andrew McCarthy wrote that Trump is giving Putin ‘aid and comfort.’ Those words echo Article III, Section 3 of the Constitution, which defines treason against the United States, as ‘levying War against them, or in adhering to their Enemies, giving them Aid and Comfort.’

“McCarthy’s blistering article in the National Review on Saturday reminds readers that Putin is a murderous dictator who is actively supporting Iran’s military operations against the United States with both intelligence and drones and provoking U.S. allies in the North Atlantic Treaty Organization. And yet Trump is siding with him against Ukraine, a U.S. ally. McCarthy demanded Congress hold ‘immediate hearings about this week’s shocking escalation of the Trump-Putin alliance.’”

Krugman warned that a cornered Trump may resort to what he calls “policy looting” by “using his remaining power to take actions that are both deeply unpatriotic and deeply unpopular, but fit his own preferences and prejudices. With nothing left to lose, he will be free to be his worst self — a malignant narcissist who instinctively supports authoritarians and is preoccupied with revenge against anything and anyone that threatens his fragile grandiosity.” In other words, a raging lunatic with access to the nuclear codes.

“Anyone who believes Trump will become more cautious, more moderate in the aftermath of midterm defeat just doesn’t understand the new political reality. The truth is that political bankruptcy will free Trump to pursue his true desires. And those desires are horrifying,” he wrote.

Left to his own devices, Trump is an existential threat to the United States that came into being in 1776. Don’t overlook the fact that the US Supreme Court has enabled and emboldened him, based on a legal theory known as “the unitary executive,” a creation of conservative groups like the Federalist Society that has no basis in the Constitution.

Richardson concludes her essay with this observation:

“The Framers of the Constitution thought they had designed a system that would prevent the rise of a tyrant. They believed U.S. representatives would be so fearful of a leader’s abuse of power that they would impeach any president who overstepped his authority, and that U.S. senators would be so jealous of their own power they would convict, and tyranny would not be able to take root.

“The Framers especially did not anticipate that senators, to whom they deliberately gave longer terms than they gave the president, would put party over country and stand aside as a president and his appointees captured the prerogatives of the Senate.

“But even given the evidence of the past ten years, it is truly astonishing to see Republicans, who for more than forty years have championed President Ronald Reagan for his stance against the Soviet Union, stand aside as Trump trashes the post–World War II system of global security in order to ally the United States with a Russian dictator.”

In the end, the people who fashioned the Constitution simply failed to anticipate the overwhelmingly corrosive influence of power, nor did they provide a way to rein in a madman. RIP, America. The world may never see the likes of you again.

Paul Krugman likes to end his Substack posts with a song. Here’s one that seems pertinent, given the news reports that the US has warned the Gulf nations to expect renewed attacks on Iran shortly after the midterm elections.

#5776. US to install 36% more wind turbines in 2025 than previous year

The United States is expected to add more than 7 GW of wind installations in 2025, a 36% increase over the previous year, and the five-year outlook remains unchanged quarter-over-quarter from previous forecast, according to the “U.S. Wind Energy Monitor” report released by Wood Mackenzie and the American Clean Power Association (ACP).

Amid a challenging market environment, the report shows that the United States is on track to add 46 GW of new wind capacity from 2025 to 2029, with total projected volumes unchanged quarter-on-quarter from previous forecasts. However, timing has shifted, as 2026 and 2027 will deliver significant gains, at 10.7 GW and 12.7 GW, respectively, as more assets advance through the development pipeline.

While Q3 installations came in 23% below forecast at 932 MW, the market has shown progress with 3.8 GW queued for Q4 2025 — representing 52% of the year’s total expected capacity. This back-loaded installation pattern is consistent with typical project-commissioning timelines.

U.S. turbine order intake has rebounded to pre-One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA) levels quarter-over-quarter, supported by 2+ GW of firm commitments in Q3, the biggest intake in the region in the last nine months and a 79% quarter-over-quarter increase. However, true visibility remains limited as OEMs increasingly withhold project details and much qualifying “start-of-construction” activity occurs through off-site component manufacturing.

The market will then see a noticeable drop off, as 2029 weakens meaningfully quarter-over-quarter following project cancellations and inactive designations for late-decade capacity, driven by permitting and broader development challenges.

“The U.S. power market is facing mounting strain after a decade of flat demand, with utilities committing to 160 GW of large-load additions. This represents a significant opportunity for wind energy, which benefits from strengthened economic fundamentals and a compelling business case driven by its competitively low LCOE,” said Leila Garcia da Fonseca, director of research at Wood Mackenzie. “However, turbine costs remain elevated due to tariffs and mid-term wind growth will depend on resolving permitting and policy uncertainty.”

Power demand growth through 2029 is expected to average around 3% compared to just 0.7% over the previous decade, with data centers accounting for approximately 59 GW of the 90 GW total peak demand growth. This surge in baseload demand positions wind as a natural fit to meet rising power needs.

The five-year capacity outlook remains unchanged at 39.8 GW of added capacity quarter-over-quarter. The 2025-2027 pipeline is fully committed with all projects having turbine orders in place. More than 60% of the three-year capacity outlook has been commissioned or is under construction.

Activity is led by western states, such as Wyoming, New Mexico and others, that will account for 34% of activity in this time period. Major projects driving the outlook include Pattern’s 3.5-GW SunZia project in New Mexico, which will position the developer as 2026’s top installer, and Invenergy’s 998-MW Towner Energy Center in Colorado, the largest single project expected in 2027.

The market continues expanding geographically, with Arkansas bringing its first utility-scale onshore wind project online through Cordelio’s Crossover Wind.

The repowering market remains strong, as Wood Mackenzie projects that 18 projects will drive 2.5 GW of capacity additions in the next three years.

Wood Mackenzie expects the offshore installation pace to slow in Q4 2025 due to harsh winter weather conditions, pushing remaining capacity into 2026. Despite these near-term adjustments, Vineyard Wind has demonstrated strong execution, connecting 15 turbines in Q3 and delivering 200 GWh through nine months.

“U.S. offshore wind shows diverging momentum,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Projects under construction with COD estimated for 2026 continue to hit key milestones, but post-2027 developments face potential delays amid constrained wind turbine installation vessel capacity, driving delays and contract terminations.”

The offshore sector is also experiencing significant financial pressure.

The report highlights that uncertainty around tariffs is a threat to projections. Wood Mackenzie projects that tariffs will drive up turbine costs in 2026, before moderating in subsequent years. In total, U.S. onshore wind capex is projected to increase by 5% through 2029.

“U.S. wind turbine pricing is experiencing unprecedented uncertainty as conflicting market and regulatory forces interact,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Domestic manufacturing overcapacity relative to permitted project volumes, particularly after 2028, would normally place downward pressure on prices. Despite this, onshore wind costs are expected to continue rising due to tariff exposure on raw material inputs and subcomponents.”
