#5761. As Diesel Costs Surge for Farmers & Rural Businesses, Solar Offers a Lifeline

The cost of diesel fuel rose nearly 33% from 2025 to 2026, with some countries experiencing even higher price spikes. For many of the world’s rural farmers, processors, and small businesses, solar-powered equipment offers an attractive alternative.

On a dry hillside in Chamwino, central Tanzania, mango farmers used to watch their trees struggle through the long months without rain. Irrigation was technically possible: A diesel pump could lift water from a nearby borehole. But in practice, fuel was too expensive and too unpredictable for smallholders to run the system often enough. When diesel prices rose, farmers irrigated less, production dropped, and most households stayed locked into low-yield farming and razor-thin margins.

In 2021, a solar-powered pump and drip irrigation system replaced the diesel setup as part of a pilot project. Within one season, farmers reported that their mango production and average income from mango sales roughly doubled, as farmers could irrigate beyond the rainy season and manage their orchards more intensively. The technology change was simple—solar instead of diesel. But for farmers, it meant shifting from a fuel prone to wild price swings to an energy source they could rely on and afford.

Chamwino is not an isolated case. Across Africa and other regions, farmers, processors, and rural businesses are among the most exposed to diesel price spikes, like those happening right now. Because diesel powers irrigation pumps, generators, mills, and refrigerators, every price jump shows up as higher operating costs, thinner margins, and greater risk.

Distributed clean energy is emerging as one of the most practical ways to break that dependence.

While access to electricity has expanded significantly over the last 10 years, the reality is that diesel still powers much of rural life in places like sub-Saharan Africa. Only 33% of the rural population can access electricity. Even among those with power connections, frequent outages and poor quality have led farmers, processors, retailers, mines and telecommunications companies to rely on diesel generators for backup or primary power. An Energy for Growth Hub study estimated that 150–190 gigawatts (GW) of distributed diesel generator capacity was operating across Africa in 2024, compared with 268 GW of grid-connected capacity. Removing South Africa from the equation, Sub-Saharan Africa had nearly 1.5 times as much diesel generator capacity as grid-connected capacity. And diesel capacity exceeded grid capacity in at least 26 African countries.

The war on Iran has exposed the vulnerability created by this “shadow power system.” The average global retail diesel price exceeded $1.50 per liter in the second quarter of 2026, 20% higher than the previous three months and 32.9% higher than a year earlier. In Africa, retail diesel prices rose by 25% on average across 42 countries between mid-January and mid-June 2026.

The regional average masks sharp differences between countries. In some, subsidies, tax cuts, and administered fuel prices have cushioned consumers from rising international prices, but at a cost to government budgets, especially in fuel-importing economies with limited fiscal space. Elsewhere, consumers have faced much larger increases at the pump. Diesel prices increased by 86% in Nigeria and more than 50% in Tanzania and Ethiopia from January to June 2026.

The effects extend beyond the cost of running generators. Diesel powers agricultural machinery, irrigation pumps, mills and refrigerators. Diesel-fueled road transport connects farms with suppliers and consumer markets. The impacts of the Iran conflict are already rippling through interconnected energy, fertilizer, trade and logistics networks, raising the costs of agricultural production and food distribution across Africa and elsewhere. Fertilizer prices have increased by 30-45% in several African markets since the conflict began, increasing the risk that farmers will reduce application rates, switch to other crops or miss their planting windows.

The effects are felt most acutely at the local level. For example, members of a women’s groundnut processing cooperative in Nigeria reported that rising fuel and fertilizer costs increased the price of raw groundnuts and groundnut oil by 33%. In Myanmar, which imports more than 90% of its fuel and relies predominantly on road transport, higher diesel prices have increased the cost of moving food and agricultural inputs, and have led to rising rice prices for consumers.

We already have better alternatives. Many locations are struggling with diesel-based irrigation, cooling and backup power, but solar and other distributed renewables can provide more affordable and reliable electricity than diesel-dependent systems.

The shift is already gaining momentum. In Africa, 17 GW of solar capacity will be installed in 2026 — a 45% increase from 2025, equivalent to approximately 100,000 panels installed each day. Much of the recent growth is happening outside large power plants: Roughly three-quarters of Africa’s solar growth between 2023 and 2025 came from distributed systems, as households and businesses sought alternatives to unreliable supply and diesel generation.

The benefits are already visible for some who have made the switch.

Ethiopia’s Berbere district, or woreda, is surrounded by four rivers, yet for years farmers could not use that water for irrigation. Limited access to electricity and the high cost and unreliability of petrol and diesel made pumping water from rivers to fields impractical, keeping production closely tied to rain-fed maize and teff. In 2025, WRI and partners installed two solar-powered water pumps to lift river water to irrigated plots. In the first production season, households involved in the project earned an average $709 from onion cultivation alone, compared with around $207 annually from maize and teff before the solar-powered pumps were installed. Farmers also reported being able to harvest more than once a year, plant surplus crops such as onions and chilies to increase their incomes, and make new investments in livestock and home improvements.

Similarly, when Kenya experienced its longest nationwide power blackout in 2023, one Nairobi hospital maintained critical services thanks to its solar-plus-battery storage system. For rural clinics and county hospitals, similar systems can ensure power even when the grid fails, without tying budgets to volatile fuel prices and expensive back-up diesel generators.

There’s huge potential for distributed clean energy to further break diesel dependence and improve livelihoods in places like rural Africa and elsewhere. Energy Access Explorer, an open online geospatial platform hosted by the WRI Polsky Energy Center, lets planners examine solar potential alongside grid networks, health facilities, schools, and farming communities. Bringing these layers together can help governments and practitioners identify where clean power could cost-effectively serve livelihoods and services that currently depend on diesel.

For example, Energy Access Explorer is already informing electrification planning in Ethiopia. The Ministry of Water and Energy, working with the World Bank, has used the platform to prioritize off-grid electrification through a results-based financing program. Our latest analysis reveals that about 12.6 million people in Ethiopia live in areas with high agricultural activity and high solar potential, but little access to grid-connected electricity. These areas could be assessed for solar-powered irrigation, processing, cooling and other services that could benefit from distributed clean power.

A Uganda analysis applies the same approach to health services. The map below shows 243 health facilities and approximately 4.6 million people living in areas with limited or no connection to electricity. Yet these areas do receive abundant sunlight, making them suitable for solar power. Used alongside data on facility electricity demand, existing supply reliability, and roof or land availability, the maps can help prioritize clean power investments for rural health services.

As diesel prices continue to swing wildly with every conflict and crisis, farmers, processors, and rural businesses pay the price, alongside governments that try to keep costs low through subsidies. It’s time for energy ministers, donors and financiers to look closely at where distributed clean power can viably replace diesel and direct their investments there. The latest Tracking SDG 7 report estimates that $21 billion is needed by 2030 to provide off-grid solar to the 398 million people for whom it is the least-cost electricity option—approximately 6 times the sector’s recent investment level!

Moving from energy crisis to energy security is ultimately about knowing where clean energy truly changes livelihoods. For many of the world’s rural farmers, processors, and small businesses, that starts with swapping diesel for solar.

By Dimitris Mentiss, Lead, Energy Access Explorer

#5760. Participation in Sunrun’s Puerto Rico VPP program doubled in 2026

Sunrun announced that participation in its Puerto Rico distributed power plant has doubled to more than 60,000 customers, representing nearly 70,000 home batteries. The distributed power plant frequently and reliably delivers 45 MW of dispatchable power for four hours at a time, providing critical support to Puerto Rico’s electric grid.

Since April, Sunrun has dispatched its power plant more than 60 times to weather severe energy shortfalls and prevent rolling blackouts. During an extended period of unseasonably hot temperatures this fall, Sunrun dispatched its home batteries to the grid for 18 consecutive days beginning October 13.

Sunrun’s Puerto Rico distributed power plant has scaled rapidly since 2024, when approximately 4,000 customers were enrolled. Enrollment surged to more than 30,000 customers in 2025. Sunrun is the largest aggregator in Puerto Rico’s Customer Battery Energy Sharing program.

Puerto Rico’s electric utility operator, LUMA, said in June and again in July that energy from home batteries helped “maintain system stability and prevent outages that would have affected thousands of customers.”

“What began in Puerto Rico out of necessity for family resilience has become a vital blueprint for the U.S. mainland, where severe weather and the proliferation of AI data centers are demanding new energy solutions,” said Sunrun CEO Mary Powell. “Sunrun is uniquely positioned to provide these solutions, whether to support a grid at its breaking point or to provide capacity for hyperscalers looking to come online.”

Sunrun’s increased dispatching capacity in Puerto Rico comes after residents on the island experienced an average of 36 hours of power outages in 2025 that were not caused by major adverse weather, which is 19% more than the previous year, according to the U.S. Energy Information Administration. When severe weather is factored in, residents experienced an additional 23 hours of power interruptions.

Customers who participate in Sunrun’s Puerto Rico distributed power plant choose how much energy to share, with most contributing a small portion of their total battery capacity. This approach ensures they retain reserve power for personal backup power in the event of grid outages. Participants are financially compensated for the power they choose to share, while Sunrun generates recurring revenue by operating the distributed power plant.

#5758. De-Escalated Treatment for HPV-Related Throat Cancer Gets Long-Term Supporting Data

— More than 90% of patients alive without evidence of disease at 5 years

Reduced-intensity treatment for human papillomavirus (HPV)-positive oropharyngeal cancer (OPC) led to durable cancer control in more than 90% of patients, long-term follow-up from a prospective study showed.

After a median follow-up of 5 years, 94% of patients had no evidence of disease and 90.6% were alive at last follow-up after de-escalated elective nodal irradiation (ENI) during definitive concurrent chemoradiation (CRT). Five-year locoregional control, distant metastasis-free survival (DMFS), and overall survival (OS) exceeded 90%. All but one patient with locoregional failure had recurrences in areas with known residual macroscopic disease. No treatment failures occurred in nodal basins that were omitted from treatment.

De-escalation came in the form of 30-Gy ENI instead of the usual 50-60 Gy dose and omission of low-risk nodal basins, reported Nancy Y. Lee, MD, of Memorial Sloan Kettering Cancer Center in New York City, and colleagues in JAMA Oncology.

"These outcomes were attained across the spectrum of T- and N-stage groups and smoking histories among patients predominantly treated with concurrent platinum-based chemoradiosensitization," the authors noted. "The systematic omission of uninvolved nodal levels IB/V did not result in failures in those areas and provides a simple class solution to reducing irradiated volume and toxic effects. The 30-Gy ENI dose remains the lowest among published approaches, is tailored to HPV-associated disease, and results in the lowest reported gastrostomy tube insertion rate."

"Although low-dose ENI without concurrent chemotherapy has been investigated, recent phase III randomized trials have demonstrated the superiority of concurrent platinum-based chemotherapy for achieving cure in HPV-associated OPC, underscoring the importance of de-escalating ENI within an optimally curative paradigm for HPV-associated OPC," they wrote.

The study showed that focusing on radiation dose is not the only strategy to de-escalate treatment for HPV-associated OPC, said David Sher, MD, of UT Southwestern Medical Center in Dallas, who was not involved in the study.

"While we often focus on the total dose delivered to visible cancer, in actuality, ENI is responsible for a substantial amount of normal tissue irradiation and dose to critical normal structures, such as the salivary glands and swallowing structures," Sher, an expert for the American Society for Radiation Oncology, told MedPage Today. "There has been a progressive recognition that ENI dose can be a significant contributor to quality-of-life outcomes, and there are several studies, both prospective and retrospective, that have shown that the standard dose of 50 Gy can be substantially lowered."

"[The] degree of success is remarkable given the extent of dose reduction, and it is exciting to see such superb outcomes," he added. "Although we do not have quality-of-life data from this study, prior reports were also very favorable."

Providing additional context for the study results, Sher noted that all patients underwent PET-CT imaging and the vast majority had MRI as well. Such advanced imaging may play a key role in identifying smaller lymph nodes that might harbor cancer and require higher radiation doses. Additionally, all the patients received cisplatin-containing chemotherapy, and HPV-associated OPC is cisplatin sensitive. Also, the authors did not report how many "equivocal" lymph nodes were treated with a higher radiation dose.

Results of the current study add to those of a previous phase III trial that used a slightly higher nodal irradiation dose, he added.

"All of this work strongly suggests we are delivering ENI doses that are far too high, and we need to be more aggressive in reducing the acute and long-term side-effect burden of our treatments while preserving oncologic efficacy," said Sher. "For such a result to become a true standard of care, though, we do need a phase III randomized study to confirm non-inferiority in a multi-institutional setting, and hopefully such a trial can be available soon to patients and our community."

Lee and colleagues previously reported 2-year outcomes with the de-escalation strategy, showing good locoregional control and survival with "excellent" quality of life. The current report aimed to establish the durability of the strategy, as assessed after a median follow-up of 5 years.

The analysis involved 276 patients treated with intensity-modulated radiotherapy (n=256) or intensity-modulated proton therapy (n=20). Treated nodal and subclinical regions received 30 Gy in 15 fractions and a sequential boost of 40 Gy in 20 fractions.

The 5-year results showed that 260 patients had no evidence of disease at 5 years and 250 were alive at last follow-up. Five-year outcomes were:

A single elective nodal failure occurred 6 months after concurrent CRT in a contralateral indeterminate lymph node present at baseline and treated with 30 Gy, the authors reported. The last regional failure occurred 18 months post-treatment. Distant-only recurrences occurred in 23 patients. At last follow-up, 12 of the patients remained alive with no evidence of disease, and one remained alive with disease.

#5757. More of the Same Expensive Dependence on Fossil Fuels in TEP and APS 2026 Energy Plans

PHOENIX — Arizona utilities Tucson Electric Power Company (TEP) and Arizona Public Service (APS) filed their 2026 Integrated Resource Plans (IRPs) on Friday that evaluate how utilities will obtain energy for the next 15 years. While their plans include new renewable energy and storage, they continue to spend on fossil fuels, building new gas, and in the case of APS, extending coal use. Instead of facilitating a timely transition to affordable, clean energy, both utilities — and especially APS — continue an expensive dependence on fossil fuels that keep ratepayers in a dangerous cycle, paying the price for decades.

Arizonans already experience some of the highest electric bills in the country, with some of the most planned gas in the country. Yet, instead of easing energy burden and saving its customers millions of dollars, APS prolongs costly coal operations at the Four Corners coal plant, worsening climate impacts, increasing air pollution, and threatening public health.

Of all the state’s utilities, APS has the most planned fossil gas, adding over 4,000 MW of gas by 2041. APS’s gas projects include the planned Desert Sun Power Plant and the Redhawk Expansion Project, which includes constructing eight new methane gas-fired generating turbines in Maricopa County. It has also signed a coal supply contract extension to resuscitate its Four Corners Plant, abandoning APS’s previous plan to exit the plant in 2031 and instead extending coal-fired operations to 2038. In a pending case before the Arizona Corporation Commission, APS is also proposing a 14% rate hike that would increase the average residential customer’s utility bill by roughly $240 per year.

TEP’s energy plan includes coal to gas conversions of units 1 and 2 at its Springerville plant by 2031 rather than retirement of those units as TEP had previously proposed. TEP plans to exit its share of the Four Corners coal plant by 2031, but also plans to add 300 MW of new fossil gas generation. TEP had previously committed to reduce carbon emissions 80 percent by 2035 but now says in its plan that it won’t achieve that goal.

The public can submit comments on both utility energy plans. Both APS and TEP’s plans will come before the Arizona Corporation Commission to be approved or rejected.

“Arizonans across the state support a timely transition from fossil fuels to 100% clean energy by 2050, but what the utilities have presented in their energy plans further delays our move from gas and coal to solar and wind,” said Sierra Club Grand Canyon Chapter Director Sandy Bahr. “We’re seeing these utilities protect their own bottom lines, instead of making the most economical decisions for ratepayers like you and me. More than ever, we need to embrace a clean energy future to protect our health, the climate, and our wallets.”

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5756. They Are Trying to Silence Us

State and federal government officials are trying to harass, intimidate, and silence NRDC and other nonprofit environmental advocates.

The Attorney General of West Virginia recently initiated an investigation of NRDC and a handful of other groups on the stated ground that we may have committed consumer fraud. The theory appears to be that it’s deceptive for NRDC to say that the burning of fossil fuels contributes to climate change, which, in turn, causes harm to the planet and its people. But that’s fact, not fraud.

To pursue his investigation, the AG served a subpoena seeking a vast trove of confidential internal and external communications relating to our advocacy around climate change for the past 10 years. The demand could cover sensitive strategy memos for policy advocacy, draft comments on regulations, exchanges with coalition partners, back-and-forth among scientists, dialogues with donors, and drafts of NRDC publications; all disclosed to a hostile adversary.

Co-counseling with Democracy Forward, we responded to the subpoena by preparing a federal lawsuit asserting infringement of our First Amendment rights to speak, associate with others, and advocate for effective solutions to the climate crisis. Those solutions include the replacement of fossil fuels with renewable sources of energy. And that is what drives the AG’s attempt to punish NRDC: pure retaliation for the content of our point of view.

At 2:13 p.m. on October 22, we advised the AG that we were going to file suit early that evening. A little more than two hours later, at 4:32 p.m., the AG withdrew the subpoena.

The AG is not alone in trying to bully NRDC. In September, the Committee on Natural Resources of the U.S. House of Representatives sent a letter of inquiry to NRDC and 18 other nonprofit environmental groups, informing us they are investigating the groups’ litigation practices. Why? According to the Committee, the targeted groups “build powerful and profitable litigation machines funded by taxpayer dollars through the exploitation of statutory fee-shifting provisions.” On this purported basis, the Committee demanded that NRDC disclose an exhaustive array of intrusive information and documents that could take months of concentrated effort to produce.

The premise of the Committee’s inquiry is that NRDC “effectively function[s] as [a] law firm[] funded by taxpayer dollars.” It calls NRDC “one of the most active litigating environmental nonprofit organizations driving the exploitation of statutory fee–shifting provisions.”

While NRDC is indeed an active litigant in federal court, it rarely receives attorney fees from the federal government. During our past five fiscal years, NRDC’s income from such fees in cases involving agencies or statutes identified in the Committee’s letter constituted 0.112 percent of our operating budget. In light of this numerical fact, contending that NRDC “build[s] a business model focused on siphoning tax dollars by weaponizing litigation awards” is unmoored from reality. With minimal effort, Committee members or staff could have discovered this from documents in the public record.

Courts have repeatedly held that the citizen-based enforcement litigation NRDC conducts, which holds government agencies accountable to the law, is a public service, and that Congress meant to encourage such suits by providing the opportunity to recover fees when a court determines an award is appropriate. This is the paradox: The Committee, which is part of Congress, accuses NRDC of stealing taxpayer money, when NRDC is doing precisely what Congress itself has empowered and encouraged NRDC to do.

It is not the only paradox. The Committee claims that its purpose is “to root out waste, fraud, and abuse of taxpayer dollars.” If so, is it investigating the nearly $4 billion in taxpayer funds the U.S. Department of the Interior has agreed to pay companies to abandon offshore wind projects that would produce economical, renewable energy? The government bought back the wind leases without any statutory authority to do so, drawing funds from the same pot of federal money that often covers attorney fee awards. The amount of taxpayer money expended on the buybacks during the past six months is more than 3,000 times the amount of attorney fees paid to NRDC over the past five years. To our knowledge, the Committee is not investigating the lease buybacks.

In response to the Committee majority’s attempt to intimidate NRDC, we sent this letter, asserting our First Amendment right to litigate to enforce the law. Thus far, the Committee has not sought to compel us to respond to its demand for documents.

The Natural Resources Committee has plenty of company within the House. The effort to harass environmental nonprofits and their funders is part of a multipronged attack that includes the House Committees on the Judiciary; Science, Space, and Technology; and Oversight and Government Reform. While NRDC is not yet a specific, named target of any of these additional House inquiries, other environmental nonprofits are.

These investigations and inquiries are designed to chill dissent, to punish those who disagree with the policies of those in power. The method of harassment is the same pernicious ploy we see repeatedly from the White House: Start with a lie (e.g., NRDC spreads deception, not facts, about climate change; NRDC feasts on federal attorney fees), then parlay the lie into a malicious attempt to deflect attention, divert resources, silence perceived enemies, and trample constitutional rights. It’s retaliation for protected speech. The West Virginia AG doesn’t want to debate the relative merits of renewable energy and fossil fuels—he wants to punish us because we disagree with him.

The recent moves by the West Virginia AG and the House Committees erode the rule of law. Not to speak out against them is to cede the narrative—to be complicit in a dangerous form of attempted repression. In the face of hostile questioning, the upright prosecutor Jack Smith recently told Congress that, while fear is contagious, so is courage. He’s right. Everyone who acquiesces in these abuses of government authority makes it easier for everyone else to do the same. Everyone who speaks out encourages others to resist.

It is not enough to survive this moment, silent and unscathed. As an organization rooted in the law, NRDC is compelled to fight for freedoms central to our democracy. The climate crisis is the existential issue of our day. But climate is not the only thing at risk.

We will persist without fear to address the ravaging effects of climate change and enforce the law against polluters. Our millions of members and supporters expect and deserve that from us. We did not succumb to intimidation, and we will not succumb to it if it comes our way again. This is the sole silver lining in the misguided attempt to bully NRDC into submission and silence: It will not work.

NRDC is the nation's most effective environmental action group, combining the grassroots power of 1.3 million members and online activists with the courtroom clout and expertise of more than 350 lawyers, scientists, and other professionals.

#5755. GTA VI Will Take Up To 212 GB On Your Console's Hard Drive

"Grand Theft Auto VI" is set to be the biggest video game of the decade, if not one of the biggest games ever made. That distinction isn't just figurative either because it's set to be absolutely huge in the literal, bits and bytes sense, too. Developer Rockstar Games announced Tuesday that the highly anticipated title will be available for digital pre-load starting November 10, and that the full game file will take up to 212 GB, "depending on your device," even telling gamers to "make sure you've got ample space to begin pre-loading as soon as possible."

To put that number into context, the standard PlayStation 5 comes with an advertised 1 TB or 825 GB of storage depending on whether you get the disc or digital version, and about 158 GB of that is taken up by the operating system. In other words, assuming the standard PS5 version of the game is indeed 212 GB and you've got a digital console without any external drives, just GTA VI will take up more than 25% of all your available space.

On the Xbox side, a Series X comes with either 1 TB or 2 TB while the entry-level Series S is offered in 1 TB or 512 GB drives, and of course, a good chunk of that is always reserved for the OS. Big thoughts and prayers for owners of the 512-gig Series S.

Given how much content this next Grand Theft Auto game is set to come with, a massive download isn't entirely surprising. The map is said to be double that of "GTA V," three times bigger than "Red Dead Redemption 2," and will apparently include "hundreds" of accessible building interiors. There are also hundreds of cars to account for, super detailed character models, and, y'know, the underlying logic and systems that make it all a playable game.

All that said, it's entirely possible that the quoted 212 GB size is only for the higher-fidelity PS5 Pro version of the game, and that console comes with 2 TB out of the box. However, the fact that Rockstar is quoting that number only as a blanket sort of thing leads us to believe that the standard version most people will download is indeed just as big, or not meaningfully far off.

Grand Theft Auto VI will release (or, for those who pre-load, become playable) on November 19 on PlayStation 5 and Xbox Series X and S consoles. For the real keeners, pre-load starts on November 10 — midnight local time on PS5, midnight GMT for Xbox.

#5752. The Future of Buildings in Illinois Is Electric

The Illinois Commerce Commission authorized a study of the most affordable way for the state to meet its building decarbonization goals. The report has fundamental flaws but critical insights.

Illinois’s landmark Climate and Equitable Jobs Act (CEJA) sets the goal of 100 percent clean energy across the state by 2050. For nonelectric sources of emissions and transportation—such as the 17 percent of emissions that occur when we burn gas to heat our homes and other buildings where we live, work, and play—CEJA provides little direction on how these sectors are expected to align with the state’s climate goals.

The Illinois Commerce Commission (ICC), which regulates the state’s electric and gas utilities, showed real leadership in 2023 when it found that “if the decarbonization goals of CEJA are to be met, the gas distribution system as currently operated will need to change.” To chart that change, the ICC launched a Future of Gas (FOG) open stakeholder process to identify the most efficient, cost-effective path to a gas system that fits the state’s climate goals.

As part of this FOG process, the ICC authorized a consultant to model five pathways to that CEJA-compliant system by 2050. In its recently released Gas Decarbonization Pathways Study, the consultant modeled a range of scenarios, from one where the state invests heavily in energy efficiency and helps customers replace outdated gas appliances with high-efficiency electric alternatives to others that rely on unproven biofuels with no clear evidence that they can be produced at anything close to the scale needed. For each scenario, the consultant was expected to assess technical feasibility, up-front and operating costs, impacts on the gas and electric systems, emissions reductions, and effects on environmental justice communities, all to help Illinois meet its gas decarbonization goals at the lowest cost and with the most benefits to residents.

Unfortunately, the consultant’s final report is biased by faulty (and at times, plainly unrealistic) assumptions that do not allow for accurate comparison across scenarios. Despite this, the study yields a clear finding: Gas use in Illinois’s buildings and industrial facilities must decline by at least 50 percent in order to reach the state’s climate target of net-zero emissions by 2050. Together with the state’s recently released Comprehensive Climate Action Plan, the study offers Illinois policymakers a clear agenda for initiating the transition to an affordable, equitable, and clean energy future.

The study offers some useful insights, but treating all of its findings as a road map for Illinois’s gas transition would be a mistake. Before getting into the no-regrets steps that Illinois can take now, we need to unpack the biggest problems with the report, including the flawed assumptions behind some of its conclusions.

Despite the study’s shortcomings detailed above, several key findings should influence the recommendations in the final FOG report:

The remaining technologies are incredibly early-stage, and to the extent that they are available at all over the next several decades, they are likely to be extremely expensive. The study’s assumptions around e-methane are particularly troubling. Modeling initially showed that the Continued Gas Use and Hybrid Gas cases could not meet the net-zero-by-2050 emissions requirement using only available technologies, like biomethane. As a result, in the final report, the model selected e-methane to backfill any resource gap—despite the fact that e-methane derived from green hydrogen and direct air carbon capture is still in “prototype and demonstration stage” today.

While the pathways study acknowledges that “[h]igher-gas scenarios present … greater challenges in alternative fuel procurement, technology readiness, long-term affordability, and air quality” compared to electrification-focused scenarios, this is a significant understatement. Scenarios relying on e-methane are infeasible and should not be considered on equal footing with scenarios that primarily rely on electric heat pumps, which already comprise 47 percent of air conditioner sales and have annually outsold gas furnaces since 2021.

The pathways study reinforces the need for Illinois to proactively plan for a future where gas demand declines, which occurs under all five scenarios despite flawed assumptions that heavily favor continued gas use scenarios. The state can and must act now to achieve the state’s climate goals in an affordable and equitable way. Illinois does not need to solve every aspect of the transition in the next few years. However, it can begin taking the following no-regrets steps now to prepare for a changing energy landscape.

Despite its numerous flaws, the study underscores the transformative role that clean, electric heat pumps will play in Illinois’s energy future and the need to plan for a smaller gas system. The ICC and the state legislature can and must begin advancing no-regrets building electrification policies today to ensure tomorrow’s energy system is healthy, clean, and affordable for all of the state’s residents.

By Madeline Semanisin, Policy Director, Illinois, Climate & Energy, NRDC; and Kiki Velez, Equitable Gas Transition Lead, Climate & Energy, NRDC

Featured photo by Diego Alberto Martínez Mendoza

#5751. Dodge Rejoins NASCAR Cup Series After 14-Year Absence

The entire Big Three will be battling for stock car supremacy in 2027. Dodge announced Monday that it will return to the NASCAR Cup Series next season. Kaulig Racing's pair of Dodge Chargers will be the first to compete in stock car racing's marquee series since the manufacturer's departure after the 2012 season. Plenty of questions remain after the announcement, including who will drive the entries and what the Gen 7 Charger's bodywork will look like.

The team partnering with Dodge was never a question. Kaulig Racing first paired up with Ram for the brand's return to the NASCAR Craftsman Truck Series this season, fielding five Ram trucks. It's surprising how quickly Stellantis North America cobbled together a Cup program. NASCAR confirmed that Dodge completed its wind tunnel testing and will have its production body panels ready later this month. Tim Kuniskis, Stellantis' Head of American Brands, said:

"Everyone said it was impossible to get back in one year. We said, 'watch this,' Sure, we could spend three years planning, testing and perfecting ... or we could do all three in real time, on the track. It's going to be exponentially harder this way. But slow has never been part of Dodge DNA. LFG."

The biggest reason why a Dodge program for the Cup Series was organized in only a year is that the manufacturer will not be building its own engines. Dodge has yet to announce where it will source Kaulig's 2027 motors, but it is in talks with a third party. The Athletic reported that ECR Engines could potentially build engines for Dodge. The partnership has issues because ECR currently produces Chevrolet engines and is owned by Richard Childress, a Chevrolet team owner. NASCAR, Ford and Toyota would need to approve an exemption for ECR to build engines for both Chevy and Dodge.

Besides the powerplant, Kaulig expects to announce its 2027 Cup Series driver lineup this weekend ahead of the season finale at Homestead-Miami Speedway. It wouldn't be a shock if the team retained its current pairing of Ty Dillon and A.J. Allmendinger. However, the driver market has seen a major shakeup over the past month. Brad Keselowski is departing RFK Racing, a team he co-owns, after falling out with fellow co-owners Fenway Sports Group. He stated last weekend that he will be available to race elsewhere regardless of manufacturer. Keselowski won Dodge's most recent Cup Series title with Penske in 2012. The 42-year-old admitted that he hasn't discussed anything with Dodge or Kaulig, but he's open to the idea.

#5750. Congress Is Cutting Off Transportation Investments that Help Families Save Money

The loss of guaranteed federal funding could stall projects that make it easier and cheaper for Americans to get where they need to go.

UPDATE: On October 27, 2026, an update to the Senate Commerce Committee’s Chopping Block report provided a state-by-state assessment of the harm to transportation systems if Congress fails to renew the Bipartisan Infrastructure Law’s $36.8 billion in advance appropriations funding. Led by Ranking Member Cantwell, the report demonstrates that these cuts will only drive increased costs for families and business across the country.

At a time when Americans are struggling to pay staggering prices at the fuel pump and the Trump administration sows uncertainty for states and communities by clawing back committed transportation infrastructure funds, Congress is moving to scale back more critical federal funding for affordable and clean transportation.

The need for consistent funding is clear: Transportation costs are the second-largest household expense in the United States, and our roads and transit infrastructure remain structurally inadequate. Nevertheless, state transportation and transit authorities are facing a significant funding cliff due to the expiration of the Bipartisan Infrastructure Law (BIL) on September 30, 2026. Over the past five years, the infrastructure law has provided a guaranteed pot of funding worth $36.8 billion for transportation projects. These “advanced appropriations” cover a wide breadth of programs and include large amounts of funding for clean transportation, such as electric vehicle (EV) charging infrastructure projects, transit, and rail investments. This funding has helped state transit and transportation authorities plan for longer-term projects and has been critical to the infrastructure law’s support of almost 70,000 unique transportation projects across the country.

But transportation funding from the infrastructure law ran out on September 30, 2026, and while Congress passed a short-term extension until December 11, 2026, it does not include these guaranteed dollars. This means that, for the three-month duration of the extension, states will lack the continuity and certainty they need to plan for both large- and small-scale infrastructure projects.

To add insult to injury, Congress also seems to be contemplating leaving this guaranteed funding out of the next transportation bill altogether. The House BUILD America 250 Act proposal, released in May, proposes cutting all advanced appropriations funding. The proposal, in fact, slashes transportation funding from current infrastructure law levels by almost 50 percent, leaving Americans without access to travel options that help defray rising costs.

Historically, transportation bills have included authorizations of two types of funding: (1) contract authority, which is supported by revenues that flow into the Highway Trust Fund and (2) general funds, which Congress has to “appropriate” and assign annually. The current infrastructure law includes another category—advanced appropriations—which makes money available in the future for certain programs without the need for any congressional action in future years, guaranteeing states and communities secure funding over the five-year bill period. This certainty allows for states to plan in advance for future projects and grant opportunities.

Any cut to this pot of money takes a sledgehammer to clean transportation programs that reduce household costs and improve the health of our communities. Particular programs at risk include the National Electric Vehicle Infrastructure program, which helps states build a national network of EV chargers; the Capital Investment Grants, a transit-related discretionary grant program; and the Reconnecting Communities Pilot, which aims to address harms caused by past transportation decisions. Also of concern are the BUILD and MEGA grants, which help support the build-out of transit, bicycle, and pedestrian infrastructure.

Congress is unlikely to agree on a new bill before the current extension comes to an end in December 2026, meaning that there is an opportunity to include advanced appropriations in any future extension of the current infrastructure law and the next transportation bill so that states and local governments can avoid having to grapple with temporary or potentially even permanent funding interruptions. This guaranteed funding is even more important now as transportation decision-makers contend with the Trump administration interfering with implementation and funding guidance. Additional funding clawbacks will only serve to stall progress. The sustained funding will also allow states and local governments to continue to build on the progress that the current infrastructure law made on clean transportation investments.

Even more important, American households are currently struggling to afford their basic daily necessities, and soaring gas prices have left many searching for ways to save on their transportation costs. In 2024, when gas prices averaged below $3.50 a gallon (a far cry from the current average price of $4.35 a gallon), transportation was the second-largest cost for American households, averaging 17 percent of household budgets. Cutting advanced appropriations funding for transit, EV charging, and rail will only serve to penalize the very households struggling the most, at a time when those investments would give Americans access to a wide range of affordable, reliable, and safe transportation options.

By Shruti Vaidyanathan, Director, Transportation Advocacy, Climate & Energy, NRDC

NRDC is the nation's most effective environmental action group, combining the grassroots power of 1.3 million members and online activists with the courtroom clout and expertise of more than 350 lawyers, scientists, and other professionals.

#5749. Ford Settles Transit Trail Class Action Lawsuit By Paying Owners Less Than The Package Cost

The current trend of automakers "off-roadifying" their road cars has created a new breed of hiked up, black plastic-clad soft roaders that buyers can't seem to get enough of. Ford gave its Transit full-size van the treatment back in 2022 with the all-wheel-drive Transit Trail, but recalled all of them in early 2024 and replaced their 30.5-inch all-terrain tires that rubbed against the wheel wells with the same 28.5-inch tires on standard Transits, and owners were not pleased.

They were so displeased, in fact, that they filed a class action lawsuit that we first reported on last year. Ford finally reached a settlement with the plaintiffs last week, offering Transit Trail owners a $6,000 payout for the diminished off-road abilities of their vans. This is a pretty healthy payout from a class action lawsuit settlement, but it's still less than half of the original price of the Trail package that cost $12,500 when new.

According to classaction.org, "Eligible for the settlement are all individuals who, as the original owner or lessee, bought or leased a model year 2023 or 2024 Ford Transit Trail before June 1, 2024 for personal, family or household use and who have not already received any compensation from Ford, including any settlement payment or repurchase, related to a covered vehicle or to any alleged defect of, or economic loss relating to, the vehicle." More fine print about how to prove membership in the settlement class is available on the Class Action site.

The Ford Transit Trail was marketed by Ford as having an "adventure-ready chassis is re-tuned and tested to meet the same BUILT FORD TOUGH durability standards as the rest of the Transit lineup," complete with 6.7-inches of ground clearance, upgraded underbody protection, and the 30.5-inch Goodyear Wrangler Workhorse tires that led to the recall. The 28.5-inch tires that Ford replaced the Wranglers with reduced the ground clearance of Transit Trails down to 5.5-inches, or the same as the standard Transit. Nearly 2,000 vehicles were included in the recall, but it's not immediately clear how many owners are eligible for this class action lawsuit settlement.

#5748. Cool Temps and HF; Death After On-Field Collision; Shave and Shake in Stenting

— Recent developments of interest in cardiovascular medicine

A large nationwide study found an association between cooler ambient temperature and heart failure (HF) hospitalizations. (JAMA Network Open)

Energy drinks and stimulant supplements were frequently involved in cases of cardiac arrest and heart attack in young people, according to an FDA adverse event report analysis highlighted by the American Heart Association.

Abbott announced FDA approval for the Amplatzer Paraclose PVL Occluder, a transcatheter device for paravalvular leakage around a surgical heart valve.

The company also shared that the next-generation Amulet 360 Left Atrial Appendage Occluder had strong 1-year closure results.

A Spanish soccer player died in the hospital after having gone into cardiac arrest following an on-field collision. (AP)

An intervention to strengthen emergency dispatch and activate trained community responders was linked to increased bystander-initiated CPR before the arrival of emergency medical services for out-of-hospital cardiac arrest, a cluster-randomized trial in France showed. (Lancet Public Health)

Heart muscle has the unique ability to burn glucose in the cytosol while simultaneously burning lactate in the mitochondria. (Nature)

In a randomized trial of people with severe pure aortic regurgitation, transfemoral transcatheter aortic valve replacement reduced 1-year clinical events at the cost of more 30-day bleeding and new permanent pacemaker implantation. (Circulation)

The SIRONA trialists compared sirolimus- versus paclitaxel-coated balloons for femoropopliteal artery disease and found no major differences in walking function and quality of life, though clinically driven target lesion revascularization rates did favor the sirolimus group. (JACC: Cardiovascular Interventions)

Ten years into the DECISION-CTO trial, an initial percutaneous coronary intervention strategy for chronic total occlusion was not associated with a survival benefit. (Journal of the American College of Cardiology)

Mount Sinai operators achieved better stent placement among calcified coronary nodules if they employed both rotational atherectomy and lithotripsy to aid stent expansion, a so-called "Shave and Shake" strategy, the NODULE-SHOCK trial showed.

Among stroke patients undergoing mechanical thrombectomy, lower cervical curvature was associated with greater recanalization success, researchers found from a retrospective analysis. (Stroke)

In Dutch patients with angina and nonobstructive coronary arteries, coronary microvascular dysfunction apparently had no effect on clinical outcomes, registry data showed. (JAMA Cardiology)

Preterm infants tended to have worse survival with pharmacological therapy for patent ductus arteriosus, without any reduction in bronchopulmonary dysplasia, according to a meta-analysis. (Lancet Child & Adolescent Health)

#5747. $150 Billion More Paid by Americans for Gas This Year Than Last

Since the beginning of America’s war against Iran, Americans have paid an additional $150+ billion for gas and diesel than they did last year during the same time period, according to a study from Brown University. Global gas prices have risen because supply has been disrupted (Iran having one of the top oil reserves in the world). Americans are paying upwards of $6 a gallon even though American oil companies have not had their own supply chains disrupted the way crude from the Middle East has.

The result? Windfall profits for big oil at the expense of American citizens.

In the leadup to his election in 2024, Donald Trump asked about 20 oil executives at Mar-a-Lago to raise $1 billion for his campaign, promising to reverse dozens of Biden-era regulations if elected. The industry seems not to have raised the full $1 billion, but oil interests still gave tens of millions to Trump-aligned committees. The policies put in place by the Republican party, who has had control of congress and the White House since the last election, have been very pro oil & gas, whether they were anti-EV, anti-solar, anti-energy efficiency, anti-fuel efficiency, anti-wind, or just flat out subsidies to oil and gas.

They ended many of the Biden Administration’s clean energy policies. They ended the Solar For All Program (or tried to). They ended residential and commercial solar tax credits. They signed Executive Order 14315 to terminate wind and solar production and investment tax credits and impose additional restrictions. They made solar development on Federal lands more difficult. On his first day in office, Trump signed a memo withdrawing federal offshore areas from new and renewed wind leasing – but the rule didn’t apply to oil and gas. They ended wind tax credits. They kneecapped the federal standards for vehicle fuel efficiency. They tried to eliminate Energy Star, a program that costs the government $32 million a year and saves Americans $14 billion a year. They weakened Home Energy Rebate programs, SPECIFICALLY stipulating that they could not be used to replace polluting gas furnaces (that increase childhood asthma by more than 15%) with energy efficient electric options. They even tried to make it illegal for future legislators to improve energy efficiency legislation. Then, as if that wasn’t enough, they just gave our tax dollars to the fossil industries, allocating about $190 billion in tax breaks and subsidies over the next decade from the OBBBA.

If this isn’t quid pro quo, I guess I don’t understand what quid pro quo is. But the Iran War took it to a whole new level.

Since it began on February 28, as Americans have spent about $150 billion more on gasoline and diesel, U.S. oil and gas companies earned more than $130 billion in profit from April through September, nearly triple what they earned a year earlier, according to the New York Times.

We can’t say the war was fought for the industry. The president has said his focus is stopping Iran from getting a nuclear weapon. But we can say who is paying at the pump and who is collecting.

Connect the dots however you like, but connect them before you vote. Add it to the roughly $50 billion that US taxpayers have directly spent on the war, and untold more that has percolated through the rest of our economy due to inflationary pressure from rising energy costs, and it’s safe to say it’s well over $200 billion that this war has cost us, alongside the unquantifiable cost in human lives and suffering.

Wars fought for oil are nothing new. But there are things about this conflict that are new.

Oil shocks focus the mind, and this week’s reporting shows how fast the world outside the US is moving to get away from oil dependency. CleanTechnica’s analysis argues that global oil demand is on track to fall by 2–3 million barrels a day this year, the first drop not caused by a recession or pandemic, as high prices push consumers away from fuel. You can see it in China, where gas-car sales fell 49% in September and EVs hit a record 67% share. Europe is also answering, with affordable small EVs like the Smart #2 and an electric Citroën 2 CV at about €15,000. In Australia, a Chinese-built electric semi is already hauling cement for about half the price of its rivals, and Africa’s e-mobility leaders are talking about building vehicles rather than just importing them. Drivers who’ve switched are happy too: 82% of XPeng owners in a 4,000-person European survey said they’d recommend the brand, versus a 53% average.

Every driver, trucker, and country that electrifies becomes less exposed to the next oil shock. The question for the U.S. is how fast it joins in. And this election will go a long way toward answering that question.

Scott Cooney (LinkedIn) is a serial eco-entrepreneur focused on making the world a better place for all its residents. Scott is the founder of CleanTechnica and was just smart enough to hire someone smarter than him to run it. He then started Pono Home, a service that greens homes, which has performed efficiency retrofits on more than 20,000 homes and small businesses, reducing carbon pollution by more than 27 million pounds a year and saving customers more than $6.3 million a year on their utilities. Scott wanted to contribute to native ownership of the clean energy revolution, so he gifted Pono Home to a long tenured employee with native Hawaiian roots for just the liquidation value, turning down a mainland company interested in purchasing the company. In a previous life, Scott was an adjunct professor of Sustainability in the MBA program at the University of Hawai'i, a consultant at Saatchi & Saatchi S, where he worked with a team to educate and empower millions of employees to live healthier and more sustainably. He is the author of Build a Green Small Business: Profitable Ways to Become an Ecopreneur (McGraw-Hill) , and Green Living Ideas. Scott is an occasional investor, currently he has investments in Rivian (RIVN).

#5746. ARRAY Technologies and Shoals plan to merge companies

Solar tracker and racking manufacturer ARRAY Technologies and electrical balance of systems (eBOS) provider Shoals Technologies have initiated a stock-for-stock merger to combine both companies.

“This transaction represents a compelling opportunity to create meaningful value for customers of both ARRAY and Shoals by bringing together our complementary strengths. Although structural and electrical systems must operate seamlessly together in the field, they have historically been designed and sourced through separate channels. This combination changes that. Together, we expect to deliver a differentiated, technically integrated and interoperable platform that simplifies project execution, enhances project economics and accelerates innovation for customers,” said Kevin Hostetler, CEO of ARRAY.

According to the press announcement, ARRAY will be Shoals’ parent company after the transaction, with Shoal’s CEO Brandon Moss assuming the role as CEO for the overall company. ARRAY’s CEO Kevin Hostetler will transition to a special advisor role for the company.

“Today marks an important milestone for Shoals, ARRAY and the customers we serve. By bringing together two highly complementary businesses with experienced teams, we are creating an integrated platform with broader capabilities, deeper customer relationships and a stronger foundation for long-term growth. We believe the combined company will be positioned to better service customers, deliver more innovative and integrated solutions, realize meaningful synergies and pursue expansion opportunities across the solar value chain, data center markets and beyond,” said Brandon Moss, CEO of Shoals.

The companies plan to create packaged warranties across their structural and eBOS products. As manufacturers, both ARRAY and Shoals have supplied their products to solar and energy storage projects globally.

ARRAY is known for producing single-axis solar trackers that use an inter-row rotating mechanism. Over the years, the company has iterated on its trackers to use multi- and dual-row architectures, and to be deployed on undulating topographies. These rotating solar structures are supported with proprietary tracker operations software with considerations such as shading and hail and wind stowing.

Shoals manufactures combiner boxes and cable management solutions for multi-market scale solar and energy storage projects, as well as data centers.

“Having had the privilege of serving as chairman of both ARRAY and Shoals, I have seen firsthand the complementary products, talented teams and shared commitment to customers offered by both companies,” said Brad Forth, chairman of the board of directors of ARRAY and Shoals. “Together, ARRAY and Shoals are well positioned to deliver enhanced value to customers, shareholders and employees. I also want to thank Kevin for his leadership and important contributions to ARRAY, as well as his valuable insights as he transitions to a Special Advisor role. I look forward to continuing to work with Brandon, who brings proven leadership, deep industry expertise and strategic vision to lead the combined company in this next phase of growth.”

Both ARRAY’s and Shoals’ boards have reportedly unanimously approved the merger, with Forth abstaining from the vote. The merger is expected to complete in Q1 2027.

While not mergers, ARRAY Technologies has been acquiring companies producing technologies complementary to solar trackers. Last year, ARRAY acquired fixed-tilt racking and foundations manufacturer APA Solar Racking, and just acquired Affordable Wire Management, a cable management solutions provider, this past September.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5745. Trump Wants More Attention For His New Algae Biofuel Showcase

US President Donald Trump has famously transformed the magnificent Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool into a high profile showcase for the algae biofuel industry, and he stands firm in his faith that the tiny green microalgae can produce renewable fuels at scale. In fact, on Monday, November 2, the President held his all-important closing message for the 2026 midterm election cycle right there at the Reflecting Pool. Hey, maybe Trump knows something about algae biofuel…

The Reflecting Pool is a massive body of shallow water that extends from the foot of the Lincoln Memorial in Washington, DC. Situated on filled ground in former marshlands, the Reflecting Pool provides a golden opportunity to showcase the ability of the domestic biofuel industry to take advantage of the built infrastructure, rather than taking up valuable farmland with conventional energy crops like corn or soybeans.

Although algae cultivation was probably not the original intention of the Reflecting Pool, it has been a perennial host to algae ever since its construction in the 1920’s, with the humid environment and temperate climate of DC lending a hand.

In earlier years, the Pool was drained and refilled twice a year to control the growth. By the 1990’s, the structure was clearly sinking into the marshes, sparking calls for a major structural overhaul and the addition of a water circulating system.

The soup-to-nuts rehab was completed in 2012, and yet the algae persisted. Finally, when Trump took office for the second time in January of 2025, he lit upon a brilliant idea of turning lemons into lemonade. The Reflecting Pool would be re-made as a monument to the domestic algae biofuel industry.

By the summer of 2026, the transformation was complete and the algae were thriving, as confirmed by satellite imagery. Others mocked, but the President stood by the plan. After all, biomass is a key element in his “American Energy Dominance” policy, and where better to showcase biofuel than in the nation’s capitol, to be seen and admired by millions of tourists.

And so, that explains why Trump chose the Reflecting Pool as the seemingly incongruous scene of his Election Day eve closing message, as reported somewhat incredulously by The Guardian among many others.

“On the eve of a consequential election day, with polls indicating that most voters feel the US president has grown too focused on building projects in the nation’s capital, he stood in front of the newly opened reflecting pool to discuss – in detail – changes to the pool’s pipe, the grass surrounding the area and granite,” observed The Guardian.

Algae biofuel surfaced regularly on the pages of CleanTechnica in the early 2000’s, with the high oil content and rapid growth cycle of algae being the attractions along with the potential for developing next-generation farming and extraction systems leading to a competitive renewable energy resource (see more algae background here).

Not much progress has been made since then, and stakeholders in the field have weathered much skepticism if not downright mockery. A seal of approval from the President of the United States of America is just what the industry needs to get to the next level.

Indeed, a fresh round of activity has surfaced in recent years, with the UK firm HutanBio being one example. The startup aims to generate profitability by leveraging the carbon-capturing angle of algae farming.

Earlier this year Hutan signed an MOU (Memorandum of Understanding) with the Irish firm Utopia World Investment and the biodiesel refiner Wakud International to scale up its algae farming system, with Oman being the location. “Our unique microalgae strain, Sphaerica, is grown in seawater, on non-arable coastal land, using CO₂ and sunlight creating a genuinely low-carbon biocrude stream that the industry currently lacks and urgently needs,” HutanBio says of itself.

As described by HutanBio, Sphaerica yields up to ten times more oil than typical algae biofuel strains. Hutan expects the high level of productivity to dovetail with Oman’s high level of solar radiation, enabling it to leapfrog over its competitors.

The Oman operation is set for operation at a demonstration scale before the end of 2027, which is just around the corner. Meanwhile, Japanese automaker Mazda is also beginning to crack the algae code.

On October 7, Mazda announced that its microalgae biofuel research program has nailed down support from NEDO, the New Energy and Industrial Technology Development Organization of Japan. The Mazda program is in early stages, with scale-up expected by the 2030’s. If all goes according to plan, Mazda will use its homemade algae biofuel to factory-fill the tanks of every diesel vehicle that rolls off its assembly lines.

Evidently Mazda does not anticipate that drivers can pump out algae biofuel at any corner gas station for their next fill-up. Still, it’s another sign that that years-long research programs are finally leading to commercial application.

For a glimpse into the future, check out newly published research from the Saitama University in Japan. Where conventional systems involve cultivating algae, drying it, and processing it to remove oils, the Saitama team has developed a strain of cyanobacteria (commonly known as blue-green algae) that can express free fatty acids outside of the cell and deposit them directly into a culture medium. Among other benefits, the Saitama system does not require destroying the algae with each harvesting cycle.

As for the US, ExxonMobil was the standard-bearer for early stage algae biofuel research throughout the early 2000’s. By 2017, the company was confident enough to put up millions for an elaborate advertising blitz that showcased its efforts to the world.

Or not, as the case may be. In 2019, Massachusetts State Attorney General Maura Healey filed suit against Exxon, alleging that the biofuel ads were deceptive. “Specifically, the complaint alleges that Exxon systematically and intentionally has misled Massachusetts investors about material climate-driven risks to its business and has deceived consumers about the central role its fossil fuel products play in causing climate change,” AG Healy’s office explained.

Exxon subsequently dropped the program altogether, but the lawsuit persists. Among the details coming to light is a deposition by an Exxon scientist who worked on the algae program. Under questioning by attorneys with the AG’s office, the scientist asserted that the claims in Exxon’s ads were “patently false,” and that he said as much to the company.

It’s too bad Exxon dropped the ball before Trump had a chance to pick up the algae biofuel torch. Oh, well. We’ll always have the Reflecting Pool…

Photo: The Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool has been host to thriving populations of algae ever since its construction in the 1920’s (cropped, courtesy of National Parks Service).

#5744. Mid-Air Helicopter Collision Near Everest Base Camp Kills Tourist, Injures 2 Others

Although an entire tourism industry has grown around climbing Mount Everest, summiting the world's tallest mountain remains a tremendously dangerous endeavor. Two helicopters collided in mid-air while attempting to land at the same spot below the Everest base camp. Helicopter company Fishtail Air stated that the crash killed an Indian tourist, and the chopper's captain and another passenger were injured. The other helicopter's pilot somehow escaped without injury. The second chopper was only carrying cargo.

The footage is harrowing, to say the least. A red helicopter is hovering, gradually descending and rotating to a landing spot at Pheriche. The Nepali village sits 13,780 feet above sea level. Then, a second helicopter comes into frame, seemingly appearing out of nowhere. Both pilots take evasive action far too late. The second chopper's main blades smash into the other rotorcraft, slicing off the tail boom. Both helicopters immediately plummet right into the snowy terrain as people waiting on the ground scramble away. Miraculously, none of the bystanders were struck by flying debris or injured. There's never a good outcome when two helicopters hit each other.

It's pretty baffling how two helicopters could collide in completely clear conditions. Maybe the pilots didn't realize they were trying to land in the same spot. Helicopter flights aren't rare in this part of the world, either. According to the BBC, helicopters are the only way to get trekkers and guides down from Everest when they are sick or exhausted in inhospitable conditions. It's also the tail end of the mountain's autumn climbing season.

The tourists who spend thousands of dollars to climb Everest do so with the understanding that it's inherently dangerous. Those dangers are typically low oxygen levels and freezing temperatures at absurdly high altitudes. Just getting to the mountain can be just as dangerous. Most journeys to Everest base camp begin at Tenzing-Hillary Airport, named after the first mountaineers to climb the mountain. The facility is often called the world's most dangerous airport because of its short 1,729-foot runway with a 12% gradient and the mountain wall at one end. Not to mention, the terrain doesn't allow pilots to go around despite the rapidly shifting weather conditions. Just flying in would be enough of an Everest adventure for me.

#5742. I Checked Out Tesla’s New Model YL, Standard Y, Model 3 In Person — Here’s What Surprised Me

I finally got to see the new Tesla Model YL, Model Y Standard, and Model 3 Standard in person a few weeks ago and wanted to give my thoughts on the three models. I drove the two Model Ys and just looked at the Model 3 Standard.  I was visiting relatives in Colorado and visited the Tesla dealer in Superior, Colorado (a very progressive part of Colorado). The dealer was very busy, but not quite as busy as I saw when the tax credit was expiring last year.

As I mentioned in this article 4 months ago, I’ve been a fan of the Model YL since it was announced in China, and finally in the US recently. As a parent of 3 kids and an owner of a series of 3 minivans over 30 years, I’m well aware of the criticism that Tesla doesn’t make a car suitable for large families. The Model X was a partial solution to this problem, but had two large flaws in my opinion.

So, I wanted to see if the Model YL solved these two issues with the Model X. The short is answer is that it solved the price issue but not the space issue. The premium launch edition at $61,990, with about $11,000 in free options, is plenty affordable — although, still a bit more than a minivan or a Grand Highlander. In fact, I optioned up a Model Y Premium with similar features (including the 3rd row) and it came out to about $60,000! So, this suggests the Model YL will be available for about $53,000 or approximately a $3,000 upcharge once they stop selling the Launch Edition (similar to the price difference in other global markets). On space, the Model YL is pretty similar to the Model X. Good enough for many families, but not as spacious as minivans, the Grand Highlander, or the Chevy Tahoe. You can see from the screenshot below that the Model YL is sold out for 2026, and the dealer was telling people to not expect them until March 2027.

I thought it would look more different than the regular Y. I found it difficult to tell the difference at a glance.

The second row was very comfortable, with heated and cooled seat and plenty of room and adjustable headrests. A significant upgrade to the second row of the regular Y!

The third row was a mixed bag. The seats are heated, but not cooled. It does have vents for both air conditioning and heat. It’s not as spacious as the first and second rows, but at 6′ 2″, I was comfortable and would be fine on a long drive. I did find it challenging to get to the 3rd row, since the second row didn’t slide forward like it does in many vehicles (including the regular Model Y). You are expected to sneak between the two seats in the second row, which works for most people, but older folks like me don’t like it.

Leg room was adequate (not not generous), but I was more comfortable putting one leg in the middle as shown. Had I moved up the second row, I would have had plenty of room, but then it would have been crowded. I was setting all three rows as best I could for 6-feet-tall people in all 3 rows. This is MUCH better than the 3rd row in the regular Model Y, which is only appropriate for children and small adults.

I was amazed I had an inch or two of space above my head in the 3rd row. I forgot to take any pictures of the first row. It does have an extra thigh support adjustment that is nice, but is mostly just like all of the other Model Y (great, but nothing new). Handling and accelerations were about the same as a Premium Model Y dual motor. The ride was a little softer (the YL has electronically controlled dampers, tuned for comfort) than the 2026 Model Y Premium (frequency dependent passive controlled dampers, tuned for a balance of comfort and handling) and much improved over my 2021 Model Y (conventional passive shock absorbers, great for handling but gives a harsh ride) and the 2026 Model Y Standard (retuned conventional passive shock absorbers, a balance between handling and ride). Full Self Driving (Supervised) was promoted by the sales advisor and was perfect on the short test drive.

The Model Y Standard reminded me most of my daughter’s 2024 Model Y Rear Wheel Drive. It is a little noisier and doesn’t ride as well as the premium model. It doesn’t have the ventilated seats and the premium audio, but for many, the $6,000 price difference makes up for that. For others, the Premium is well worth that cost. I did like the 18″ wheels that improve range and offer more sidewall for surviving large potholes. The Model Y Premium only offers 19″ and 20″ wheels. Full Self Driving (Supervised) worked perfectly on the test drive.

To adjust the seats, you use the screen, since there are not controls on the seats.

I didn’t drive the Model 3 Standard, but I want to make two points about it.

After seeing the Model YL, Model Y Standard, and the Model 3 Standard in person, the takeaway is clear: Tesla has made its most practical vehicles significantly more accessible without abandoning the core strengths that made the brand popular.

The Model YL addresses the long-standing complaint that Tesla lacked a true family hauler. While it still doesn’t match the passenger or cargo space of a minivan or large three-row SUV like the Grand Highlander or Tahoe, it is a meaningful improvement over the regular Model Y’s third row and far more usable for adults or even teenagers. At roughly $53,000 once the Launch Edition incentives end, it finally offers a realistic alternative to the much more expensive Model X or Rivian R1S (~$90,000 for comparable 6 seat layout) for families who want three rows without the premium price.

The Model Y Standard reinforces that Tesla can still deliver strong value at a lower price. It gives up ventilated seats, premium audio, and some refinement, but the $6,000 savings and more practical 18-inch wheels make it a sensible choice for some buyers. Meanwhile, the Model 3 Standard stands out as an even better relative deal than the original 2016 Model 3 that created such intense demand — more range, quicker acceleration, and Full Self-Driving capability at a price that remains extremely competitive in today’s market. I was disappointed when these models were announced a year ago, as I wrote about here, mainly because I felt Tesla promised a smaller car for under $30,000. However, all three of these vehicles have found their market in the US, as evidenced by the long waitlist to get all three and the lack of discounts and aggressive financing deals available.

Taken together, these three models show Tesla broadening its appeal. The company is no longer only competing at the premium end; it is also offering capable, reasonably priced options for both families and efficiency-focused buyers. For those who have been waiting for Tesla to better serve larger households or entry-level shoppers, the current lineup finally delivers more realistic choices.

#5741. Oil Companies Will Love That The 2027 Chevrolet Silverado's New V8s Get Worse Gas Mileage Than The Old Ones

Due to a litany of misguided decisions on behalf of the Trump administration, gas is ridiculously expensive right now, corporate fuel economy requirements are worryingly low, and we're in an affordability crisis. In totally unrelated news, the oil and gas companies that spent millions to elect Trump are raking in record profits that are more than double what they were at this time last year. Those oil companies are doing fine, but they will still likely be stoked to learn that GM announced the fuel economy numbers for V8-powered examples of the freshly redesigned 2027 Chevrolet Silverado today, and they all get worse gas mileage than the outgoing Silverado's V8s. These numbers should all also apply to the 2027 GMC Sierra, too.

According to documents posted by GM Authority, the fuel economy of all Silverados equipped with either the new 5.7-liter V8 or the new 6.6-liter V8 has dropped between 1 and 2 mpg across city ratings, highway ratings, and combined ratings. While these drops aren't especially significant, it's rare to see redesigned versions of cars debut with worse efficiency than their predecessors, even when we're talking about big trucks.

2027 Silverados fitted with the 5.7-liter V8 are rated at 16 mpg city, 19 mpg highway, and 17 combined in two-wheel-drive form; opting for four-wheel drive drops both the highway and combined ratings by an additional mpg. Those city and combined figures are each down one mpg, and the highway rating is down two mpg compared to the 2026 Silverado.

Trucks with the 6.6-liter V8 are only shown in four-wheel-drive, and are rated at 14 mpg in the city, 18 mpg on the highway, and 15 mpg combined. If you go for the off-road-oriented Silverado ZR2, it drops the city and combined figures by 1 mpg and the highway figure by 3 mpg. (The same should go for the Sierra's AT4X equivalent.) All of those numbers are down by either 1 or 2 mpg when compared to their fuel economy ratings in 2026.

Even opting for the 3.0-liter inline-six turbodiesel powertrain option won't save as much fuel as it did last year. Most diesel-powered 2027 Silverados return between 1 and 2 mpg worse than last year, except for city mileage in some four-wheel-drive trims. City and highway mileage for the diesel ZR2 remains the same. Mileage ratings for the turbocharged 2.7-liter four-cylinder powertrain have not been published yet.

Considering that the Chevrolet Silverado almost always lands in the second-place spot on the list of best-selling vehicles in the U.S. behind the Ford F-Series, and the GMC Sierra isn't too far behind, the impact of small changes in efficiency get magnified. So far through the third quarter of 2026, GMC sold 252,639 Sierras and Chevrolet sold 413,383 Silverados, and production of the new-gen trucks only just started in October. Though not all of these trucks will be fitted with one of the new V8s, certainly a ton of them will, and decreasing even just V8 gas mileage by a single mpg will still result in a massive increase in collective fuel usage.
